// enact the browser preference if "automatic",
// without expecting DOM load to have completed.
//
// Also restore any saved readingmode and ToC display preferences.
function initializeReadingPreferences() {
let saved_theme = localStorage.getItem("ar5iv_theme") || "automatic";
if (saved_theme === "automatic") {
if (window.matchMedia("(prefers-color-scheme: dark)").matches) {
saved_theme = "dark";
}
}
if (saved_theme == "dark") {
document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "dark");
} else {
document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-theme", "light");
}
const tocDisplay = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_toc_display');
if (tocDisplay) {
document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-toc-display", tocDisplay);
}
const readingMode = localStorage.getItem('arxiv_html_paper_reading_mode');
if (readingMode) {
document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-reading-mode", readingMode);
}
// Pre-apply spinout-banner dismissal here, before the banner paints, so it
// never flashes in only to be hidden later by the deferred arxiv-header.js.
// Key matches arxiv-header.js: "arxiv-banner-dismissed:" + data-banner-name.
if (localStorage.getItem('arxiv-banner-dismissed:spinout-nonprofit')) {
document.documentElement.setAttribute("data-banner-dismissed", "");
}
}
// Run as soon as JS starts, to minimize repainting
initializeReadingPreferences();

reading-mode chrome hiding. */

(otherwise it inherits the paper's warm-wash text in dark mode and washes out). */
.ds-announcement-text { color: var(--arxiv-ink, #1c1a17); }

arXiv:2606.15569v1 [cs.LG] 14 Jun 2026
A Decision-Theoretic View of Test-Time Training:
When, How Far, and Which Directions to Adapt
Tomoya Wakayama
Affiliation: RIKEN Center for Advanced Intelligence Project (AIP), Tokyo, Japan
Correspondence to: tomoya.wakayama@riken.jp
Abstract
Test-time training (TTT) adapts a pretrained model to each prompt via parameter updates, improving accuracy under pretraining-to-test distribution shifts. Yet, its performance often suffers from instability and sensitivity to hyperparameters such as update steps and subspace. We explain this behavior through a decision-theoretic lens, treating TTT as implicit Bayesian inference in the kernel regime. Under a Gaussian process benchmark, we show that TTT reduces prediction error when updates are spectrally matched to the prompt’s signal-to-noise ratio and aligned with query-relevant eigen-directions. This perspective underpins the following results: (1) we show when fixed update steps and subspaces fail under distribution shifts, motivating adaptive strategies; (2) we prove that selecting update steps via prompt evidence admits a PAC-Bayes guarantee against overfitting; and (3) we characterize the Bayes-optimal update subspace under a linear-Gaussian correction model, yielding a scoring rule for selecting Transformer blocks and heads. Our theory helps explain the empirical instability of TTT, taking a step toward principled guidance for when, how far, and which directions to adapt.
Keywords: Test-Time Training, Bayesian Inference, Kernel Methods, Transformers,Pac-Bayes
1 Introduction
Modern foundation models increasingly serve as general-purpose predictors, answering diverse user queries. Yet real deployments frequently deviate from pretraining conditions, in which a fixed (no-update) inference procedure can become brittle. This has kindled interest in test-time training (TTT), which updates model parameters using only the prompt to adapt to a specific test task. Following the seminal work by Sun et al. [44], a growing body of work has shown that TTT improves robustness and domain generalization in vision [51, 52, 55], extends to high-stakes settings such as medical image segmentation [46], and adapts end-to-end automatic speech recognition models to non-stationary acoustic conditions [23, 27]. More recently, analogous ideas have also been explored for large language models [18, 19].
While these empirical improvements are noteworthy, TTT often exhibits instability in practice. TTT’s performance is sensitive to optimization hyperparameters (e.g., update spaces and step count), and more steps can negatively impact performance [57]. This sensitivity is exacerbated by non-stationary shifts and small-batch streaming [9, 35], and analogous issues have been reported for language models [1]. While recent works seek more stable updates and safer parameter choices [2, 40], we still lack principled guidance for TTT.
Prior theories [17, 24] have started to characterize when TTT can improve in-context learning (ICL, i.e., no parameter updates) in specific regimes (e.g., linearized transformers or structured task models). Complementary to the ICL-centered analyses, Hübotter et al. [20] propose a mechanistic explanation for why TTT can help at scale even on nominally in-distribution data. Still, general principles guiding the choice of optimization hyperparameters remain elusive. Our work focuses on the following questions: (i) when TTT reduces the predictive risk relative to ICL, (ii) how the number of test-time update steps affects performance, and (iii) which parameter subspace is effective for updating.
In a kernel (linearized-quadratic) regime, where TTT typically operates, we present a Bayes-risk perspective by interpreting TTT as implicit Bayesian inference. Here, the update steps and the update subspace act as hyperparameters of a prompt-induced prior. Our claims are as follows.
(C1)
Under a Gaussian process benchmark that couples prompt and query, we derive a conditional risk identity showing that query predictions benefit from both filter match (in eigenvalue space) and eigen-alignment with the prompt-query coupling vector (Thm. 5.2).
We further show that fixed steps and fixed update subspaces can fail (Propositions 6.1–6.3).
(C2)
We show that selecting the number of updates by prompt evidence provides a PAC-Bayes guarantee and mitigates overfitting to the prompt (Thm. 7.2).
(C3)
Under a linear-Gaussian correction prior, we characterize the Bayes-optimal update subspace via an information-capture matrix, yielding an attention block/head scoring rule for prediction (Thm. 8.2); see App. E.6 for a regret bound.
Together, these results give a principled starting point for analyzing few-step, restricted-update TTT. We do not aim to give a complete theory of fully nonlinear TTT. Rather, we use a local Bayesian lens to identify design principles for TTT: how far to update, and which directions to update. The Transformer specializations in Secs. 3.3 and 8.2 make this link to block/head-level TTT explicit.
1.1 Related Work
Test-time training.
TTT is widely used for domain generalization and test-time adaptation [44, 35]. Recent work studies parameter-efficient or specialization-oriented updates for foundation models [20]. Theoretically, Gozeten et al. [17] relate TTT to ICL in linearized transformer settings, and Kuwataka and Suzuki [24] show multi-step TTT can enhance ICL on single-index tasks. We cast TTT as Bayesian inference in a kernel regime, analyzing a risk identity and PAC-Bayes theory for steps and optimal update subspaces.
Bayesian view of test-time behavior.
ICL is often viewed as amortized Bayesian inference learned through pretraining [38, 32]. Because its inference mapping is fixed, correcting for prior mismatch [50] or the amortization gap [30] at test time remains challenging. Unlike fixed in-context inference, we view TTT as prompt-induced Bayesian inference, revealing how evidence-tuned steps and subspaces correct prior mismatch.
2 Setup: tasks, prompts, and risk
In TTT and ICL, we consider a distribution over tasks: at test time, a latent task is sampled, and then the prompt and query are generated from the corresponding task-specific data distribution. This section introduces a mixture (hierarchical) regression setting as in Wakayama and Suzuki [50] and formalizes an analytical framework.
Mixture task model and test-time information
Let α\alpha denote a latent task variable drawn from a meta-distribution π\pi over tasks. Conditioned on α\alpha, data pairs (X,Y)(X,Y) follow a task-specific distribution 𝒫α\mathcal{P}_{\alpha} over 𝒳×𝒴⊂ℝdx×ℝ\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{x}}\times\mathbb{R}. At test time, we observe an i.i.d. labeled prompt Pn:={(Xi,Yi)}i=1nP_{n}:=\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}, followed by a query input XX and target YY, all generated from the same task
α∼π,(Xi,Yi)∣α∼i.i.d.𝒫α,(X,Y)∣α∼𝒫α.\alpha\sim\pi,\quad(X_{i},Y_{i})\mid\alpha\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}\ ,\quad(X,Y)\mid\alpha\sim\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}.
(1)
At test time, only (Pn,X)(P_{n},X) is observed, while α\alpha is not. Throughout, we assume each labeled example corresponds to a single token position.
Bayes gap.
We use the squared loss ℓ⁡(u,v)=(u−v)2\ell(u,v)=(u-v)^{2}, following Gozeten et al. [17], Kuwataka and Suzuki [24]. To evaluate a measurable map M:(Pn,X)↦Y^∈ℝM:(P_{n},X)\mapsto\widehat{Y}\in\mathbb{R}, we adopt the Bayes risk [see 33, §5.3.1.2], i.e., the expected squared prediction error ℛ⁡(M):=𝔼⁡[{M⁡(Pn,X)−Y}2]\mathcal{R}(M):=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl\{M(P_{n},X)-Y\bigr\}^{2}\bigr], where the expectation is over the joint distribution in (1).
In this setting, the minimizer of the Bayes risk among all square-integrable measurable maps of (Pn,X)(P_{n},X) is the conditional mean MBayes(Pn,X):=𝔼[Y∣Pn,X]M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(P_{n},X):=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid P_{n},X]. See Appendix C for a review of Bayes risk and the Bayes predictor.
Proposition 2.1 (Orthogonal Bayes-risk decomposition).
Assume 𝔼⁡[M​(Pn,X)2]<∞\mathbb{E}[M(P_{n},X)^{2}]<\infty and 𝔼⁡[Y2]<∞\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}]<\infty. Then
ℛ⁡(M)\displaystyle\vskip-5.69054pt\mathcal{R}(M)
=𝔼⁡[(M⁡(Pn,X)−MBayes​(Pn,X))2]⏟=:𝒢⁡(M)​(Bayes gap)\displaystyle=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(M(P_{n},X)-M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(P_{n},X)\bigr)^{2}\Bigr]}_{=:\ \mathcal{G}(M)\ \textnormal{(Bayes gap)}}
+𝔼⁡[(MBayes​(Pn,X)−Y)2]⏟=:𝒱=𝔼⁡[Var⁡(Y∣Pn,X)]​(posterior variance).\displaystyle\quad+\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(P_{n},X)-Y\bigr)^{2}\Bigr]}_{=:\ \mathcal{V}\ =\ \mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(Y\mid P_{n},X)]\ \textnormal{(posterior variance)}}.
(2)
Here, 𝒱\mathcal{V} is the irreducible uncertainty about YY after observing (Pn,X)(P_{n},X) and is independent of predictor MM. In contrast, the Bayes gap 𝒢⁡(M)\mathcal{G}(M) is the excess risk relative to MBayesM_{\mathrm{Bayes}}, measuring how accurately MM approximates MBayesM_{\mathrm{Bayes}} as a map of (Pn,X)(P_{n},X). Thus, any improvement of TTT must come from reducing 𝒢\mathcal{G}.
Correction view.
To analyze the adaptation process, consider the setting where we correct a base predictor BB (e.g., ICL). We write any adapted rule as M=B+CM=B+C with correction CC. Let ΔB(Pn,X):=𝔼[Y∣Pn,X]−B(Pn,X)\Delta_{B}(P_{n},X):=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid P_{n},X]-B(P_{n},X) be the Bayes-optimal correction. Under squared loss, the Bayes gap of MM simplifies to correction gap 𝒢⁡(M)=𝔼⁡[{C⁡(Pn,X)−ΔB​(Pn,X)}2]\mathcal{G}(M)=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\{C(P_{n},X)-\Delta_{B}(P_{n},X)\}^{2}\right]. Thus, improving over BB amounts to learning a prompt-dependent correction.
As the Bayes correction under the general model is typically intractable, in Secs. 5–8, we analyze the Bayes gap relative to a tractable benchmark Bayes correction.
3 A Local Linearized Model for TTT
This section builds a tractable local model for few-step TTT. We do not assume that practical TTT is globally linear. Instead, we use the first-order model as a controlled approximation to the nonlinear update path. This approximation is appropriate when the update subspace is restricted and the number of test-time steps is modest; Appendix F.1 gives a finite-step deviation bound between the nonlinear and linearized prompt corrections. Within this model, TTT becomes a linear inverse problem in the update parameters. Since test-time supervision is available only through the labeled prompt, we use leave-one-out residuals as noisy observations of the desired correction at prompt points.
Residual correction induced by a base predictor.
Let TTT output MTTT​(Pn,x)=B⁡(Pn,x)+C⁡(Pn,x)M_{\mathrm{TTT}}(P_{n},x)\!=\!B(P_{n},x)+C(P_{n},x). We form leave-one-out residuals r∈ℝnr\in\mathbb{R}^{n} with ri:=yi−B⁡(Pn(−i),xi)r_{i}:=y_{i}-B(P_{n}^{(-i)},x_{i}), where Pn(−i):=Pn∖{(xi,yi)}P_{n}^{(-i)}:=P_{n}\setminus\{(x_{i},y_{i})\}. Define the (prompt-point) Bayes-optimal correction by f⋆,i:=𝔼[Yi∣Pn(−i),xi]−B(Pn(−i),xi)f_{\star,i}:=\mathbb{E}[Y_{i}\mid P_{n}^{(-i)},x_{i}]-B(P_{n}^{(-i)},x_{i}). Then, 𝔼[ri−f⋆,i∣Pn(−i),xi]=0\mathbb{E}[r_{i}-f_{\star,i}\mid P_{n}^{(-i)},x_{i}]=0, so rr is an unbiased noisy observation of f⋆f_{\star}. We use rr to avoid label leakage: the target yiy_{i} is not used in computing either B⁡(Pn(−i),xi)B(P_{n}^{(-i)},x_{i}) or the Jacobian feature, defined later.
3.1 Restricted update subspace and Jacobian features
Let w∈ℝpw\in\mathbb{R}^{p} denote the full parameter vector of a model and w0w_{0} its pretrained value. Let fw​(x,Pn)∈ℝf_{w}(x;P_{n})\in\mathbb{R} denote the model prediction at query input xx given prompt PnP_{n} under parameters ww. Note that B⁡(Pn,x)=fw0​(x,Pn)B(P_{n},x)=f_{w_{0}}(x;P_{n}). Since practical TTT typically updates only a small subset of parameters or a low-rank reparameterization [51, 35, 19, 20], we consider the following setting.
Assumption 3.1 (Restricted update subspace).
There exists a d(<p)d(<p)-dimensional linear subspace 𝒰⊂ℝp\mathcal{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p} such that test-time updates satisfy w∈w0+𝒰w\in w_{0}+\mathcal{U}.
Only the subspace 𝒰\mathcal{U} matters, not a particular basis, that is, any full-column-rank A~\widetilde{A} with range⁡(A~)=𝒰\mathrm{range}(\widetilde{A})=\mathcal{U} can be replaced, without loss of generality, by an orthonormal basis. Let A∈ℝp×dA\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times d} be such a basis (A⊤​A=IdA^{\top}A=I_{d}), so that any update can be written as w⁡(θ)=w0+A​θw(\theta)=w_{0}+A\theta. We denote the orthogonal projector by Π𝒰:=A​A⊤\Pi_{\mathcal{U}}:=AA^{\top}.
Since TTT uses few gradient steps within a low-dimensional update subspace, a first-order expansion in θ\theta is a natural local surrogate [21, 25, 11]. We refer to this few-step approximation as a local linear (kernel/NTK) regime.
Assumption 3.2 (Linearized surrogate model).
We analyze TTT through the linearized surrogate fθ​(x,Pn):=fw0​(x,Pn)+ϕ​(x,Pn)⊤​θf_{\theta}(x;P_{n})\ :=\ f_{w_{0}}(x;P_{n})+\phi(x;P_{n})^{\top}\theta, with Jacobian features ϕ⁡(x,Pn):=∇θfw⁡(θ)​(x,Pn)|θ=0\phi(x;P_{n}):=\nabla_{\theta}f_{w(\theta)}(x;P_{n})\big|_{\theta=0}.
Note that the approximation fθ​(x,Pn)≈fw0​(x,Pn)+ϕ​(x,Pn)⊤​θf_{\theta}(x;P_{n})\approx f_{w_{0}}(x;P_{n})+\phi(x;P_{n})^{\top}\theta is valid in the few-step regime, as in common practice [1, 44]. See Appendix F.1 for validation of the assumption.
Stack the Jacobian features into the matrix Φ:=(ϕ⁡(x1,Pn(−1)),…,ϕ⁡(xn,Pn(−n)))⊤∈ℝn×d\Phi:=(\phi(x_{1};P_{n}^{(-1)}),\ldots,\phi(x_{n};P_{n}^{(-n)}))^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}. Under Assumption 3.2, the vector of prediction shifts on the prompt is approximately Φ​θ\Phi\theta. Hence, fitting the residuals rr reduces to a linear inverse problem in θ\theta.
3.2 Quadratic prompt loss and the prompt-induced geometry K⁡(Pn)K(P_{n})
Based on the linearization, we use the quadratic prompt objective L⁡(θ):=12​σ2​‖r−Φ​θ‖22L(\theta):=\tfrac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\|r-\Phi\theta\|_{2}^{2}. For fixed σ2\sigma^{2}, minimizing LL is maximum likelihood estimation in the Gaussian regression model r=Φ​θ⋆+εr=\Phi\theta_{\star}+\varepsilon with ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}), where θ⋆\theta_{\star} is the (unknown) optimal update parameter that best explains the residuals under the linearization and σ2\sigma^{2} is an effective noise level that accounts for label noise and linearization errors. In Sec. 4, we study the few-step optimization dynamics of LL.
In this kernel regime, the optimization dynamics on this objective are governed by the prompt-space Gram structure of the features, captured by K⁡(Pn):=Φ​Φ⊤∈ℝn×nK(P_{n}):=\Phi\Phi^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}. We refer to KK as the prompt-induced geometry, as it determines how updates couple the nn prompt residuals during optimization. Crucially, this geometry depends on both the prompt instance PnP_{n} and the update mechanism AA (e.g., which attention heads are adaptable at test time). This dependence distinguishes TTT from classical early stopping with a fixed geometry, where the Gram structure is predetermined.
To relate the prompt geometry K=Φ​Φ⊤K=\Phi\Phi^{\top} to the full parameter space and the chosen update subspace, collect the nn supervised prompt outputs as sw​(Pn)=(s1​(w),…,sn​(w))∈ℝns_{w}(P_{n})=(s_{1}(w),\ldots,s_{n}(w))\in\mathbb{R}^{n} with si​(w):=fw​(xi,Pn(−i))s_{i}(w):=f_{w}(x_{i};P_{n}^{(-i)}) and targets y∈ℝny\in\mathbb{R}^{n}; then r=y−sw0​(Pn)r=y-s_{w_{0}}(P_{n}). Let Jw:=∇wsw​(Pn)|w=w0∈ℝn×pJ_{w}:=\nabla_{w}s_{w}(P_{n})\big|_{w=w_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}. Since w⁡(θ)=w0+A​θw(\theta)=w_{0}+A\theta, the Jacobian in update parameters is
∇θsw⁡(θ)​(Pn)|θ=0=Jw​A\nabla_{\theta}s_{w(\theta)}(P_{n})\big|_{\theta=0}=J_{w}A, i.e., Φ=Jw​A\Phi=J_{w}A, and thus K=Φ​Φ⊤=(Jw​A)​(Jw​A)⊤=Jw​Π𝒰​Jw⊤K=\Phi\Phi^{\top}=(J_{w}A)(J_{w}A)^{\top}=J_{w}\Pi_{\mathcal{U}}J_{w}^{\top}.
3.3 Specializing the Prompt Kernel to Transformers
We specialize KK to Transformer attention blocks [47] under a local frozen-feature regime. Specifically, we evaluate all forward activations (including attention weights) and upstream gradients at w0w_{0} and treat them as constants (i.e., we ignore their dependence on the test-time updates), in the spirit of Chizat et al. [11], Fu et al. [15]. See Appendix H.1 for detailed discussion.
Let h∈{1,…,H}h\in\{1,\dots,H\} index attention heads. We index the nn supervised prompt positions (tokens) by k∈{1,…,n}k\in\{1,\dots,n\} and define the head output at token kk by
ok(h):=WO(h)​uk(h),uk(h):=∑s≤kαk​s(h)​WV(h)​zs,o_{k}^{(h)}:=W_{O}^{(h)}u_{k}^{(h)},u_{k}^{(h)}:=\sum_{s\leq k}\alpha_{ks}^{(h)}W_{V}^{(h)}z_{s},
where zsz_{s} is the residual stream and αk​s(h)\alpha_{ks}^{(h)} are attention weights (evaluated at w0w_{0}). Under tokenwise supervision (i≡ki\equiv k), let gk(h):=∇ok(h)sk​(w)|w0g_{k}^{(h)}:=\nabla_{o_{k}^{(h)}}s_{k}(w)\big|_{w_{0}} be the upstream gradient.
Define mk(h):=∑s≤kαk​s(h)​zsm_{k}^{(h)}:=\sum_{s\leq k}\alpha_{ks}^{(h)}z_{s} and βk(h):=(WO(h))⊤​gk(h),\beta_{k}^{(h)}:=(W_{O}^{(h)})^{\top}g_{k}^{(h)}, so uk(h)=WV(h)​mk(h).u_{k}^{(h)}=W_{V}^{(h)}m_{k}^{(h)}. Then the Jacobian feature w.r.t. WV(h)W_{V}^{(h)} is mk(h)⊗βk(h)m_{k}^{(h)}\otimes\beta_{k}^{(h)} and Kk​k′(h,V)=(mk(h)⊤​mk′(h))​(βk(h)⊤​βk′(h))K^{(h,V)}_{kk^{\prime}}=(m_{k}^{(h)\top}m_{k^{\prime}}^{(h)})(\beta_{k}^{(h)\top}\beta_{k^{\prime}}^{(h)}).
Stack Z=[z1⊤;…;zn⊤]Z=[z_{1}^{\top};\dots;z_{n}^{\top}], 𝒜k​s(h)=αk​s(h)\mathcal{A}^{(h)}_{ks}=\alpha^{(h)}_{ks}, M(h):=𝒜(h)​ZM^{(h)}:=\mathcal{A}^{(h)}Z, and 𝐁(h)=[β1(h)⊤;…;βn(h)⊤]\mathbf{B}^{(h)}=[\beta_{1}^{(h)\top};\dots;\beta_{n}^{(h)\top}].
Then
K(h,V)=(M(h)​M(h)⊤)⊙(𝐁(h)​𝐁(h)⊤),K^{(h,V)}=\bigl(M^{(h)}M^{(h)\top}\bigr)\odot\bigl(\mathbf{B}^{(h)}\mathbf{B}^{(h)\top}\bigr),
(3)
and if gk(h)≡g(h)g_{k}^{(h)}\equiv g^{(h)} (hence βk(h)≡β(h)\beta_{k}^{(h)}\equiv\beta^{(h)}),
K(h,V)=‖β(h)‖22​𝒜(h)​(Z​Z⊤)​𝒜(h)⊤.K^{(h,V)}=\|\beta^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathcal{A}^{(h)}(ZZ^{\top})\mathcal{A}^{(h)\top}.
(4)
4 Few-step TTT as implicit Bayesian inference
This section shows that the resulting update is a spectral filter and admits an exact auxiliary Bayesian interpretation.
Gradient descent dynamics.
To analyze the update dynamics under the local linear–quadratic surrogate of Sec. 3, fix a prompt PnP_{n} and consider gradient descent on the quadratic prompt loss L⁡(θ)L(\theta) starting from θ0=0\theta_{0}=0. With a scaled step size ρ:=η/σ2\rho:=\eta/\sigma^{2}, the parameter update is
θt+1=θt−η∇L(θt)=θt+ρΦ⊤(r−Φθt).\theta_{t+1}=\theta_{t}-\eta\nabla L(\theta_{t})=\theta_{t}+\rho\,\Phi^{\top}(r-\Phi\theta_{t}).
(5)
Multiplying (5) by Φ\Phi yields the dynamics of the in-prompt prediction correction f^t:=Φ​θt\hat{f}_{t}:=\Phi\theta_{t} as f^t+1=f^t+ρ​K​(r−f^t),\hat{f}_{t+1}=\hat{f}_{t}+\rho K(r-\hat{f}_{t}), where K:=Φ​Φ⊤K:=\Phi\Phi^{\top}. This reveals that TTT operates on rr solely through K⁡(Pn)K(P_{n}).
Spectral filtering.
In this setup, the standard early-stopping identity [54, 6] gives f^T=GT​r\hat{f}_{T}=G_{T}r, where GT:=In−(In−ρ​K)TG_{T}:=I_{n}-(I_{n}-\rho K)^{T}. If K=U​diag​(λ1,…,λn)​U⊤K=U\,\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\,U^{\top} denotes an eigen-decomposition with λi≥0\lambda_{i}\geq 0, then
f^T=U​diag​(gT​(λ1),…,gT​(λn))​U⊤​r,\hat{f}_{T}=U\,\mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(g_{T}(\lambda_{1}),\dots,g_{T}(\lambda_{n})\bigr)\,U^{\top}r,
(6)
where gT​(λ):=1−(1−ρ​λ)Tg_{T}(\lambda):=1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T}. Set 0<ρ<1/λmax​(K)0<\rho<1/\lambda_{\max}(K) to ensure gT​(λ)∈[0,1)g_{T}(\lambda)\in[0,1) for all λ∈[0,λmax​(K)]\lambda\in[0,\lambda_{\max}(K)] and T<∞T<\infty.
Under the same linearized model, the TT-step update also induces a query-point correction of the form
h^T​(x):=ϕ​(x,Pn)⊤​θT=kx⊤​qT​(K)​r\hat{h}_{T}(x):=\phi(x;P_{n})^{\top}\theta_{T}=k_{x}^{\top}q_{T}(K)\,r, where kx:=Φ​ϕ​(x,Pn)∈ℝnk_{x}:=\Phi\,\phi(x;P_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n} and
qT​(λ):={1−(1−ρ​λ)T}/λq_{T}(\lambda):=\{1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T}\}/\lambda for λ>0\lambda>0 (with qT​(0):=ρ​Tq_{T}(0):=\rho T). Thus, to understand when TTT helps, it suffices to compare the induced filter qTq_{T} with the Bayes-optimal filter over the spectrum of KK, with spectral weights scaled by the alignment of kxk_{x} with the eigenvectors of KK (Sec. 5).
Bayesian view.
Recalling that the quadratic prompt objective corresponds to a Gaussian observation model with noise level σ2\sigma^{2} as in Sec. 3.2, we next show that the smoother r↦f^Tr\mapsto\hat{f}_{T} admits an exact representation as a Gaussian posterior mean. This interprets the algorithm-induced map GTG_{T}, and we do not assume the true residuals are generated from this prior. This view treats TT as a hyperparameter and selects it based on evidence (Sec. 7).
Proposition 4.1 (TTT equals a Gaussian posterior mean).
Assume 0<ρ<1/λmax​(K)0<\rho<1/\lambda_{\max}(K).
There exists a Gaussian prior f∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​GT​(In−GT)−1)f\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}G_{T}\,(I_{n}-G_{T})^{-1}) such that, under the auxiliary observation model r=f+ε,ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)r=f+\varepsilon,\,\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}), the posterior mean satisfies 𝔼⁡[f∣r]=f^T\mathbb{E}[f\mid r]=\hat{f}_{T}.
The result suggests that TTT corresponds to Bayesian inference under a prompt-induced Gaussian prior, in which the prior shape (eigenvectors) and the prior strength (eigenvalues via TT) vary across prompts. In particular, changing which attention blocks/heads are updated changes the eigenmodes of KK, i.e., it changes the shape of the implicit prior.
Detailed derivations and discussions regarding extensions to other loss functions are provided in the Appendix E.2.
5 When does TTT help? Bayes-gap identities
Sec. 4 shows that few-step TTT applies a spectral filter to the prompt residuals through K⁡(Pn)K(P_{n}). For prediction, however, our interest lies in the induced query-point correction: under the local linear model (Sec. 3), h^q​(x)=kx⊤​q​(K)​r\hat{h}_{q}(x)=k_{x}^{\top}q(K)r with kx:=Φ​ϕ​(x,Pn)k_{x}:=\Phi\,\phi(x;P_{n}). See Prop. E.1 for the derivation.
To keep the presentation simple and make the Bayes-optimal query correction explicit, we adopt the following Gaussian process benchmark [37]. Below, fBayes​(x)f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(x) denotes the posterior mean under a benchmark (a Bayes-optimal correction).
Assumption 5.1 (Gaussian benchmark).
Fix prompt inputs (x1,…,xn)(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}) and a query input xx. Let K⪰0K\succeq 0 be the prompt geometry and let the prompt–query covariance vector kxk_{x} and the query variance κx\kappa_{x} satisfy (Kkxkx⊤κx)⪰0\begin{pmatrix}K&k_{x}\\
k_{x}^{\top}&\kappa_{x}\end{pmatrix}\succeq 0. Assume latent functions (f⋆,fx)(f_{\star},f_{x}) are Gaussian with mean 00 and covariance τ2​(Kkxkx⊤κx)\tau^{2}\begin{pmatrix}K&k_{x}\\
k_{x}^{\top}&\kappa_{x}\end{pmatrix}, and we observe prompt residuals r=f⋆+εr=f_{\star}+\varepsilon with ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}) independent. Write λ⋆:=σ2/τ2\lambda_{\star}:=\sigma^{2}/\tau^{2}.
We do not claim real TTT residuals follow this model; rather, it provides an interpretable baseline for quantifying filter mismatch and motivates extensions beyond Gaussianity. See Appendix F.2 and F.3 for extensions.
Theorem 5.2 (Conditional Bayes-gap identity).
Grant Assumption 5.1 and let K=U​diag​(λi)​U⊤K=U\,\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{i})\,U^{\top} be an eigendecomposition with eigenvectors {ui}i=1n\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}. The Bayes-optimal estimator (posterior mean) of the query correction is fBayes​(x)=kx⊤​(K+λ⋆​In)−1​rf_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(x)=k_{x}^{\top}(K+\lambda_{\star}I_{n})^{-1}r. Moreover, for any kernel-linear estimator h^q​(x)=kx⊤​q​(K)​r\hat{h}_{q}(x)=k_{x}^{\top}q(K)\,r with q⋆​(λ):=(λ+λ⋆)−1q_{\star}(\lambda):=(\lambda+\lambda_{\star})^{-1}, the Bayes gap satisfies 𝔼[{h^q(x)−fBayes(x)}2∣K,kx,κx]=∑i=1nai(x){q(λi)−q⋆(λi)}2\mathbb{E}\big[\{\hat{h}_{q}(x)-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(x)\}^{2}\mid K,k_{x},\kappa_{x}\big]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(x)\,\bigl\{q(\lambda_{i})-q_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\bigr\}^{2}, where the mode weights are ai​(x):=(τ2​λi+σ2)​(ui⊤​kx)2a_{i}(x):=(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\,(u_{i}^{\top}k_{x})^{2}.
Thm. 5.2 suggests that achieving a small Bayes gap at the query requires both: (i) filter match, meaning that qT​(λi)q_{T}(\lambda_{i}) is close to q⋆​(λi)=(λi+λ⋆)−1q_{\star}(\lambda_{i})=(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{\star})^{-1} on the modes with large weight ai​(x)a_{i}(x); and (ii) eigen-alignment, since only modes with non-negligible (ui⊤​kx)2(u_{i}^{\top}k_{x})^{2} contribute through ai​(x)a_{i}(x). This perspective parallels spectral analyses of generalization on kernel regression [8]. Importantly, simply fitting the model to the prompt is not sufficient.
Specialization to TTT and ICL (Corollary E.5).
TTT uses q=qTq=q_{T}, while naive ICL uses q≡0q\equiv 0. Thus, conditioned on (K,kx,κx)(K,k_{x},\kappa_{x}), TTT improves over ICL in terms of the Bayes gap if and only if ∑i=1nai​(x)​{qT​(λi)−q⋆​(λi)}2<∑i=1nai​(x)​q⋆​(λi)2\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(x)\,\bigl\{q_{T}(\lambda_{i})-q_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\bigr\}^{2}<\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(x)\,q_{\star}(\lambda_{i})^{2}.
Takeaway (C1-1): When TTT helps.
TTT improves upon ICL via 1. Optimal Steps: the step count tunes shrinkage (so that qTq_{T} matches q⋆q_{\star}), avoiding underfitting/overfitting; 2. Geometric Alignment: the update subspace contains directions learned from the prompt that are also aligned with the query prediction.
5.1 Geometry and Dynamics of Transformer TTT
Prompt geometry as token coupling.
In a Transformer, the prompt geometry K=Φ​Φ⊤K=\Phi\Phi^{\top} is an n×nn\times n token–token Gram matrix: Kk​k′=⟨∇θsk,∇θsk′⟩K_{kk^{\prime}}=\langle\nabla_{\theta}s_{k},\nabla_{\theta}s_{k^{\prime}}\rangle measures the extent to which a small update (within the chosen update subspace) changes the outputs at tokens kk and k′k^{\prime} in similar directions. For value-matrix updates in head hh, K(h,V)=(M(h)​M(h)⊤)⊙(𝐁(h)​𝐁(h)⊤)K^{(h,V)}=(M^{(h)}M^{(h)\top})\odot(\mathbf{B}^{(h)}\mathbf{B}^{(h)\top}) (Eq. (3)), so a token pair is emphasized only when it is both similar in attention-mixed representations M(h)M^{(h)} and aligned in upstream gradient directions 𝐁(h)\mathbf{B}^{(h)}. In the global-upstream regime, this simplifies to K(h,V)=‖β(h)‖22​𝒜(h)​(Z​Z⊤)​𝒜(h)⊤K^{(h,V)}=\|\beta^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathcal{A}^{(h)}(ZZ^{\top})\mathcal{A}^{(h)\top}, an attention-sandwiched token-similarity matrix (symmetric PSD even for causal 𝒜(h)\mathcal{A}^{(h)}). Since head/block contributions sum to K=∑b∈ℬK(b)K=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}}K^{(b)}, choosing which blocks/heads to update determines which eigen-directions in token-space are available for test-time correction.
Filtering dynamics and query coupling.
Few-step TTT applies the polynomial filter f^T=∑t=0T−1ρ​K​(I−ρ​K)t​r\hat{f}_{T}=\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\rho K(I-\rho K)^{t}r, which can be viewed as diffusion/smoothing of residual information over this attention-induced token geometry; the horizon TT sets the diffusion time and thus the amount of shrinkage across eigenmodes. Finally, Thm. 5.2 implies that query-point improvement comes only from eigen-directions that also align with the prompt-query vector kxk_{x} (weights (ui⊤​kx)2(u_{i}^{\top}k_{x})^{2}), so head/block choices matter both through the spectrum of KK and through its coupling to the query.
6 Necessity of adapting the update steps and directions
The identity in Theorem 5.2 identifies two requirements for query improvement: the update filter must match the prompt signal-to-noise structure, and the updated eigendirections must align with the query.
In this section, we show that fixing the number of update steps TT or the update subspace AA as in standard optimization can fail. We then argue for the necessity of prompt-dependent step selection and subspace design.
Recall from Sec. 4 that, for a fixed prompt PnP_{n}, few-step TTT on the quadratic prompt loss produces an in-prompt correction f^T=gT​(K)​r\hat{f}_{T}=g_{T}(K)\,r with filter gT​(λ)=1−(1−ρ​λ)Tg_{T}(\lambda)=1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T}. In this section, we consider the broader class of prompt-only spectral estimators f^g:=g⁡(K)​r\hat{f}_{g}:=g(K)\,r for measurable g:[0,∞)→ℝg:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R} with g⁡(0)=0g(0)=0, and denote by 𝒮⁡(Pn)=range⁡(K⁡(Pn))\mathcal{S}(P_{n})=\mathrm{range}(K(P_{n})) the representable subspace in prompt space.
We outline the three propositions below. For the formal statements, see Appendix E.4.
Proposition 6.1 (Necessity of adapting TT under prior shift; informal).
Assume KK is a rank-mm projector with {0,1}\{0,1\}-eigenvalues. Under Assumption 5.1, the Bayes-optimal estimator is fBayes=11+λ⋆​Π𝒮​rf_{\mathrm{Bayes}}=\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{\star}}\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}r, which varies with the test-time SNR 1λ⋆\tfrac{1}{\lambda_{\star}}. Hence, no fixed horizon TT can be Bayes-optimal across two different priors.
Connection to phenomenon.
Prop. 6.1 underpins empirical reports that TTT is sensitive to the number of update steps [1, 57] and that, in particular under test-time condition shifts across scenarios, fixing TT can lead to catastrophic failure [9].
Proposition 6.2 (∞\infty-steps TTT can be sub-optimal; informal).
Assume the linearized quadratic prompt loss and 0<ρ<2/λmax​(K)0<\rho<2/\lambda_{\max}(K). GD initialized at zero satisfies f^T→Π𝒮​r\hat{f}_{T}\to\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}r as T→∞T\to\infty. If dim(𝒮)≥3\dim(\mathcal{S})\geq 3 (with known Gaussian noise level), there exists a shrinkage rule within 𝒮\mathcal{S} that reduces its squared-error risk.
Connection to phenomenon. Prior experiments have reported that increasing the number of test-time adaptation steps can eventually hurt performance due to overfitting [57, 2]. Prop. 6.2 provides a principled explanation of this.
Proposition 6.3 (Irreducible error of subspace-mismatch; informal).
For any target f⋆∈ℝnf_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, the TTT estimator f^gT\hat{f}_{g_{T}} satisfies infT≥0‖f^gT−f⋆‖22≥‖Π𝒮⟂​f⋆‖22\inf_{T\geq 0}\|\hat{f}_{g_{T}}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\|\Pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}.
In the global-upstream regime (4), the representable prompt-space directions satisfy
range⁡(K(h,V))=range⁡(𝒜(h)​Z)\mathrm{range}(K^{(h,V)})=\mathrm{range}(\mathcal{A}^{(h)}Z); see Lem. H.3 in the
Appendix.
Connection to phenomenon.
Prop. 6.3 formalizes a representational bottleneck, that is, regardless of hyperparameters, the correction f^g\hat{f}_{g} lies in 𝒮\mathcal{S}, so components outside 𝒮\mathcal{S} are unavoidable. Consistent with this view, Zhang et al. [56] discuss gains from scaling test-time state capacity (expanding 𝒮\mathcal{S} in our context). However, simply expanding the update space is not sufficient. Choi et al. [12] report settings where full-parameter updates can degrade performance, motivating careful design of the update mechanism AA (and hence the induced 𝒮\mathcal{S}).
Takeaway (C1-2): why TT and AA must adapt
A fixed TT can be Bayes-suboptimal under test-time prior shift; taking T→∞T\!\to\!\infty can overfit; and any fixed representable subspace induces an irreducible mismatch floor.
In addition, Appendix G explains how the spectrum of K⁡(Pn)K(P_{n}) governs when a fixed TT can (or cannot) match Bayes shrinkage across modes via the gradient flow and random matrix theory (RMT).
7 Per-prompt update steps via PAC-Bayes
The first failure mode above concerns how far to adapt.
Building on the evidence (marginal likelihood) framework [28], we interpret TT as a hyperparameter in the implicit Bayesian formulation of Section 4 and analyze it via PAC-Bayes [10, 3].
Evidence family induced by TTT.
Fix a prompt-induced geometry K⪰0K\succeq 0 and ρ∈(0,1/λmax​(K))\rho\in(0,1/\lambda_{\max}(K)). Motivated by Prop. 4.1, we consider the associated auxiliary evidence family in which rr is distributed according to the Gaussian marginal induced by the implicit prior:
r∣(K,T)∼𝒩(0,ΣT),ΣT=σ2(I−ρK)−T.r\mid(K,T)\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{T}),\quad\Sigma_{T}=\sigma^{2}(I-\rho K)^{-T}.
This induces a data-dependent score for TT, given by the normalized negative log-evidence (ignoring TT-independent constants) ℓT(r):=12​n{logdet(ΣT)+r⊤ΣT−1r}\ell_{T}(r):=\frac{1}{2n}\big\{\log\det\nolimits(\Sigma_{T})+r^{\top}\Sigma_{T}^{-1}r\big\}. We will use these scores to form a posterior over TT via a Gibbs construction introduced below.
To analyze the statistical validity of selecting TT via this objective, we formalize the data generating process as follows.
Assumption 7.1 (Reference model for residuals).
Conditional on KK, the true residual vector is Gaussian: r∣K∼𝒩(0,Σ⋆),Σ⋆≻0.r\mid K\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star}),\,\Sigma_{\star}\succ 0.
Under this reference model, define the normalized population cross-entropy risk as ℒ⁡(T∣K):=𝔼⁡[ℓT​(r)∣K]\mathcal{L}(T\mid K):=\mathbb{E}[\ell_{T}(r)\mid K]. Since Σ⋆\Sigma_{\star} is unknown, we use the observed prompt residuals rr to evaluate ℓT​(r)\ell_{T}(r) across candidates and convert these scores into the Gibbs posterior over TT introduced next.
Gibbs posterior over update steps.
Fix a finite candidate set 𝒯⊂ℕ0\mathcal{T}\subset\mathbb{N}_{0} and a prior π0\pi_{0} on 𝒯\mathcal{T} with full support. For β>0\beta>0, define the Gibbs posterior νβ​(T∣r)∝π0​(T)​exp⁡{−β​n​ℓT​(r)}\nu_{\beta}(T\mid r)\propto\pi_{0}(T)\exp\{-\beta n\,\ell_{T}(r)\}. This posterior can be used either to select a point estimate TMAP∈arg⁡maxT​νβ​(T∣r)T_{\mathrm{MAP}}\in\arg\max_{T}\nu_{\beta}(T\mid r) or to perform model averaging (mixture-of-filters [13]) G¯:=𝔼T∼νβ​[gT​(K)]\bar{G}:=\mathbb{E}_{T\sim\nu_{\beta}}[g_{T}(K)]. When π0\pi_{0} is uniform, TMAPT_{\mathrm{MAP}} coincides with evidence minimization [16], defined as TMAP∈arg⁡minT∈𝒯​ℓT​(r)T_{\mathrm{MAP}}\in\arg\min_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\ell_{T}(r).
Formally, this Gibbs posterior is defined as a Radon–Nikodym tilt of π0\pi_{0}; see Remark E.10.
Theorem 7.2 (PAC-Bayes guarantee for
selecting TT).
Consider a point mass posterior ν=δT\nu=\delta_{T} and uniform prior π0\pi_{0} on 𝒯\mathcal{T}. Grant Assumption 7.1 and let 𝒯\mathcal{T} be finite. Define AT:=Σ⋆1/2​ΣT−1​Σ⋆1/2⪰0A_{T}:=\Sigma_{\star}^{1/2}\Sigma_{T}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star}^{1/2}\succeq 0 and v¯:=supT∈𝒯1n​tr​(AT2)\bar{v}:=\sup_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2}). Then, for any δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta over r|Kr\mid K, choosing β=2​{log⁡|𝒯|+log⁡(1/δ)}/n​v¯\beta=2\sqrt{\{\log|\mathcal{T}|+\log(1/\delta)\}/n\bar{v}} yields
ℒ⁡(TMAP∣K)≤ℓTMAP​(r)+v¯​(log⁡|𝒯|+log⁡(1/δ))n.\mathcal{L}(T_{\mathrm{MAP}}\mid K)\leq\ell_{T_{\mathrm{MAP}}}(r)+\sqrt{\frac{\bar{v}\bigl(\log|\mathcal{T}|+\log(1/\delta)\bigr)}{n}}.
(7)
Thm. 7.2 controls the population negative log-evidence of the prompt-residual model. It is not an end-to-end theorem that the evidence-minimizing horizon is always query-risk optimal. The link to prediction is through the shared filter: Sec. 5 shows that the query correction uses the same qT​(K)q_{T}(K), weighted by the prompt–query coupling kxk_{x}, while Sec. 8 handles query-dependent update directions. Thus, evidence selects the shrinkage level within the TTT filter family; query-aware subspace selection addresses the remaining query dependence. Under the GP reference model, App. E.5.3 further relates evidence closeness to a normalized Bayes gap.
Takeaway (C2): per-prompt TT selection
Selecting TT by prompt evidence enjoys a PAC-Bayes bound for the out-of-sample negative log-evidence, so tuning TT on the prompt does not overfit the prompt residuals.
8 Bayes-optimal subspace selection
Evidence selection chooses how far to move within a fixed prompt geometry; it does not by itself choose which geometry is most useful for the query.
We next turn from the update horizon to the update subspace.
The update subspace should capture query-aware prompt signal since h^T​(x)=kx⊤​qT​(K)​r\hat{h}_{T}(x)=k_{x}^{\top}q_{T}(K)\,r (Sec. 4) and improvements come primarily from eigenmodes aligned with kxk_{x} (Thm. 5.2). We formalize this test-time update subspace as a Bayesian design variable, in the spirit of Attia et al. [4].
Assumption 8.1 (Gaussian benchmark of subspace).
There exists a latent parameter correction u⋆∈ℝpu_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p} such that u⋆∼𝒩⁡(0,Σ)u_{\star}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma), r=Jw​u⋆+εr=J_{w}u_{\star}+\varepsilon, ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}), and u⋆⟂εu_{\star}\perp\varepsilon, where Σ≻0\Sigma\succ 0 is task-to-task variability and Jw:=∇wsw​(Pn)|w=w0∈ℝn×pJ_{w}:=\nabla_{w}s_{w}(P_{n})|_{w=w_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}.
This assumption is the subspace version of Assumption 5.1 (see Lem. E.20). For a query input xx, denote the linearized query correction by fx:=jx⊤​u⋆f_{x}:=j_{x}^{\top}u_{\star}, where jx:=∇wfw0​(x,Pn)∈ℝpj_{x}:=\nabla_{w}f_{w_{0}}(x;P_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{p}.
8.1 Bayes-optimal update subspace
To focus on query prediction, we assess an estimator u^​(r)\widehat{u}(r) by the mean squared error of the linearized query correction 𝔼⁡[{jX⊤​(u^−u⋆)}2]\mathbb{E}\!\left[\{j_{X}^{\top}(\widehat{u}-u_{\star})\}^{2}\right], where the expectation is over the test-time query XX (conditioned on the prompt, unless stated otherwise). Define Q:=𝔼⁡[jX​jX⊤]⪰0Q:=\mathbb{E}\!\left[j_{X}j_{X}^{\top}\right]\succeq 0. Then 𝔼⁡[{jX⊤​(u^−u⋆)}2]=𝔼⁡[‖u^−u⋆‖Q2]\mathbb{E}[\{j_{X}^{\top}(\widehat{u}-u_{\star})\}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\|\widehat{u}-u_{\star}\|_{Q}^{2}] with ‖v‖Q2:=v⊤​Q​v\|v\|_{Q}^{2}:=v^{\top}Qv.
To align the subspace constraint with the query metric, we reparameterize via v:=Q1/2​uv:=Q^{1/2}u (restricted to range⁡(Q)\mathrm{range}(Q) if QQ is singular), so that ‖u‖Q2=‖v‖22\|u\|_{Q}^{2}=\|v\|_{2}^{2}. We choose an update subspace of dimension budget kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} by selecting a rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} Euclidean projector Π~(=Π~⊤=Π~2)\widetilde{\Pi}(=\widetilde{\Pi}^{\top}=\widetilde{\Pi}^{2}) acting on vv, and restrict estimators to v^​(r)∈range​(Π~)\widehat{v}(r)\in\mathrm{range}(\widetilde{\Pi}).
Let u¯​(r):=𝔼⁡[u⋆∣r]\bar{u}(r):=\mathbb{E}[u_{\star}\mid r] denote the Bayes estimator under the linear-Gaussian model, and
set v¯​(r):=Q1/2​u¯​(r)\bar{v}(r):=Q^{1/2}\bar{u}(r). The key quantity for subspace design is how much recoverable signal the prompt provides about u⋆u_{\star}, captured by the covariance of the posterior mean: ℐ:=Var⁡(u¯​(r))=Σ​Jw⊤​(Jw​Σ​Jw⊤+σ2​In)−1​Jw​Σ.\mathcal{I}:=\mathrm{Var}(\bar{u}(r))=\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}(J_{w}\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}J_{w}\Sigma. Its query-whitened version ℐQ:=Var⁡(v¯​(r))=Q1/2​ℐ​Q1/2\mathcal{I}_{Q}:=\mathrm{Var}(\bar{v}(r))=Q^{1/2}\mathcal{I}Q^{1/2} will determine the Bayes-optimal rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} update subspace.
Theorem 8.2 (Optimal rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} update subspace).
Under Assumption 8.1, define the query-weighted Bayes risk
ℛ⁡(Π~)\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\widetilde{\Pi})
:=infv^​(⋅)∈range​(Π~)𝔼⁡[‖v^​(r)−Q1/2​u⋆‖22]\displaystyle:=\inf_{\widehat{v}(\cdot)\in\mathrm{range}(\widetilde{\Pi})}\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\widehat{v}(r)-Q^{1/2}u_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\right]
=tr⁡(Q​Σ)−tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ).\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}(Q\Sigma)-\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\mathcal{I}_{Q}).
Then, any minimizer over rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} projectors Π~\widetilde{\Pi} is the projector onto the top-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} eigenspace of ℐQ\mathcal{I}_{Q}.
To realize the designed subspace in the original coordinates uu, map back from v=Q1/2​uv=Q^{1/2}u. If Q≻0Q\succ 0, the corresponding uu-space subspace is Q−1/2range(Π~)Q^{-1/2}\mathrm{range}(\widetilde{\Pi}). If QQ is singular, interpret Q−1/2Q^{-1/2} as the pseudoinverse on range⁡(Q)\mathrm{range}(Q).
By the law of total variance, the QQ-weighted total variability of the correction Var⁡(Q1/2​u⋆)\mathrm{Var}(Q^{1/2}u_{\star}) decomposes into ℐQ\mathcal{I}_{Q} (recoverable from the prompt) and 𝔼⁡[Q1/2​Var​(u⋆∣r)​Q1/2]\mathbb{E}\!\left[Q^{1/2}\mathrm{Var}(u_{\star}\mid r)\,Q^{1/2}\right] (irreducible).
Hence, tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ)\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\mathcal{I}_{Q}) is the query-aligned posterior-mean variability recovered by the update subspace. This is large when the subspace both (i) contains directions whose posterior means are identifiable from the prompt and (ii) aligns with query sensitivities (via QQ), consistent with the eigen-alignment findings of Thm. 5.2. A global (population-level) update mechanism can be obtained by averaging ℐQ\mathcal{I}_{Q} across prompts and applying a plug-in top-eigenspace estimator. Details and a martingale-type regret bound are deferred to Appendix E.6.
8.2 Block/head selection for Transformer
Transformer partitioning.
To obtain a practical approximation to Thm. 8.2, we restrict the update projector to be aligned with a Transformer parameter partition (motivated by previous analyses showing that attention-head contributions are so uneven that many heads are prunable [31, 49]).
Specifically, we partition parameters into disjoint blocks (e.g., by layer/head/matrix type), writing the corresponding Jacobian block columns by Jw=[J(b)]b∈ℬallJ_{w}=[J^{(b)}]_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm all}}, and restrict updates to a block-diagonal projector Π=diag​(Π(b))b∈ℬall\Pi=\mathrm{diag}\bigl(\Pi^{(b)}\bigr)_{b\in\mathcal{B}_{\rm all}}, which includes block on/off selection (Π(b)∈{0,Ipb}\Pi^{(b)}\in\{0,I_{p_{b}}\}).
For block-diagonal Π\Pi, let ℐb​b\mathcal{I}_{bb} denote the diagonal block of ℐ\mathcal{I} for block bb. If we specify QQ by a single query xx (i.e., Q=jx​jx⊤Q=j_{x}j_{x}^{\top}) and perform on/off selection, then the risk reduction attributable to block bb is proportional to the score
jx(b)⊤​ℐb​b​jx(b)(Query-Aware),j_{x}^{(b)\top}\mathcal{I}_{bb}j_{x}^{(b)}\qquad(\textsc{Query-Aware}),
(8)
where jx(b)j_{x}^{(b)} is the restriction of jxj_{x} to block bb. We refer to ranking blocks by this score as Query-Aware selection.
Under the isotropic correction prior Σ=Ip\Sigma=I_{p}, we have ℐ=Jw⊤​(Kfull+σ2​In)−1​Jw\mathcal{I}=J_{w}^{\top}(K_{\rm full}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}J_{w} with Kfull:=Jw​Jw⊤K_{\rm full}:=J_{w}J_{w}^{\top} (the prompt geometry under full parameter updates). Let Kfull(b):=J(b)​J(b)⊤K_{\rm full}^{(b)}:=J^{(b)}J^{(b)\top}. Then the query-marginal block score becomes
tr⁡(ℐb​b)=tr⁡(Kfull(b)​(Kfull+σ2​In)−1).(Trace-TopK)\mathrm{tr}\bigl(\mathcal{I}_{bb}\bigr)=\mathrm{tr}\!\left(K_{\rm full}^{(b)}(K_{\rm full}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}\right).\quad(\textsc{Trace-TopK})
(9)
Selecting the top-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} blocks by this score corresponds to the Trace-TopK baseline in Sec.9. In contrast, for a specific query xx, the Query-Aware score can be written as kx(b)⊤​(Kfull+σ2​In)−1​kx(b)k_{x}^{(b)\top}(K_{\rm full}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}k_{x}^{(b)}, where kx(b):=J(b)​jx(b)k_{x}^{(b)}:=J^{(b)}j_{x}^{(b)} is the prompt-query coupling induced by block bb. The factor (Kfull+σ2​In)−1(K_{\rm full}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1} downweights directions already explained by other blocks (or dominated by noise), making the score explicitly redundancy-aware and query-aligned.
For on/off selection Π(b)∈{0,Ipb}\Pi^{(b)}\in\{0,I_{p_{b}}\} under a block/parameter budget, we rank blocks by an appropriate score (e.g., Query-Aware: jx(b)⊤​ℐb​b​jx(b)j_{x}^{(b)\top}\mathcal{I}_{bb}j_{x}^{(b)} when Q=jx​jx⊤Q=j_{x}j_{x}^{\top}), optionally normalized by pbp_{b}. For a global update mechanism, use the query-averaged Q=𝔼X​[jX​jX⊤]Q=\mathbb{E}_{X}[j_{X}j_{X}^{\top}].
The exact query-specific Query-Aware score requires one backward pass through the query output to obtain jxj_{x}. This overhead is additive rather than per-block: a single query backward pass gives jxj_{x} for all blocks, after which the block-specific vectors jx(b)j_{x}^{(b)} are obtained by slicing. The remaining inverses and trace scores are in prompt space (dimension nn) and can be implemented with Jacobian–vector products without materializing JwJ_{w}. When per-query latency is undesirable, the query-averaged choice Q=𝔼X​[jX​jX⊤]Q=\mathbb{E}_{X}[j_{X}j_{X}^{\top}] gives an amortized global design that removes the per-query backward pass while remaining the Bayes-optimal solution for the averaged query objective; see Appendix H.4 for details.
Takeaway (C3): optimal update directions
For TTT prediction, the Bayes-optimal rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} update subspace is the top-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} eigenspace of ℐQ\mathcal{I}_{Q}. For Transformer, this yields query-aware scores for attention block/head selection.
9 Experiments
The experiments are performed for mechanism-level checks of the theory rather than a comprehensive benchmark study. Experiment 1 tests whether evidence selects prompt-dependent horizons, and Experiment 2 tests whether query-aligned directions improve query prediction more reliably than prompt-fit directions.
Figure 1: (a) Evidence adaptively selects TT (Exp1). ECDF of the selected TMAPT_{\mathrm{MAP}}. The optimal TT is prompt-dependent, so adapting TT is preferable to using a fixed TT (Claims (C1)–(C2)). See Table 1 for predictive performance. (b) Query gains depend on alignment (Exp2). Query-MSE improvement relative to random selection (Δ>0\Delta>0 is better). Query-Aware consistently outperforms Trace-TopK (Claims (C1), (C3)). (c) Prompt fit is a different objective (Exp2). The same Δ​MSE\Delta\mathrm{MSE} evaluated using prompt-fit MSE. Trace-TopK tends to improve prompt fit, but this does not necessarily generalize to query improvement in (b). Error bars indicate standard error of the mean across tasks.
Experimental setup.
We use a digit-shift task with distilgpt2 [53].
We generate 2,000 tasks, each sampled from one of two settings: clean (pnoise=0.0p_{\text{noise}}=0.0) and noisy (pnoise=0.55p_{\text{noise}}=0.55).
For each task, we sample a shift s∼Unif⁡(0,…,9)s\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,\ldots,9), draw digits x∼Unif⁡(0,…,9)x\sim\mathrm{Unif}(0,\ldots,9), and set labels y=(x+s)mod10y=(x+s)\bmod 10.
Each test instance contains n=10n=10 prompt pairs (x,y)(x,y) and an independent query digit xx from the same task. Prompt labels are corrupted independently with probability pnoisep_{\text{noise}} by replacing yy with a uniformly random digit in {0,…,9}\{0,\ldots,9\}.
We update only attention Value weights at the (layer, head) level via gradient descent on leave-one-out prompt residuals. Although the model outputs logits over digits, we treat this as a regression problem: labels are normalized to y~:=y/9\tilde{y}:=y/9 and the model’s prediction is taken as the expected digit y^=∑j=09j9​softmax​(ℓ)j\hat{y}=\sum_{j=0}^{9}\frac{j}{9}\,\mathrm{softmax}(\ell)_{j}. Full details are in Appendix I.
(Exp1) Per-prompt TT selection.
To demonstrate claim (C2), we compare ICL (no update), TTT with a fixed TT (T=8T=8), and evidence-based per-prompt selection (TMAPT_{\mathrm{MAP}})11
1
We implement two evidence variants: a fixed-σ\sigma plug-in and an MLE-σ\sigma variant; details in Appendix.. We report averaged MSE across 2,000 tasks with SE\mathrm{SE} denoting the standard error of the mean across tasks.
Table 1 shows that evidence-based TT selection improves over both ICL and fixed-TT TTT.
Although the effect sizes are modest, the gains are statistically significant under a task-matched paired analysis.
For each task, we compute the paired improvement as the baseline query MSE minus the method’s query MSE, so that positive values indicate lower error.
The best fixed horizon T=8T{=}8 improves over ICL with a 95% bootstrap confidence interval [0.000648,0.001796][0.000648,0.001796] and paired permutation p-value p=5.0×10−5p=5.0\times 10^{-5}.
Both evidence-based variants further improve over this fixed-TT baseline: fixed-σ\sigma evidence gives CI [0.000545,0.002894][0.000545,0.002894] with p=0.00325p=0.00325, and MLE-σ\sigma evidence gives CI [0.000605,0.003079][0.000605,0.003079] with p=0.00250p=0.00250.
Figure 1 shows the empirical cumulative distribution function (ECDF) of the selected TMAPT_{\mathrm{MAP}} under the fixed-σ\sigma evidence method, revealing substantial variation across prompts rather than a single globally optimal TT.
Moreover, TMAPT_{\mathrm{MAP}} responds to prompt signal-to-noise: in the noisy regime, evidence tends to select smaller TTs to mitigate overfitting, whereas, in the clean regime, it favors larger TT to maximize adaptation benefits.
(Exp2) Alignment-aware block selection.
We next investigate update-subspace design under a fixed budget of kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} blocks. We compare Query-Aware, Trace-TopK, Trace-BottomK, and random selection.
Consistent with Claim (C3), Figure 1 illustrates that the Query-Aware selection consistently outperforms random selection. Notably, the Trace-TopK baseline, which prioritizes dominant prompt features without considering the query, often fits the prompt well but fails to generalize to the query. This confirms that effective adaptation requires not just capturing prompt variance, but capturing variance aligned with the query, as predicted by (C1).

10 Discussion
We have presented a unified Bayes-risk framework for TTT, interpreting it as implicit Bayesian inference. We found that predictive improvements depend critically on filter match and eigen-alignment (C1), explaining why fixed hyperparameters often fail in practice. This Bayesian view also motivates evaluating TT through prompt evidence and yields a PAC-Bayes non-overfitting guarantee (C2). Finally, the Bayes-optimal update subspace is characterized by an information-capture matrix, leading to query-aligned block/head criteria for Transformers (C3). Experiments support these implications, highlighting the roles of adaptive regularization and query-aligned update geometry. More broadly, as the kernel regime analysis (such as NTK) informed neural network engineering, we believe this work represents an important step toward making TTT more reliable in practice.
Novelty boundary.
The implicit-Bayesian view is a conceptual lens rather than our sole technical contribution. Our novelty lies in the TTT-specific consequences of this lens: query-level Bayes-gap identities, PAC-Bayes step selection, and Bayes-optimal update-subspace design.
What is expected to transfer.
We expect two lessons to be useful beyond the exact Gaussian-quadratic model. First, the update horizon should depend on the prompt, because prompts can have different signal-to-noise levels and different overfitting risks. Second, update directions should be chosen with the query in mind, because fitting the prompt alone need not improve the query. Our Transformer derivations in Secs. 3.3 and 8.2 show how these ideas can be expressed as attention block/head geometry and query-aware scores.
Scope and future directions.
Our analysis is local. It uses restricted update parameters, a first-order approximation to the model, and a locally quadratic prompt objective. It is therefore best viewed as a theory of few-step TTT around a fixed pretrained model, rather than as a complete theory of arbitrary test-time adaptation. Appendix F.1 gives a finite-step deviation bound, but the present theory does not yet cover large updates, changing representations along the adaptation path, full-parameter TTT, or the optimization dynamics of self-supervised TTT losses.
Several extensions are natural. First, one could move from a fixed local model to a trajectory-level analysis, where the tangent features, kernel, and prompt–query coupling are allowed to evolve during adaptation. Second, one could extend the Bayesian view to non-squared and self-supervised losses, such as entropy minimization, masked prediction, consistency losses, or pseudo-label training. This would require modeling pseudo-residuals, local curvature, and the bias introduced by noisy self-supervision. Third, one could develop scalable approximations to query-aware subspace selection, for example through amortized, low-rank, or query-averaged scores for choosing Transformer blocks, heads, or adapters. Finally, broader experiments on language, vision, and tabular tasks are needed to test which parts of the local Bayesian picture transfer to realistic nonlinear TTT.

Appendix A Roadmap
This appendix provides full proofs and additional analyses supporting the main claims. We begin with a self-contained primer on the relevant decision-theoretic background. We then derive the Bayes risk identities and other theorems stated in the main text. Finally, we describe the experimental setup to facilitate reproducibility.
•
Notation and background.
We list the notation used throughout (App. B), provide a brief primer on decision theory (App. C),
and relate our setting to classical early stopping (App. D).
•
Proofs for the main results.
We provide proofs for the decision-theoretic setup (App. E.1), the spectral-filter and implicit-prior equivalence and out-of-sample (query) forms (App. E.2), and Bayes-gap identities under GP benchmarks
including the query-coupled form used in the main text (App. E.3). We provide rigorous versions of the three necessity propositions (App. E.4) and full PAC-Bayes details for evidence-based per-prompt horizon selection (App. E.5). Finally, we prove the Bayes-optimal subspace characterization, and include global-design and plug-in regret bounds
(App. E.6).
•
Validity and robustness of the local model.
We bound finite-step deviations from linearization (App. F.1), extend filter identities beyond
Gaussianity via second-moment arguments (App. F.2), and discuss anisotropic priors and
preconditioning (App. F.3).
•
Interpretability: gradient flow and random-matrix theory.
We provide a gradient-flow interpretation of the update horizon, derive deterministic oracle curves via random-matrix theory, and connect spectral properties to horizon selection (App. G).
•
Details specific to Transformers and stability.
We derive Transformer Jacobian and geometry formulas (App. H.1), include a simple step-size
stability bound (App. H.2), and characterize representable prompt subspaces for value updates
(App. H.3).
•
Experimental details.
We describe task generation, optimization and evidence procedures, and the evaluation protocol (App. I).
•
Supplementary real-data check.
We include a small SST-2 experiment with distilgpt2, using 10 labeled prompt examples and one held-out query per task, to test the same two mechanisms—evidence-based horizon selection and query-aware head selection—outside the synthetic digit-shift setting (App. J).

Appendix C Primer on Decision Theory and Bayesian Estimation
To render our theoretical framework self-contained and accessible to readers less familiar with statistical decision theory [26, 39, 33], we briefly review predictive risk, Bayes risk under our mixture task model, and the Bayes posterior (risk-minimizing) estimator. Throughout this primer, we use the squared loss and assume square-integrability (e.g., 𝔼⁡[Y2]<∞\mathbb{E}[Y^{2}]<\infty) so that conditional expectations under squared loss are well-defined. We highlight the minimal assumptions needed for each identity and indicate where they are relaxed in later appendices.
C.1 Risk and Bayes-optimal prediction for a fixed distribution
Let (Ω,ℱ,ℙ)(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}) be a probability space and let (X,Y):Ω→𝒳×ℝ(X,Y):\Omega\to\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R} be a random pair with joint law 𝒫\mathcal{P}.
A (deterministic) predictor is a measurable map M:𝒳→ℝM:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}, and the induced prediction is the random variable Y^:=M⁡(X)\widehat{Y}:=M(X).
Predictive risk.
Assume 𝔼⁡[M​(X)2]<∞\mathbb{E}[M(X)^{2}]<\infty. The (squared-loss) risk [7, 33] of MM under 𝒫\mathcal{P} is
R𝒫​(M):=𝔼⁡[(M⁡(X)−Y)2].R_{\mathcal{P}}(M)\ :=\ \mathbb{E}\bigl[(M(X)-Y)^{2}\bigr].
Even the best possible predictor incurs nonzero risk when YY is noisy given XX.
Bayes predictor under squared loss.
Any minimizer of R𝒫​(M)R_{\mathcal{P}}(M) over measurable MM with 𝔼⁡[M​(X)2]<∞\mathbb{E}[M(X)^{2}]<\infty is given (uniquely 𝒫X\mathcal{P}_{X}-a.s.) by the conditional expectation:
MBayes,𝒫​(X):=𝔼⁡[Y∣X].M_{\mathrm{Bayes},\mathcal{P}}(X)\ :=\ \mathbb{E}[Y\mid X].
Equivalently, any two minimizers agree 𝒫X\mathcal{P}_{X}-almost surely.
C.2 Bayes risk under the mixture task model
In the context of TTT and ICL, we do not face a single static distribution. Instead, we face a distribution of tasks (e.g., a mixture of different functions or domains [50]).
As in (1), let α∼π\alpha\sim\pi and, conditional on α\alpha, let (Xi,Yi)i=1n(X_{i},Y_{i})_{i=1}^{n} and (X,Y)(X,Y) be conditionally i.i.d. with law 𝒫α\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}. Let Pn:={(Xi,Yi)}i=1nP_{n}:=\{(X_{i},Y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n} and let the available information be σ\sigma-field σ⁡(Pn,X)\sigma(P_{n},X). A predictor is any σ⁡(Pn,X)\sigma(P_{n},X)-measurable map:
M:(Pn,X)↦Y^∈ℝ,M:\ (P_{n},X)\ \mapsto\ \widehat{Y}\in\mathbb{R},
with 𝔼⁡[Y^2]<∞\mathbb{E}[\widehat{Y}^{2}]<\infty.
Bayes risk [39, 33].
Assume 𝔼⁡[M​(Pn,X)2]<∞\mathbb{E}[M(P_{n},X)^{2}]<\infty. We evaluate MM by the task-averaged (Bayes) risk
ℛ⁡(M):=𝔼α∼π,Pn∼𝒫α⊗n,(X,Y)∼𝒫α​[(M⁡(Pn,X)−Y)2],\mathcal{R}(M)\ :=\ \mathbb{E}_{\alpha\sim\pi,P_{n}\sim\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{\otimes n},(X,Y)\sim\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}}\bigl[(M(P_{n},X)-Y)^{2}\bigr],
where the expectation is taken with respect to the joint distribution of (α,Pn,X,Y)(\alpha,P_{n},X,Y) induced by the mixture model in (1). Here, 𝒫α⊗n\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}^{\otimes n} denotes n-fold product (tensor) measure of 𝒫α\mathcal{P}_{\alpha}.
Bayes predictor under the mixture model.
Under squared loss, any minimizer of ℛ⁡(M)\mathcal{R}(M) over measurable maps M⁡(Pn,X)M(P_{n},X) (with finite second moment) is given (uniquely a.s.) by the conditional expectation
MBayes(Pn,X):=𝔼[Y∣Pn,X].M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(P_{n},X)\ :=\ \mathbb{E}[Y\mid P_{n},X].
Bayes-risk decomposition (Prop. 2.1).
For any predictor MM with finite second moment, the Bayes risk decomposes as
ℛ⁡(M)=𝒢⁡(M)​ (Bayes gap)+𝒱​ (posterior variance).\mathcal{R}(M)=\mathcal{G}(M)\text{ (Bayes gap)}+\mathcal{V}\text{ (posterior variance)}.
•
The posterior variance (𝒱\mathcal{V}) represents the inherent uncertainty in the targets YY that cannot be resolved even with infinite compute and perfect knowledge of the prior given σ⁡(Pn,X)\sigma(P_{n},X). It is constant with respect to the model MM.
•
The Bayes Gap (𝒢\mathcal{G}) measures how closely the model MM approximates the optimal Bayes predictor. In other words, this is the excess risk to the Bayes predictor.
To improve performance via TTT, we must reduce the Bayes Gap. This means our adapted model must essentially perform a better approximation of the conditional expectation 𝔼[Y∣Pn,X]\mathbb{E}[Y\mid P_{n},X] than the base model.
Existence and regular conditional distributions
Let 𝒢⊆ℱ\mathcal{G}\subseteq\mathcal{F} be a sub-σ\sigma-field and let Y∈L1​(ℙ)Y\in L^{1}(\mathbb{P}).
A conditional expectation 𝔼⁡[Y∣𝒢]\mathbb{E}[Y\mid\mathcal{G}] is any 𝒢\mathcal{G}-measurable random variable ZZ
satisfying ∫AZ​𝑑ℙ=∫AY​𝑑ℙ\int_{A}Z\,d\mathbb{P}=\int_{A}Y\,d\mathbb{P} for all A∈𝒢A\in\mathcal{G}.
Existence follows from the Radon–Nikodym theorem and ZZ is unique ℙ\mathbb{P}-a.s.; throughout we fix a
𝒢\mathcal{G}-measurable version when writing 𝔼[Y∣Pn,X]\mathbb{E}[Y\mid P_{n},X].
In our setting (Pn,X,Y)(P_{n},X,Y) take values in standard Borel spaces, so there exists a regular conditional distribution ℙ⁡(d​y∣Pn,X)\mathbb{P}(dy\mid P_{n},X) and, whenever YY is integrable, 𝔼[Y∣Pn,X]=∫yℙ(dy∣Pn,X)\mathbb{E}[Y\mid P_{n},X]=\int y\,\mathbb{P}(dy\mid P_{n},X) holds σ⁡(Pn,X)\sigma(P_{n},X)-a.s.
Appendix D Relation to classical optimization theory
Classical early stopping and iterative regularization analyzes gradient descent on a fixed quadratic problem, yielding the spectral filter form f^T=gT​(K)​r\hat{f}_{T}=g_{T}(K)r with gT​(λ)=1−(1−ρ​λ)Tg_{T}(\lambda)=1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T} Yao et al. [54, e.g.,], Engl et al. [14, e.g.,]. We reuse this identity, but our TTT setting differs in three critical ways.
(1) The geometry KK is not fixed.
Under our local linearization, the prompt-space geometry is
K⁡(Pn)=Φ​Φ⊤=Jw​Π𝒰​Jw⊤K(P_{n})=\Phi\Phi^{\top}=J_{w}\Pi_{\mathcal{U}}J_{w}^{\top}.
Unlike classical analyses where KK (or the design) is exogenous, here KK varies across prompts and changes with the update mechanism Π𝒰\Pi_{\mathcal{U}} (which blocks/heads/directions are adaptable). Thus, TTT behavior is inherently prompt- and mechanism-dependent.
(2) The target is query prediction, not only in-sample fitting.
Beyond the prompt correction f^T\hat{f}_{T}, prediction relies on the query-point correction
h^T​(x)=kx⊤​qT​(K)​r\hat{h}_{T}(x)=k_{x}^{\top}q_{T}(K)r with kx=Φ​ϕ​(x,Pn)k_{x}=\Phi\,\phi(x;P_{n}).
Hence, success depends not only on filter choice (via qTq_{T}) but also on prompt–query coupling: only eigenmodes aligned with kxk_{x} affect the query (Thm. 5.2).
(3) TT and update directions are on-the-fly objects.
In TTT, a fixed TT can be Bayes-suboptimal under test-time prior shift, and T→∞T\!\to\!\infty can overfit (Sec. 6), motivating per-prompt TT selection (Sec. 7).
Moreover, no choice of TT can overcome subspace mismatch: corrections lie in 𝒮⁡(Pn)=range⁡(K⁡(Pn))\mathcal{S}(P_{n})=\mathrm{range}(K(P_{n})), so update-direction design is essential.
Together, these distinctions explain why step sensitivity and update-direction choices are intrinsic to TTT, rather than secondary hyperparameter details.
Appendix E Proofs (by main-text order)
E.1 Proofs for Sec. 2
This appendix provides proofs for the decision-theoretic statements in Sec. 2. Throughout, expectations are with respect to the joint distribution induced by the mixture model (1).
E.1.1 Proof of Prop. 2.1
Proof.
Let
MBayes(Pn,X):=𝔼[Y∣Pn,X],M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(P_{n},X):=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid P_{n},X], and ξ:=Y−MBayes​(Pn,X)\xi:=Y-M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(P_{n},X).
Then 𝔼[ξ∣Pn,X]=0\mathbb{E}[\xi\mid P_{n},X]=0 and Y=MBayes​(Pn,X)+ξY=M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(P_{n},X)+\xi.
For any predictor M⁡(Pn,X)M(P_{n},X) with 𝔼⁡[M​(Pn,X)2]<∞\mathbb{E}[M(P_{n},X)^{2}]<\infty,
(M⁡(Pn,X)−Y)2\displaystyle\bigl(M(P_{n},X)-Y\bigr)^{2}
=(M⁡(Pn,X)−MBayes​(Pn,X)−ξ)2\displaystyle=\bigl(M(P_{n},X)-M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(P_{n},X)-\xi\bigr)^{2}
=(M⁡(Pn,X)−MBayes​(Pn,X))2+ξ2\displaystyle=\bigl(M(P_{n},X)-M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(P_{n},X)\bigr)^{2}+\xi^{2}
−2​ξ​(M⁡(Pn,X)−MBayes​(Pn,X)).\displaystyle\quad-2\xi\bigl(M(P_{n},X)-M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(P_{n},X)\bigr).
Taking expectation and using the tower property,
𝔼⁡[ξ⁡(M−MBayes)]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\xi\bigl(M-M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}\bigr)\right]
=𝔼[𝔼[ξ(M−MBayes)∣Pn,X]]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathbb{E}\!\left[\xi\bigl(M-M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}\bigr)\mid P_{n},X\right]\right]
=𝔼[(M−MBayes)𝔼[ξ∣Pn,X]]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(M-M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}\bigr)\,\mathbb{E}[\xi\mid P_{n},X]\right]
=0.\displaystyle=0.
Therefore,
ℛ⁡(M)=𝔼⁡[(M−MBayes)2]+𝔼⁡[ξ2].\mathcal{R}(M)=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(M-M_{\mathrm{Bayes}}\bigr)^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\!\left[\xi^{2}\right].
Finally, 𝔼⁡[ξ2]=𝔼⁡[Var⁡(Y∣Pn,X)]\mathbb{E}[\xi^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(Y\mid P_{n},X)] by definition of conditional variance, and it does
not depend on MM. This yields (2).
∎
E.2 Proofs and additional details for Sec. 4
This appendix derives the spectral-filter representation of TTT and proves the equivalence to Gaussian posterior means stated in Sec. 4. Before proceeding, several points should be noted.
Beyond squared loss.
Beyond squared loss, an analogous one-step update can be written by replacing the residual rr with the scaled pseudo-residual r~:=−σ2​∇sℓ​(sw0​(Pn),y)\tilde{r}:=-\sigma^{2}\nabla_{s}\ell(s_{w_{0}}(P_{n}),y) evaluated at θ=0\theta=0. Extending the exact multi-step spectral-filter identities to general losses requires additional local curvature approximations (e.g., Gauss–Newton/Hessian freezing), which we leave as future work.
Well-posedness of the prior.
Since gT​(0)=0g_{T}(0)=0, the operator GT=gT​(K)G_{T}=g_{T}(K) vanishes on null⁡(K)\mathrm{null}(K) and hence f^T∈range⁡(K)\hat{f}_{T}\in\mathrm{range}(K) for all TT. The implicit prior covariance CTC_{T} is supported on range⁡(K)\mathrm{range}(K). It has eigenvalue 00 on null⁡(K)\mathrm{null}(K) and positive eigenvalues only on range⁡(K)\mathrm{range}(K).
E.2.1 Derivation of (6)
Proof.
Recall the GD update in update parameters (Sec. 4):
θt+1=θt+ρ​Φ⊤​(r−Φ​θt),ρ=η/σ2,θ0=0.\theta_{t+1}=\theta_{t}+\rho\,\Phi^{\top}(r-\Phi\theta_{t}),\quad\rho=\eta/\sigma^{2},\quad\theta_{0}=0.
Define f^t:=Φ​θt∈ℝn\hat{f}_{t}:=\Phi\theta_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}. Multiplying both sides by Φ\Phi gives the prompt-space recursion
f^t+1=f^t+ρ​Φ​Φ⊤​(r−Φ​θt)=f^t+ρ​K​(r−f^t).\hat{f}_{t+1}=\hat{f}_{t}+\rho\,\Phi\Phi^{\top}(r-\Phi\theta_{t})=\hat{f}_{t}+\rho K(r-\hat{f}_{t}).
Rearranging yields
r−f^t+1=(In−ρ​K)​(r−f^t).r-\hat{f}_{t+1}=(I_{n}-\rho K)(r-\hat{f}_{t}).
Since f^0=0\hat{f}_{0}=0, we have r−f^0=rr-\hat{f}_{0}=r, and iterating gives r−f^T=(In−ρ​K)T​rr-\hat{f}_{T}=(I_{n}-\rho K)^{T}r, hence f^T=(In−(In−ρ​K)T)​r\hat{f}_{T}=\bigl(I_{n}-(I_{n}-\rho K)^{T}\bigr)r.
Diagonalizing K=U​diag​(λi)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{i})U^{\top} implies
GT\displaystyle G_{T}
=U​diag​(1−(1−ρ​λ1)T,…,1−(1−ρ​λn)T)​U⊤\displaystyle=U\,\mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(1-(1-\rho\lambda_{1})^{T},\dots,1-(1-\rho\lambda_{n})^{T}\bigr)\,U^{\top}
=U​diag​(gT​(λ1),…,gT​(λn))​U⊤,\displaystyle=U\,\mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(g_{T}(\lambda_{1}),\dots,g_{T}(\lambda_{n})\bigr)\,U^{\top},
where gT​(λ)=1−(1−ρ​λ)Tg_{T}(\lambda)=1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T}, which yields the statement.
Finally, if 0<ρ<2/λmax​(K)0<\rho<2/\lambda_{\max}(K), then all eigenvalues of In−ρ​KI_{n}-\rho K lie in (−1,1](-1,1], and if moreover 0<ρ<1/λmax​(K)0<\rho<1/\lambda_{\max}(K), then 0≤1−ρ​λi<10\leq 1-\rho\lambda_{i}<1 for all ii, so gT​(λi)∈[0,1)g_{T}(\lambda_{i})\in[0,1) for all finite TT.
∎
E.2.2 Full proof of Prop. 4.1
Proof.
Recall 0<ρ<1/λmax​(K)0<\rho<1/\lambda_{\max}(K). Let K=U​diag​(λ1,…,λn)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})U^{\top}.
Then
GT=U​diag​(gT​(λ1),…,gT​(λn))​U⊤,G_{T}=U\,\mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(g_{T}(\lambda_{1}),\dots,g_{T}(\lambda_{n})\bigr)\,U^{\top},
0≤gT​(λi)<10\leq g_{T}(\lambda_{i})<1 for all ii.
Define the implicit prior covariance CTC_{T}:
CT:=σ2​GT​(In−GT)−1.C_{T}:=\sigma^{2}\,G_{T}\,(I_{n}-G_{T})^{-1}.
In the eigenbasis of KK (equivalently of GTG_{T}), the matrix (In−GT)−1(I_{n}-G_{T})^{-1} is diagonal with entries
(1−gT​(λi))−1={11−gT​(λi),λi>0,1,λi=0,(1-g_{T}(\lambda_{i}))^{-1}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{1-g_{T}(\lambda_{i})},&\lambda_{i}>0,\\
1,&\lambda_{i}=0,\end{cases}
but since gT​(0)=0g_{T}(0)=0, the product gT​(0)​(1−gT​(0))−1=0g_{T}(0)(1-g_{T}(0))^{-1}=0.
Hence CTC_{T} has the form of CT=U​diag​(cT,1,…,cT,n)​U⊤C_{T}=U\mathrm{diag}(c_{T,1},\dots,c_{T,n})U^{\top}, where
cT,i={σ2​gT​(λi)1−gT​(λi),λi>0,0,λi=0.c_{T,i}=\begin{cases}\sigma^{2}\,\dfrac{g_{T}(\lambda_{i})}{1-g_{T}(\lambda_{i})},&\lambda_{i}>0,\\
0,&\lambda_{i}=0.\end{cases}
Now consider the Gaussian latent-variable model r=f+εr=f+\varepsilon with
f∼𝒩⁡(0,CT)f\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C_{T}) and ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}) independent.
Working in the eigenbasis of CTC_{T}, we have coordinate-wise for r~:=U⊤​r\tilde{r}:=U^{\top}r,
f~:=U⊤​f\tilde{f}:=U^{\top}f, and ε~:=U⊤​ε\tilde{\varepsilon}:=U^{\top}\varepsilon:
r~i=f~i+ε~i,f~i∼𝒩⁡(0,cT,i),ε~i∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2).\tilde{r}_{i}=\tilde{f}_{i}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i},\quad\tilde{f}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,c_{T,i}),\quad\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}).
For each coordinate, the posterior mean is given by the scalar Gaussian conditioning formula [37, 7]:
𝔼⁡[f~i∣r~i]=cT,icT,i+σ2​r~i.\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}_{i}\mid\tilde{r}_{i}]=\frac{c_{T,i}}{c_{T,i}+\sigma^{2}}\,\tilde{r}_{i}.
If λi>0\lambda_{i}>0, then by the definition of cT,ic_{T,i},
cT,icT,i+σ2=σ2​gT​(λi)1−gT​(λi)σ2​gT​(λi)1−gT​(λi)+σ2=gT​(λi).\frac{c_{T,i}}{c_{T,i}+\sigma^{2}}=\frac{\sigma^{2}\frac{g_{T}(\lambda_{i})}{1-g_{T}(\lambda_{i})}}{\sigma^{2}\frac{g_{T}(\lambda_{i})}{1-g_{T}(\lambda_{i})}+\sigma^{2}}=g_{T}(\lambda_{i}).
If λi=0\lambda_{i}=0, then cT,i=0c_{T,i}=0 and also gT​(0)=0g_{T}(0)=0, so the identity still holds. Therefore,
𝔼⁡[f~i∣r~i]=gT​(λi)​r~ifor all ​i,\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}_{i}\mid\tilde{r}_{i}]=g_{T}(\lambda_{i})\tilde{r}_{i}\quad\text{for all }i,
and transforming back gives
𝔼⁡[f∣r]=U​diag​(gT​(λ1),…,gT​(λn))​U⊤​r=f^T,\mathbb{E}[f\mid r]=U\mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(g_{T}(\lambda_{1}),\dots,g_{T}(\lambda_{n})\bigr)U^{\top}r=\hat{f}_{T},
where the last equality uses (6).
∎
E.2.3 Out-of-sample (query) form of TT-step GD under the prompt geometry
We record the kernel-linear form of the TT-step prediction at a query input xx under the linearized model
(Assumption 3.2). This form is used by the predictive Bayes-gap identity in
Appendix E.3.2.
Proposition E.1 (Out-of-sample form of TTT).
Work under Assumptions 3.1–3.2 and the quadratic prompt loss. Let K=Φ​Φ⊤∈ℝn×nK=\Phi\Phi^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n} and let r∈ℝnr\in\mathbb{R}^{n} be the
prompt residual vector. For a query input xx, define the prompt–query kernel vector
kx:=Φ​ϕ​(x,Pn)∈ℝn,k_{x}:=\Phi\,\phi(x;P_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n},
(10)
so (kx)i=⟨ϕ⁡(xi,Pn(−i)),ϕ⁡(x,Pn)⟩(k_{x})_{i}=\langle\phi(x_{i};P_{n}^{(-i)}),\phi(x;P_{n})\rangle.
Let {θt}t≥0\{\theta_{t}\}_{t\geq 0} be the GD iterates (5) with θ0=0\theta_{0}=0, and define the query correction
h^T​(x):=ϕ​(x,Pn)⊤​θT\hat{h}_{T}(x):=\phi(x;P_{n})^{\top}\theta_{T}. Then for every T≥1T\geq 1,
h^T​(x)=kx⊤​qT​(K)​r,qT​(K):=ρ​∑t=0T−1(In−ρ​K)t,\hat{h}_{T}(x)=k_{x}^{\top}q_{T}(K)\,r,\quad q_{T}(K):=\rho\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(I_{n}-\rho K)^{t},
(11)
where ρ=η/σ2\rho=\eta/\sigma^{2}. Equivalently, if K=U​diag​(λ1,…,λn)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})U^{\top}, then h^T​(x)=∑i=1n(ui⊤​kx)​qT​(λi)​(ui⊤​r),\hat{h}_{T}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(u_{i}^{\top}k_{x})\,q_{T}(\lambda_{i})\,(u_{i}^{\top}r), where
qT​(λ)={1−(1−ρ​λ)Tλ,λ>0,ρ​T,λ=0.q_{T}(\lambda)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T}}{\lambda},&\lambda>0,\\[5.16663pt]
\rho T,&\lambda=0.\end{cases}
(12)
Moreover, on the prompt points one recovers f^T=gT​(K)​r\hat{f}_{T}=g_{T}(K)r as f^T=K​qT​(K)​r\hat{f}_{T}=Kq_{T}(K)r.
Proof.
Unrolling the GD recursion (5) gives
θT=ρ​∑t=0T−1(Id−ρ​Φ⊤​Φ)t​Φ⊤​r.\theta_{T}=\rho\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(I_{d}-\rho\Phi^{\top}\Phi)^{t}\,\Phi^{\top}r.
Therefore,
h^T​(x)=ϕ​(x)⊤​θT=ρ​∑t=0T−1ϕ​(x)⊤​(Id−ρ​Φ⊤​Φ)t​Φ⊤​r.\hat{h}_{T}(x)=\phi(x)^{\top}\theta_{T}=\rho\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\phi(x)^{\top}(I_{d}-\rho\Phi^{\top}\Phi)^{t}\,\Phi^{\top}r.
Using the identity (In−ρ​K)​Φ=Φ⁡(Id−ρ​Φ⊤​Φ)(I_{n}-\rho K)\Phi=\Phi(I_{d}-\rho\Phi^{\top}\Phi) and induction, one has
(In−ρ​K)t​Φ=Φ​(Id−ρ​Φ⊤​Φ)t(I_{n}-\rho K)^{t}\Phi=\Phi(I_{d}-\rho\Phi^{\top}\Phi)^{t}. Hence
ϕ​(x)⊤​(Id−ρ​Φ⊤​Φ)t​Φ⊤​r\displaystyle\phi(x)^{\top}(I_{d}-\rho\Phi^{\top}\Phi)^{t}\,\Phi^{\top}r
=(Φ​(Id−ρ​Φ⊤​Φ)t​ϕ​(x))⊤​r\displaystyle=\bigl(\Phi(I_{d}-\rho\Phi^{\top}\Phi)^{t}\phi(x)\bigr)^{\top}r
=((In−ρ​K)t​kx)⊤​r\displaystyle=\bigl((I_{n}-\rho K)^{t}k_{x}\bigr)^{\top}r
=kx⊤​(In−ρ​K)t​r.\displaystyle=k_{x}^{\top}(I_{n}-\rho K)^{t}r.
Substituting back yields (11). Diagonalizing KK gives the scalar form
(12). Finally, for λ>0\lambda>0 we have λ​qT​(λ)=1−(1−ρ​λ)T=gT​(λ)\lambda q_{T}(\lambda)=1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T}=g_{T}(\lambda),
and gT​(0)=0g_{T}(0)=0, so K​qT​(K)=gT​(K)Kq_{T}(K)=g_{T}(K) on range⁡(K)\mathrm{range}(K), implying f^T=K​qT​(K)​r\hat{f}_{T}=Kq_{T}(K)r on the prompt points.
∎
E.3 Proofs and additional details for Sec. 5
This appendix proves the spectral Bayes-gap identity (Thm. E.4 (prompt values)) and records its query-point analogue under a standard GP extension.
E.3.1 Prompt-value (in-sample) identities: Bayes-optimal shrinkage and filter-mismatch gap
This subsection identifies the Bayes-optimal predictor and shows that the Bayes gap of any spectral estimator is a weighted squared filter mismatch.
Fix a prompt PnP_{n} and the associated geometry K⪰0K\succeq 0. Recall the prompt residuals r∈ℝnr\in\mathbb{R}^{n} from Sec. 3. We model the (unknown) latent prompt-level correction as a random vector
f⋆∈ℝnf_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} and adopt the following benchmark prior.
Assumption E.2 (Aligned GP benchmark on prompt values).
Conditioned on KK, the latent prompt-level correction and observed residuals satisfy f⋆∣K∼𝒩(0,τ2K),r=f⋆+ε,ε∼𝒩(0,σ2In),f_{\star}\mid K\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}K),r=f_{\star}+\varepsilon,\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}), and f⋆⟂ε,f_{\star}\perp\varepsilon, for some τ2>0\tau^{2}>0 and effective noise level σ2>0\sigma^{2}>0.
The key structural assumption is alignment: Cov⁡(f⋆∣K)\mathrm{Cov}(f_{\star}\mid K) shares eigenvectors with KK (here Cov⁡(f⋆∣K)=τ2​K\mathrm{Cov}(f_{\star}\mid K)=\tau^{2}K). This is what makes the Bayes-optimal estimator a spectral shrinkage rule on KK and enables the exact filter-mismatch identities below.
Within this benchmark, we model test-time prior shift as changes in the effective SNR τ2/σ2\tau^{2}/\sigma^{2} (equivalently λ⋆\lambda_{\star}), which shifts the Bayes-optimal shrinkage g⋆g_{\star}.
Proposition E.3 (Bayes-optimal estimator on prompt values).
Under Assumption E.2 with K=U​diag​(λ1,…,λn)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})U^{\top}, conditioned on (K,r)(K,r), the Bayes-optimal estimator of f⋆f_{\star}
under squared loss is the posterior mean fBayes=U​diag​(g⋆​(λ1),…,g⋆​(λn))​U⊤​rf_{\mathrm{Bayes}}=U\,\mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(g_{\star}(\lambda_{1}),\dots,g_{\star}(\lambda_{n})\bigr)\,U^{\top}r, where g⋆​(λ):=λ/(λ+λ⋆)g_{\star}(\lambda):=\lambda/(\lambda+\lambda_{\star}).
The Bayes-optimal estimator is a spectral shrinkage rule: each eigenmode with eigenvalue λ\lambda is scaled by g⋆​(λ)∈[0,1]g_{\star}(\lambda)\in[0,1], where λ⋆\lambda_{\star} encodes the test-time noise-to-signal ratio. This provides a precise target for adaptation: a procedure helps when it moves its spectral shrinkage closer to g⋆g_{\star} on the eigenvalues of K⁡(Pn)K(P_{n}).
We next quantify the Bayes gap of any spectral estimator under the GP benchmark.
Let K=U​diag​(λ1,…,λn)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})U^{\top}.
Given a measurable map g:[0,∞)→ℝg:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R} with g⁡(0)=0g(0)=0, define the spectral estimator f^g:=g⁡(K)​r\hat{f}_{g}:=g(K)r, where g⁡(K):=U​diag​(g⁡(λ1),…,g⁡(λn))​U⊤.g(K):=U\,\mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(g(\lambda_{1}),\dots,g(\lambda_{n})\bigr)\,U^{\top}.
Theorem E.4 (Bayes risk and Bayes gap in the spectral domain).
Assume Assumption E.2 and condition on K=U​diag​(λi)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{i})U^{\top}.
Let f^g=g⁡(K)​r\hat{f}_{g}=g(K)r be any spectral estimator. Then
𝔼⁡[‖f^g−f⋆‖22|K]=∑i=1n[τ2​λi​(1−g⁡(λi))2+σ2​g​(λi)2].\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{g}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left[\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}(1-g(\lambda_{i}))^{2}+\sigma^{2}g(\lambda_{i})^{2}\right].
(13)
Moreover, the unique minimizer over g⁡(λi)g(\lambda_{i}) is g⋆​(λi)=λi/(λi+λ⋆)g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})=\lambda_{i}/(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{\star}),
and the excess Bayes risk (equivalently, Bayes gap to fBayesf_{\mathrm{Bayes}}) satisfies the exact identity
𝔼⁡[‖f^g−f⋆‖22|K]−𝔼⁡[‖fBayes−f⋆‖22|K]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{g}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K\right]-\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K\right]
=∑i=1n(τ2​λi+σ2)​(g⁡(λi)−g⋆​(λi))2.\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\,\bigl(g(\lambda_{i})-g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\bigr)^{2}.
(14)
Proof.
Work under Assumption E.2 and condition on
K=U​diag​(λ1,…,λn)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})U^{\top}.
Let f~⋆:=U⊤​f⋆\tilde{f}_{\star}:=U^{\top}f_{\star}, ε~:=U⊤​ε\tilde{\varepsilon}:=U^{\top}\varepsilon, and r~:=U⊤​r\tilde{r}:=U^{\top}r.
Then, coordinate-wise for i=1,…,ni=1,\dots,n,
f~⋆,i∼𝒩⁡(0,τ2​λi),ε~i∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2),r~i=f~⋆,i+ε~i,\tilde{f}_{\star,i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}),\quad\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}),\quad\tilde{r}_{i}=\tilde{f}_{\star,i}+\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i},
and (f~⋆,i,ε~i)(\tilde{f}_{\star,i},\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}) are independent across ii.
For a spectral estimator f^g=g⁡(K)​r\hat{f}_{g}=g(K)r, we have in the same eigenbasis
f^~g,i:=(U⊤​f^g)i=g⁡(λi)​r~i.\widetilde{\hat{f}}_{g,i}:=(U^{\top}\hat{f}_{g})_{i}=g(\lambda_{i})\tilde{r}_{i}.
Hence
f^~g,i−f~⋆,i=(g⁡(λi)−1)​f~⋆,i+g⁡(λi)​ε~i.\widetilde{\hat{f}}_{g,i}-\tilde{f}_{\star,i}=\bigl(g(\lambda_{i})-1\bigr)\tilde{f}_{\star,i}+g(\lambda_{i})\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}.
By independence and zero means,
𝔼⁡[(f^~g,i−f~⋆,i)2|K]=τ2​λi​(1−g⁡(λi))2+σ2​g​(λi)2.\mathbb{E}\!\left[(\widetilde{\hat{f}}_{g,i}-\tilde{f}_{\star,i})^{2}\,\middle|\,K\right]=\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}(1-g(\lambda_{i}))^{2}+\sigma^{2}g(\lambda_{i})^{2}.
Summing over ii yields (13).
To obtain the excess-risk identity, fix λ≥0\lambda\geq 0 and define the scalar risk
rλ​(g):=τ2​λ​(1−g)2+σ2​g2.r_{\lambda}(g):=\tau^{2}\lambda(1-g)^{2}+\sigma^{2}g^{2}.
This is a strictly convex quadratic in gg with derivative
dd​g​rλ​(g)=−2​τ2​λ​(1−g)+2​σ2​g=2​((τ2​λ+σ2)​g−τ2​λ),\frac{d}{dg}r_{\lambda}(g)=-2\tau^{2}\lambda(1-g)+2\sigma^{2}g=2\bigl((\tau^{2}\lambda+\sigma^{2})g-\tau^{2}\lambda\bigr),
so the unique minimizer is
g⋆​(λ)=τ2​λτ2​λ+σ2=λλ+λ⋆,λ⋆=σ2/τ2.g_{\star}(\lambda)=\frac{\tau^{2}\lambda}{\tau^{2}\lambda+\sigma^{2}}=\frac{\lambda}{\lambda+\lambda_{\star}},\quad\lambda_{\star}=\sigma^{2}/\tau^{2}.
Completing the square gives
rλ​(g)−rλ​(g⋆)=(τ2​λ+σ2)​(g−g⋆)2.r_{\lambda}(g)-r_{\lambda}(g_{\star})=(\tau^{2}\lambda+\sigma^{2})\,(g-g_{\star})^{2}.
Applying this identity with λ=λi\lambda=\lambda_{i} and summing over ii yields (14).
∎
Thm. E.4 shows that, conditional on K⁡(Pn)K(P_{n}), each prompt-only algorithm corresponds to a spectral shrinkage rule gg, and its suboptimality is the weighted squared distance to the Bayes-optimal rule g⋆g_{\star} on the spectrum of K⁡(Pn)K(P_{n}). We interpret prior mismatch as this filter-level distance.
We now compare the two procedures of interest within the prompt-level correction model:
•
Naive ICL (no update). No correction is fit on the prompt: f^ICL=0\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ICL}}=0,
equivalently gICL​(λ)≡0g_{\mathrm{ICL}}(\lambda)\equiv 0.
•
TT-step TTT. TTT yields f^T=gT​(K)​r\hat{f}_{T}=g_{T}(K)r with
gT​(λ)=1−(1−ρ​λ)Tg_{T}(\lambda)=1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T} (Eq. (6)).
Corollary E.5 (Conditional Bayes gaps for ICL and TTT).
Under Assumption E.2 and conditioned on K=U​diag​(λi)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{i})U^{\top},
𝔼⁡[‖f^ICL−fBayes‖22|K]=∑i=1n(τ2​λi+σ2)​g⋆​(λi)2,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{ICL}}-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}\|_{2}^{2}\middle|K\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})^{2}, and 𝔼⁡[‖f^T−fBayes‖22|K]=∑i=1n(τ2​λi+σ2)​(gT​(λi)−g⋆​(λi))2.\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{T}-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}\|_{2}^{2}\middle|K\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\bigl(g_{T}(\lambda_{i})-g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\bigr)^{2}.
In particular, within this benchmark, TT-step TTT improves over naive ICL (in Bayes gap on prompt values)
if and only if the weighted squared filter mismatch decreases:
