∑i=1n(τ2​λi+σ2)​(gT​(λi)−g⋆​(λi))2<∑i=1n(τ2​λi+σ2)​g⋆​(λi)2.\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\bigl(g_{T}(\lambda_{i})-g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\bigr)^{2}<\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})^{2}.
(15)
E.3.2 Proof of Thm. 5.2
Proof.
Condition on (K,kx,κx)(K,k_{x},\kappa_{x}) and work under Assumption 5.1.
By construction, (f⋆,fx)(f_{\star},f_{x}) is jointly Gaussian with mean 00, and
Cov​(f⋆fx)=τ2​(Kkxkx⊤κx)\mathrm{Cov}\!\begin{pmatrix}f_{\star}\\
f_{x}\end{pmatrix}=\tau^{2}\begin{pmatrix}K&k_{x}\\
k_{x}^{\top}&\kappa_{x}\end{pmatrix},
r=f⋆+ε,r=f_{\star}+\varepsilon, ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}), and ε⟂(f⋆,fx)\varepsilon\perp(f_{\star},f_{x}).
Hence
Cov⁡(r)\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(r)
=τ2​K+σ2​In=τ2​(K+λ⋆​In),\displaystyle=\tau^{2}K+\sigma^{2}I_{n}=\tau^{2}(K+\lambda_{\star}I_{n}),
Cov⁡(fx,r)\displaystyle\mathrm{Cov}(f_{x},r)
=τ2​kx⊤,λ⋆:=σ2/τ2.\displaystyle=\tau^{2}k_{x}^{\top},\qquad\lambda_{\star}:=\sigma^{2}/\tau^{2}.
Gaussian conditioning yields the Bayes (posterior-mean) estimator
fBayes​(x)\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(x)
=𝔼[fx∣r,K,kx,κx]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[f_{x}\mid r,K,k_{x},\kappa_{x}]
=Cov⁡(fx,r)​Cov​(r)−1​r\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cov}(f_{x},r)\,\mathrm{Cov}(r)^{-1}r
=kx⊤​(K+λ⋆​In)−1​r.\displaystyle=k_{x}^{\top}(K+\lambda_{\star}I_{n})^{-1}r.
Let h^q​(x)=kx⊤​q​(K)​r\hat{h}_{q}(x)=k_{x}^{\top}q(K)\,r be any kernel-linear estimator and define
Δ​q​(K):=q⁡(K)−q⋆​(K)\Delta q(K):=q(K)-q_{\star}(K) with q⋆​(λ):=(λ+λ⋆)−1q_{\star}(\lambda):=(\lambda+\lambda_{\star})^{-1}.
Then h^q​(x)−fBayes​(x)=kx⊤​Δ​q​(K)​r\hat{h}_{q}(x)-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(x)=k_{x}^{\top}\Delta q(K)\,r, and conditioning on (K,kx,κx)(K,k_{x},\kappa_{x}),
𝔼[{h^q(x)−fBayes(x)}2∣K,kx,κx]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\{\hat{h}_{q}(x)-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(x)\}^{2}\mid K,k_{x},\kappa_{x}\right]
=kx⊤​Δ​q​(K)​Cov​(r)​Δ​q​(K)​kx\displaystyle=k_{x}^{\top}\Delta q(K)\,\mathrm{Cov}(r)\,\Delta q(K)\,k_{x}
=kx⊤​Δ​q​(K)​(τ2​K+σ2​In)​Δ​q​(K)​kx.\displaystyle=k_{x}^{\top}\Delta q(K)\,(\tau^{2}K+\sigma^{2}I_{n})\,\Delta q(K)\,k_{x}.
Diagonalize K=U​diag​(λ1,…,λn)​U⊤K=U\,\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\,U^{\top} with columns {ui}i=1n\{u_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}.
Since q⁡(K)q(K) and q⋆​(K)q_{\star}(K) are functions of KK, they share eigenvectors with KK, so expanding in this basis gives
𝔼[{h^q(x)−fBayes(x)}2∣K,kx,κx]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\{\hat{h}_{q}(x)-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}(x)\}^{2}\mid K,k_{x},\kappa_{x}\right]
=∑i=1n(τ2​λi+σ2)​(ui⊤​kx)2​{q⁡(λi)−q⋆​(λi)}2,\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\,(u_{i}^{\top}k_{x})^{2}\,\{q(\lambda_{i})-q_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\}^{2},
which matches Thm. 5.2 with ai​(x):=(τ2​λi+σ2)​(ui⊤​kx)2a_{i}(x):=(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})(u_{i}^{\top}k_{x})^{2}.
∎
E.4 Formal statements and proofs for Sec. 6
E.4.1 Prior-shift separation for fixed spectral baselines
Here, we use the term prior shift to denote test-time distribution shift that changes the task prior (equivalently, the effective SNR parameter λ⋆=σ2/τ2\lambda_{\star}=\sigma^{2}/\tau^{2}) in the GP benchmark, thereby altering the Bayes-optimal shrinkage level.
Proposition E.6 (Prior-shift separation on a projector kernel).
Work under Assumption 5.1 and focus on the prompt latent correction f⋆∈ℝnf_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
observed through r=f⋆+εr=f_{\star}+\varepsilon, where (marginally) f⋆|K∼𝒩⁡(0,τ2​K)f_{\star}\mid K\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}K) and
ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}) is independent.
Assume K∈ℝn×nK\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n} is an orthogonal projector of rank mm (so K2=KK^{2}=K and tr⁡(K)=m\mathrm{tr}(K)=m),
and recall the SNR parameter λ=σ2/τ2>0\lambda=\sigma^{2}/\tau^{2}>0.
Then, the Bayes estimator of f⋆f_{\star} given (K,r)(K,r) is
fBayes(λ)=11+λ​K​r.f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}^{(\lambda)}\;=\;\frac{1}{1+\lambda}\,Kr.
Now fix any spectral estimator f^0=g0​(K)​r\hat{f}_{0}=g_{0}(K)r with g0​(0)=0g_{0}(0)=0, and write a:=g0​(1)a:=g_{0}(1) (so f^0=a​K​r\hat{f}_{0}=aKr).
Assume aa is fixed (i.e., it does not depend on λ\lambda).
Then for any two distinct prior parameters λ1≠λ2\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}, letting
sj:=11+λj,τj2:=σ2λj(j∈{1,2}),s_{j}:=\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{j}},\qquad\tau_{j}^{2}:=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\lambda_{j}}\quad(j\in\{1,2\}),
one has the separation bound
maxj∈{1,2}⁡𝔼λj​[‖f^0−fBayes(λj)‖22|K]\displaystyle\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}\mathbb{E}_{\lambda_{j}}\!\left[\bigl\|\hat{f}_{0}-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}^{(\lambda_{j})}\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K\right]
≥m​minj∈{1,2}⁡(τj2+σ2)​(|s1−s2|2)2,\displaystyle\geq\ m\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}(\tau_{j}^{2}+\sigma^{2})\left(\frac{|s_{1}-s_{2}|}{2}\right)^{2},
where 𝔼λj\mathbb{E}_{\lambda_{j}} denotes expectation under the model with prior parameter λj\lambda_{j}.
Proof.
Under the stated marginal prompt model,
Cov⁡(f⋆∣K)=τ2​K,Cov⁡(r∣K)=τ2​K+σ2​In.\mathrm{Cov}(f_{\star}\mid K)=\tau^{2}K,\qquad\mathrm{Cov}(r\mid K)=\tau^{2}K+\sigma^{2}I_{n}.
By standard Gaussian conditioning for r=f⋆+εr=f_{\star}+\varepsilon,
fBayes(λ)\displaystyle f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}^{(\lambda)}
=𝔼[f⋆∣r,K]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[f_{\star}\mid r,K]
=τ2​K​(τ2​K+σ2​In)−1​r\displaystyle=\tau^{2}K(\tau^{2}K+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}r
=K​(K+λ​In)−1​r,\displaystyle=K(K+\lambda I_{n})^{-1}r,
where λ=σ2/τ2\lambda=\sigma^{2}/\tau^{2}. Since KK is a projector, it has only eigenvalues 00 and 11, hence K​(K+λ​In)−1=11+λ​KK(K+\lambda I_{n})^{-1}=\frac{1}{1+\lambda}K, which yields fBayes(λ)=11+λ​K​rf_{\mathrm{Bayes}}^{(\lambda)}=\frac{1}{1+\lambda}Kr.
Diagonalize K=U​diag​(Im,0)​U⊤K=U\,\mathrm{diag}(I_{m},0)\,U^{\top}. Because g0​(0)=0g_{0}(0)=0, the functional calculus gives
g0​(K)=U​diag​(g0​(1)​Im,0)​U⊤=a​K,a:=g0​(1),g_{0}(K)=U\,\mathrm{diag}(g_{0}(1)I_{m},0)\,U^{\top}=aK,\quad a:=g_{0}(1),
so f^0=g0​(K)​r=a​K​r\hat{f}_{0}=g_{0}(K)r=aKr.
Fix j∈{1,2}j\in\{1,2\}. Under prior λj\lambda_{j} (equivalently τj2=σ2/λj\tau_{j}^{2}=\sigma^{2}/\lambda_{j}), we have fBayes(λj)=sj​K​rf_{\mathrm{Bayes}}^{(\lambda_{j})}=s_{j}Kr, and sj:=11+λjs_{j}:=\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{j}}. Therefore, f^0−fBayes(λj)=(a−sj)​K​r⇒‖f^0−fBayes(λj)‖22=(a−sj)2​‖K​r‖22\hat{f}_{0}-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}^{(\lambda_{j})}=(a-s_{j})Kr\Rightarrow\bigl\|\hat{f}_{0}-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}^{(\lambda_{j})}\bigr\|_{2}^{2}=(a-s_{j})^{2}\|Kr\|_{2}^{2}. Taking conditional expectation given KK (under prior jj),
𝔼λj​[‖f^0−fBayes(λj)‖22|K]=(a−sj)2​𝔼λj​[‖K​r‖22∣K].\mathbb{E}_{\lambda_{j}}\!\left[\bigl\|\hat{f}_{0}-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}^{(\lambda_{j})}\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K\right]=(a-s_{j})^{2}\,\mathbb{E}_{\lambda_{j}}[\|Kr\|_{2}^{2}\mid K].
Since 𝔼λj​[r∣K]=0\mathbb{E}_{\lambda_{j}}[r\mid K]=0 and Covλj​(r∣K)=τj2​K+σ2​In\mathrm{Cov}_{\lambda_{j}}(r\mid K)=\tau_{j}^{2}K+\sigma^{2}I_{n},
𝔼λj​[‖K​r‖22∣K]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\lambda_{j}}[\|Kr\|_{2}^{2}\mid K]
=tr⁡(Covλj​(K​r∣K))\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\!\bigl(\mathrm{Cov}_{\lambda_{j}}(Kr\mid K)\bigr)
=tr⁡(K⁡(τj2​K+σ2​In)​K)\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\!\bigl(K(\tau_{j}^{2}K+\sigma^{2}I_{n})K\bigr)
=(τj2+σ2)​tr​(K)\displaystyle=(\tau_{j}^{2}+\sigma^{2})\mathrm{tr}(K)
=m⁡(τj2+σ2).\displaystyle=m(\tau_{j}^{2}+\sigma^{2}).
Hence, for each j∈{1,2}j\in\{1,2\},
𝔼λj​[‖f^0−fBayes(λj)‖22|K]=m⁡(τj2+σ2)​(a−sj)2.\mathbb{E}_{\lambda_{j}}\!\left[\bigl\|\hat{f}_{0}-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}^{(\lambda_{j})}\bigr\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K\right]=m(\tau_{j}^{2}+\sigma^{2})\,(a-s_{j})^{2}.
(16)
Because s1≠s2s_{1}\neq s_{2}, for any scalar aa at least one of the distances |a−s1||a-s_{1}| or |a−s2||a-s_{2}| is at least
12​|s1−s2|\frac{1}{2}|s_{1}-s_{2}| (e.g., by considering the midpoint (s1+s2)/2(s_{1}+s_{2})/2). Thus
maxj∈{1,2}⁡(a−sj)2≥(|s1−s2|2)2.\max_{j\in\{1,2\}}(a-s_{j})^{2}\ \geq\ \left(\frac{|s_{1}-s_{2}|}{2}\right)^{2}.
Combine this with (16) and lower bound (τj2+σ2)(\tau_{j}^{2}+\sigma^{2}) by
minj∈{1,2}⁡(τj2+σ2)\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}(\tau_{j}^{2}+\sigma^{2}) to obtain the stated separation bound.
The final claim follows because a single fixed aa cannot equal both s1s_{1} and s2s_{2}.
∎
E.4.2 Full least-squares fitting can be dominated
The following proposition is a corollary of the Stein phenomenon [22, 42].
Proposition E.7 (Inadmissibility of projection on a pp-dimensional subspace).
Let r=f⋆+εr=f_{\star}+\varepsilon with ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}) (known σ2\sigma^{2}),
and assume f⋆∈𝒮f_{\star}\in\mathcal{S} where 𝒮⊆ℝn\mathcal{S}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n} is a fixed subspace of
dimension dst:=dim(𝒮)≥3d_{\mathrm{st}}:=\dim(\mathcal{S})\geq 3. Let Π𝒮\Pi_{\mathcal{S}} be the orthogonal projector onto 𝒮\mathcal{S}.
Then the full-fit estimator f^∞:=Π𝒮​r\hat{f}_{\infty}:=\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}r is inadmissible under squared loss ‖f^−f⋆‖22\|\hat{f}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}; namely, it is dominated by the (subspace) James–Stein shrinkage estimator
f^JS={0,‖Π𝒮​r‖2=0,(1−(dst−2)​σ2‖Π𝒮​r‖22)​Π𝒮​r,otherwise.\hat{f}_{\mathrm{JS}}=\begin{cases}0,&\|\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}r\|_{2}=0,\\[2.58334pt]
\left(1-\dfrac{(d_{\mathrm{st}}-2)\sigma^{2}}{\|\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}r\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}r,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}
Equivalently, for all f⋆∈𝒮f_{\star}\in\mathcal{S},
𝔼f⋆​‖f^JS−f⋆‖22≤𝔼f⋆​‖f^∞−f⋆‖22,\mathbb{E}_{f_{\star}}\|\hat{f}_{\mathrm{JS}}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\ \leq\ \mathbb{E}_{f_{\star}}\|\hat{f}_{\infty}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2},
with strict inequality for some f⋆∈𝒮f_{\star}\in\mathcal{S}.
Remark E.8.
Although Prop. E.7 is stated under f⋆∈𝒮f_{\star}\in\mathcal{S}, the conclusion can be read as a statement about improving the estimation of the in-subspace component Π𝒮​f⋆\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}f_{\star} from Π𝒮​r\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}r. For general f⋆∈ℝnf_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, the orthogonal component contributes a fixed additive error by the Pythagorean decomposition
(Prop. E.9).
E.4.3 Adapting update steps cannot overcome subspace mismatch
Let K⪰0K\succeq 0 be symmetric and define the representable subspace 𝒮:=range⁡(K)⊆ℝn\mathcal{S}:=\mathrm{range}(K)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Let Π𝒮\Pi_{\mathcal{S}} and Π𝒮⟂\Pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}} be Euclidean orthogonal projectors.
Proposition E.9 (Irreducible error outside range⁡(K)\mathrm{range}(K)).
For any target vector f⋆∈ℝnf_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} and any estimate f^∈𝒮\hat{f}\in\mathcal{S},
‖f^−f⋆‖22=‖f^−Π𝒮​f⋆‖22+‖Π𝒮⟂​f⋆‖22.\|\hat{f}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\hat{f}-\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\Pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}.
(17)
In particular, ‖f^−f⋆‖22≥‖Π𝒮⟂​f⋆‖22\|\hat{f}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\|\Pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2} and
inff^∈𝒮‖f^−f⋆‖22=‖Π𝒮⟂​f⋆‖22\inf_{\hat{f}\in\mathcal{S}}\|\hat{f}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\Pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}.
Moreover, any spectral estimator f^g=g⁡(K)​r\hat{f}_{g}=g(K)r with g⁡(0)=0g(0)=0 satisfies f^g∈𝒮\hat{f}_{g}\in\mathcal{S}. In particular, TTT for any TT lies in 𝒮\mathcal{S}.
Proof.
Decompose f⋆=Π𝒮​f⋆+Π𝒮⟂​f⋆f_{\star}=\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}f_{\star}+\Pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}f_{\star}.
If f^∈𝒮\hat{f}\in\mathcal{S}, then f^−Π𝒮​f⋆∈𝒮\hat{f}-\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}f_{\star}\in\mathcal{S}, while
Π𝒮⟂​f⋆∈𝒮⟂\Pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}f_{\star}\in\mathcal{S}^{\perp}, so the cross term vanishes:
‖f^−f⋆‖22=‖f^−Π𝒮​f⋆‖22+‖Π𝒮⟂​f⋆‖22.\|\hat{f}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\hat{f}-\Pi_{\mathcal{S}}f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}+\|\Pi_{\mathcal{S}^{\perp}}f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}.
The lower bound and the infimum follow immediately.
For the final claim, diagonalize K=U​diag​(λ1,…,λn)​U⊤K=U\,\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\,U^{\top}.
If g⁡(0)=0g(0)=0, then g⁡(K)=U​diag​(g⁡(λi))​U⊤g(K)=U\,\mathrm{diag}(g(\lambda_{i}))\,U^{\top} annihilates ker⁡(K)\ker(K) and therefore maps
ℝn\mathbb{R}^{n} into range⁡(K)=𝒮\mathrm{range}(K)=\mathcal{S}. Hence f^g=g⁡(K)​r∈𝒮\hat{f}_{g}=g(K)r\in\mathcal{S}.
∎
E.5 PAC-Bayes details for Sec. 7
This appendix proves the normalized PAC-Bayes generalization bound stated in
Sec. 7.
Throughout, we condition on the prompt-induced geometry K⪰0K\succeq 0 and treat all matrices as deterministic given KK.
Remark E.10 (Gibbs posteriors as Radon–Nikodym derivatives; measurability and conditional PAC-Bayes).
We use a Gibbs construction to select (or average over) the update horizon TT.
This can be formulated cleanly in measure-theoretic terms, which clarifies measurability and why
our PAC-Bayes guarantees are naturally stated conditionally on the prompt geometry KK.
RN definition (general form).
Let (ℋ,𝒜)(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{A}) be a hypothesis space (here ℋ=𝒯\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{T}) and let π0\pi_{0} be a prior probability measure on ℋ\mathcal{H}.
Given data DD (here D=(r,K)D=(r,K) or just rr when KK is fixed) and a measurable empirical score/loss
ℓ:ℋ×𝖣→ℝ\ell:\mathcal{H}\times\mathsf{D}\to\mathbb{R},
define the normalizer
Zβ​(D):=∫ℋexp⁡{−β​n​ℓ​(h,D)}​π0​(𝑑h),Z_{\beta}(D):=\int_{\mathcal{H}}\exp\{-\beta n\,\ell(h,D)\}\,\pi_{0}(dh),
assumed finite and strictly positive.
The Gibbs posterior is the probability measure νβ(⋅∣D)\nu_{\beta}(\cdot\mid D) on ℋ\mathcal{H} defined by the Radon–Nikodym derivative
dνβ(⋅∣D)d​π0​(h)=exp⁡{−β​n​ℓ​(h,D)}Zβ​(D).\frac{d\nu_{\beta}(\cdot\mid D)}{d\pi_{0}}(h)=\frac{\exp\{-\beta n\,\ell(h,D)\}}{Z_{\beta}(D)}.
In our setting with finite 𝒯\mathcal{T}, this reduces to the familiar normalized weights
νβ​(T∣r)∝π0​(T)​exp⁡{−β​n​ℓT​(r)}\nu_{\beta}(T\mid r)\propto\pi_{0}(T)\exp\{-\beta n\ell_{T}(r)\}.
Measurability (posterior as a kernel).
If ℓ⁡(h,D)\ell(h,D) is measurable in (h,D)(h,D), then Zβ​(D)Z_{\beta}(D) is measurable in DD and the map
D↦νβ(⋅∣D)D\mapsto\nu_{\beta}(\cdot\mid D) is a measurable probability kernel from the data space to (ℋ,𝒜)(\mathcal{H},\mathcal{A}).
Consequently, posterior expectations (model averaging) and measurable selectors such as an argmin/argmax
(e.g., TMAPT_{\mathrm{MAP}} with a deterministic tie-breaking rule) are measurable functions of the observed data.
Why the PAC-Bayes statement is naturally conditional on KK.
In Sec. 7 and App. E.5, we condition on the prompt-induced geometry KK and analyze the randomness of r|Kr\mid K.
This is not merely a convenience: the Gibbs posterior νβ(⋅∣r,K)\nu_{\beta}(\cdot\mid r,K) is defined as an RN tilt of π0\pi_{0} given the realized data,
so all PAC-Bayes change-of-measure steps (e.g., Donsker–Varadhan / exponential-moment arguments) can be applied
pathwise in KK to the conditional law of r|Kr\mid K.
The resulting bounds therefore hold with high probability over r|Kr\mid K for each fixed KK.
If desired, one can then integrate over the distribution of KK to obtain unconditional statements.
Nullspace contributions.
If KK is singular, then on null⁡(K)\mathrm{null}(K) we have ΣT=σ2​I\Sigma_{T}=\sigma^{2}I for all TT, so both
logdet(ΣT)\log\det(\Sigma_{T}) and r⊤​ΣT−1​rr^{\top}\Sigma_{T}^{-1}r contribute only TT-independent constants from null⁡(K)\mathrm{null}(K).
Equivalently, after ignoring TT-independent constants, all determinants/inverses may be interpreted on range⁡(K)\mathrm{range}(K).
Let 𝒯\mathcal{T} be a finite set of candidates of TT and let π0\pi_{0} be a prior on 𝒯\mathcal{T} with full support. Write Δ⁡(𝒯)\Delta(\mathcal{T}) for the simplex of distributions over 𝒯\mathcal{T}.
Lemma E.11 (PAC-Bayes with exponential-moment).
Let {XT​(r)}T∈𝒯\{X_{T}(r)\}_{T\in\mathcal{T}} be real-valued random variables and fix β>0\beta>0.
Assume there exist deterministic (given KK) numbers ψT​(β)\psi_{T}(\beta) such that
𝔼⁡[exp⁡(β​XT​(r))|K]≤exp⁡{ψT​(β)}∀T∈𝒯.\mathbb{E}\!\left[\exp\!\bigl(\beta X_{T}(r)\bigr)\ \middle|\ K\right]\leq\exp\{\psi_{T}(\beta)\}\qquad\forall T\in\mathcal{T}.
(18)
Then for any δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta over r|Kr\mid K, simultaneously for all
ν∈Δ⁡(𝒯)\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{T}),
𝔼T∼ν[XT(r)]≤1β{KL(ν∥π0)+log1δ}+1β𝔼T∼ν[ψT(β)].\mathbb{E}_{T\sim\nu}[X_{T}(r)]\ \leq\ \frac{1}{\beta}\Bigl\{\mathrm{KL}(\nu\|\pi_{0})+\log\tfrac{1}{\delta}\Bigr\}+\frac{1}{\beta}\,\mathbb{E}_{T\sim\nu}\!\bigl[\psi_{T}(\beta)\bigr].
(19)
This is a standard PAC-Bayes result based on Donsker-Varadhan’s variational formula. See Lem. 2.2 and the proof of Thm. 2.1 in Alquier and others [3].
Lemma E.12 (Exact MGF and a trace bound).
Under Assumption 7.1, for every T∈𝒯T\in\mathcal{T} and β>0\beta>0,
𝔼⁡[exp⁡(β2​(tr⁡(AT)−Z⊤​AT​Z))]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\exp\!\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\bigl(\mathrm{tr}(A_{T})-Z^{\top}A_{T}Z\bigr)\right)\right]
(20)
=exp{β2tr(AT)}det(I+βAT)−1/2,Z∼𝒩(0,In).\displaystyle=\exp\!\left\{\frac{\beta}{2}\mathrm{tr}(A_{T})\right\}\det(I+\beta A_{T})^{-1/2},\quad Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n}).
(21)
Moreover,
log⁡𝔼⁡[exp⁡(β2​(tr⁡(AT)−Z⊤​AT​Z))]≤β24​tr​(AT2).\log\mathbb{E}\!\left[\exp\!\left(\frac{\beta}{2}\bigl(\mathrm{tr}(A_{T})-Z^{\top}A_{T}Z\bigr)\right)\right]\ \leq\ \frac{\beta^{2}}{4}\,\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2}).
(22)
Proof.
Diagonalize AT=U​diag​(ai)​U⊤A_{T}=U\mathrm{diag}(a_{i})U^{\top} with ai≥0a_{i}\geq 0 and set Z~=U⊤​Z∼𝒩⁡(0,I)\widetilde{Z}=U^{\top}Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I).
Then Z⊤​AT​Z=∑iai​Z~i2Z^{\top}A_{T}Z=\sum_{i}a_{i}\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{2} and
𝔼[exp(−β2Z⊤ATZ)]=∏i(1+βai)−1/2=det(I+βAT)−1/2,\mathbb{E}[\exp(-\frac{\beta}{2}Z^{\top}A_{T}Z)]=\prod_{i}(1+\beta a_{i})^{-1/2}=\det(I+\beta A_{T})^{-1/2},
which gives (21).
For (22), use log⁡(1+u)≥u−u2/2\log(1+u)\geq u-u^{2}/2 for u≥0u\geq 0 to obtain
logdet(I+βAT)≥βtr(AT)−β22tr(AT2)\log\det(I+\beta A_{T})\geq\beta\mathrm{tr}(A_{T})-\frac{\beta^{2}}{2}\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2}),
and substitute into log\log of (21).
∎
E.5.1 Proof of the normalized PAC-Bayes bound
Lemma E.13 (PAC-Bayes bound for update steps).
Grant Assumption 7.1 and let 𝒯\mathcal{T} be finite.
Fix a prior π0\pi_{0} on 𝒯\mathcal{T} with full support and β>0\beta>0.
Then for any δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta over r|Kr\mid K, simultaneously for all
ν∈Δ⁡(𝒯)\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{T}),
𝔼T∼ν​[ℒ​(T∣K)]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{T\sim\nu}\bigl[\mathcal{L}(T\mid K)\bigr]
(23)
≤𝔼T∼ν​[ℓT​(r)]+KL(ν∥π0)+log(1/δ)β​n+β4​v¯.\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{T\sim\nu}\bigl[\ell_{T}(r)\bigr]+\frac{\mathrm{KL}(\nu\|\pi_{0})+\log(1/\delta)}{\beta n}+\frac{\beta}{4}\bar{v}.
(24)
Proof.
Write r=Σ⋆1/2​Zr=\Sigma_{\star}^{1/2}Z with Z∼𝒩⁡(0,In)Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n}).
For fixed TT, the log-determinant term logdet(ΣT)\log\det(\Sigma_{T}) is deterministic, hence
ℒ​(T∣K)−ℓT​(r)\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(T\mid K)-\ell_{T}(r)
=12​n​{tr⁡(ΣT−1​Σ⋆)−r⊤​ΣT−1​r}\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}\Bigl\{\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_{T}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star})-r^{\top}\Sigma_{T}^{-1}r\Bigr\}
=12​n​{tr⁡(AT)−Z⊤​AT​Z}.\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}\Bigl\{\mathrm{tr}(A_{T})-Z^{\top}A_{T}Z\Bigr\}.
Define
XT​(r):=n⁡(ℒ⁡(T∣K)−ℓT​(r))=12​(tr⁡(AT)−Z⊤​AT​Z).X_{T}(r):=n\bigl(\mathcal{L}(T\mid K)-\ell_{T}(r)\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathrm{tr}(A_{T})-Z^{\top}A_{T}Z\bigr).
(25)
By Lem. E.12,
log⁡𝔼⁡[exp⁡(β​XT​(r))∣K]≤β24​tr​(AT2)\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}[\exp(\beta X_{T}(r))\mid K]\leq\frac{\beta^{2}}{4}\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2})
⇒𝔼⁡[exp⁡(β​XT​(r))∣K]≤exp⁡{β24​tr​(AT2)}.\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathbb{E}[\exp(\beta X_{T}(r))\mid K]\leq\exp\!\left\{\frac{\beta^{2}}{4}\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2})\right\}.
Apply Lem. E.11 with certificate ψT​(β)=β24​tr​(AT2)\psi_{T}(\beta)=\frac{\beta^{2}}{4}\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2}) to obtain,
with probability ≥1−δ\geq 1-\delta,
𝔼T∼ν​[XT​(r)]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{T\sim\nu}[X_{T}(r)]
≤KL(ν∥π0)+log(1/δ)β+1β​𝔼T∼ν​[β24​tr​(AT2)].\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathrm{KL}(\nu\|\pi_{0})+\log(1/\delta)}{\beta}+\frac{1}{\beta}\mathbb{E}_{T\sim\nu}\!\left[\frac{\beta^{2}}{4}\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2})\right].
Divide by nn and use vT=tr⁡(AT2)/nv_{T}=\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2})/n to conclude (24).
∎
E.5.2 Optimizing the temperature β\beta (rate form)
Corollary E.14 (Rate form via β\beta-optimization).
On the event of Lem. E.13, for any ν∈Δ⁡(𝒯)\nu\in\Delta(\mathcal{T}),
𝔼T∼ν​[ℒ​(T∣K)]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{T\sim\nu}\bigl[\mathcal{L}(T\mid K)\bigr]
(26)
≤𝔼T∼ν​[ℓT​(r)]+v¯(KL(ν∥π0)+log(1/δ))n.\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}_{T\sim\nu}\bigl[\ell_{T}(r)\bigr]+\sqrt{\frac{\bar{v}\bigl(\mathrm{KL}(\nu\|\pi_{0})+\log(1/\delta)\bigr)}{n}}.
(27)
Proof.
Starting from the last bound in (24), set
A:=KL(ν∥π0)+log(1/δ)A:=\mathrm{KL}(\nu\|\pi_{0})+\log(1/\delta) and minimize
f⁡(β)=A/(β​n)+(β/4)​v¯f(\beta)=A/(\beta n)+(\beta/4)\bar{v} over β>0\beta>0.
The minimizer is β⋆=2​A/(n​v¯)\beta^{\star}=2\sqrt{A/(n\bar{v})}, yielding
f⁡(β⋆)=v¯​A/nf(\beta^{\star})=\sqrt{\bar{v}\,A/n}, which proves (27).
∎
If π0\pi_{0} is uniform on 𝒯\mathcal{T} and ν=δT\nu=\delta_{T}, then
KL(δT∥π0)=log|𝒯|\mathrm{KL}(\delta_{T}\|\pi_{0})=\log|\mathcal{T}| and Cor. E.14 gives
ℒ⁡(T∣K)≤ℓT​(r)+v¯​(log⁡|𝒯|+log⁡(1/δ))n.\mathcal{L}(T\mid K)\ \leq\ \ell_{T}(r)+\sqrt{\frac{\bar{v}\bigl(\log|\mathcal{T}|+\log(1/\delta)\bigr)}{n}}.
(28)
E.5.3 From evidence minimization to vanishing normalized Bayes gap
This subsection formalizes how evidence-based TT selection controls (and, in well-specified regimes,
drives to zero) the normalized Bayes gap under the GP benchmark of
Assumption E.2.
We adopt the notation from Sec. 7 under the GP benchmark (Assumption E.2).
Let Σ⋆:=τ2​K+σ2​In\Sigma_{\star}:=\tau^{2}K+\sigma^{2}I_{n} be the marginal residual covariance under the prior.
We define the oracle baseline risk ℒtrue​(K)\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{true}}(K) and the normalized Bayes gap Gapn​(T∣K)\mathrm{Gap}_{n}(T\mid K) as follows:
ℒtrue​(K)\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{true}}(K)
:=12​n{logdet(Σ⋆)+n},\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2n}\Bigl\{\log\det(\Sigma_{\star})+n\Bigr\},
(29)
Gapn​(T∣K)\displaystyle\mathrm{Gap}_{n}(T\mid K)
:=1n​𝔼​[‖f^T−fBayes‖22|K],\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{n}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{T}-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}\|_{2}^{2}\ \middle|\ K\right],
(30)
where f^T\hat{f}_{T} is the TT-step TTT estimator and fBayesf_{\mathrm{Bayes}} is the Bayes posterior mean.
E.5.4 A transfer inequality: KL controls the Bayes gap
Lemma E.15 (A scalar quadratic lower bound).
For every t>0t>0,
t−1−log⁡t≥(t−1)22​max⁡{t,1}.t-1-\log t\ \geq\ \frac{(t-1)^{2}}{2\max\{t,1\}}.
(31)
Proof.
If 0<t≤10<t\leq 1, define h⁡(t):=t−1−log⁡t−(t−1)22h(t):=t-1-\log t-\frac{(t-1)^{2}}{2}. Then h⁡(1)=h′​(1)=0h(1)=h^{\prime}(1)=0 and
h′′​(t)=t−2−1≥0h^{\prime\prime}(t)=t^{-2}-1\geq 0, so h⁡(t)≥0h(t)\geq 0 on (0,1](0,1].
If t≥1t\geq 1, define h⁡(t):=t−1−log⁡t−(t−1)22​th(t):=t-1-\log t-\frac{(t-1)^{2}}{2t}. Then h⁡(1)=0h(1)=0 and
h′​(t)=1−1t−12​(1−1t2)=(t−1)22​t2≥0,h^{\prime}(t)=1-\frac{1}{t}-\frac{1}{2}\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{t^{2}}\Bigr)=\frac{(t-1)^{2}}{2t^{2}}\geq 0,
so h⁡(t)≥0h(t)\geq 0 for t≥1t\geq 1. This yields (31).
∎
Lemma E.16 (Evidence-to-Bayes-gap transfer).
Under Assumption E.2 and conditioned on KK, for every T∈ℕ0T\in\mathbb{N}_{0},
𝔼[∥f^T−fBayes∥22|K]≤ 4σ2KL(𝒩(0,Σ⋆)∥𝒩(0,ΣT)).\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{T}-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}\|_{2}^{2}\ \middle|\ K\right]\ \leq\ 4\sigma^{2}\,\mathrm{KL}\!\left(\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star})\ \|\ \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{T})\right).
(32)
Moreover,
KL(𝒩(0,Σ⋆)∥𝒩(0,ΣT))=n(ℒ(T∣K)−ℒtrue(K)).\mathrm{KL}\!\left(\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star})\ \|\ \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{T})\right)=n\Bigl(\mathcal{L}(T\mid K)-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{true}}(K)\Bigr).
(33)
Proof.
Condition on K=U​diag​(λi)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{i})U^{\top}.
In the KK-eigenbasis, the Bayes shrinkage is g⋆​(λ)=λ/(λ+λ⋆)g_{\star}(\lambda)=\lambda/(\lambda+\lambda_{\star}) and the TT-step GD
shrinkage is gT​(λ)=1−(1−ρ​λ)Tg_{T}(\lambda)=1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T}.
Since r|K∼𝒩⁡(0,Σ⋆)r\mid K\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star}) with Σ⋆=τ2​K+σ2​I\Sigma_{\star}=\tau^{2}K+\sigma^{2}I, the mode-wise Bayes gap is
𝔼⁡[‖f^T−fBayes‖22|K]=∑i=1n(τ2​λi+σ2)​(gT​(λi)−g⋆​(λi))2.\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{T}-f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}\|_{2}^{2}\ \middle|\ K\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\bigl(g_{T}(\lambda_{i})-g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\bigr)^{2}.
Define the dimensionless eigenvalues
m⋆,i:=τ2​λi+σ2σ2=1+λiλ⋆≥1,m_{\star,i}:=\frac{\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2}}{\sigma^{2}}=1+\frac{\lambda_{i}}{\lambda_{\star}}\geq 1, mT,i:=eig​(ΣT)iσ2=(1−ρ​λi)−T≥1,m_{T,i}:=\frac{\mathrm{eig}(\Sigma_{T})_{i}}{\sigma^{2}}=(1-\rho\lambda_{i})^{-T}\geq 1, and ri:=m⋆,imT,i>0.r_{i}:=\frac{m_{\star,i}}{m_{T,i}}>0.
Then g⋆​(λi)=1−1/m⋆,ig_{\star}(\lambda_{i})=1-1/m_{\star,i} and gT​(λi)=1−1/mT,ig_{T}(\lambda_{i})=1-1/m_{T,i}, hence
gT​(λi)−g⋆​(λi)=m⋆,i−1−mT,i−1=(ri−1)/m⋆,ig_{T}(\lambda_{i})-g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})=m_{\star,i}^{-1}-m_{T,i}^{-1}=(r_{i}-1)/m_{\star,i} and therefore
(τ2​λi+σ2)​(gT​(λi)−g⋆​(λi))2=σ2​(ri−1)2m⋆,i.(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\bigl(g_{T}(\lambda_{i})-g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\bigr)^{2}=\sigma^{2}\,\frac{(r_{i}-1)^{2}}{m_{\star,i}}.
Since mT,i≥1m_{T,i}\geq 1, one has ri=m⋆,i/mT,i≤m⋆,ir_{i}=m_{\star,i}/m_{T,i}\leq m_{\star,i}, and also m⋆,i≥1m_{\star,i}\geq 1, so
m⋆,i≥max⁡{ri,1}m_{\star,i}\geq\max\{r_{i},1\}. Hence
(τ2​λi+σ2)​(gT​(λi)−g⋆​(λi))2≤σ2​(ri−1)2max⁡{ri,1}.(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\bigl(g_{T}(\lambda_{i})-g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\bigr)^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}\,\frac{(r_{i}-1)^{2}}{\max\{r_{i},1\}}.
(34)
On the other hand, since Σ⋆\Sigma_{\star} and ΣT\Sigma_{T} commute (both are maps of KK), the Gaussian KL decomposes
mode-wise:
KL(𝒩(0,Σ⋆)∥𝒩(0,ΣT))=12∑i=1n(ri−1−logri).\mathrm{KL}\!\left(\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star})\ \|\ \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{T})\right)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl(r_{i}-1-\log r_{i}\Bigr).
Applying Lem. E.15 to each rir_{i} yields
(ri−1)2max⁡{ri,1}≤2​(ri−1−log⁡ri)\frac{(r_{i}-1)^{2}}{\max\{r_{i},1\}}\leq 2(r_{i}-1-\log r_{i}), and combining with
(34) gives
(τ2​λi+σ2)​(gT​(λi)−g⋆​(λi))2\displaystyle(\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\bigl(g_{T}(\lambda_{i})-g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\bigr)^{2}
≤2​σ2​(ri−1−log⁡ri)\displaystyle\leq 2\sigma^{2}(r_{i}-1-\log r_{i})
=4​σ2​12​(ri−1−log⁡ri).\displaystyle=4\sigma^{2}\frac{1}{2}(r_{i}-1-\log r_{i}).
Summing over ii proves (32).
Finally, for zero-mean Gaussians,
KL(𝒩(0,Σ⋆)∥𝒩(0,ΣT))\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\!\left(\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star})\ \|\ \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{T})\right)
(35)
=12{logdet(ΣT)−logdet(Σ⋆)+tr(ΣT−1Σ⋆)−n}.\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Bigl\{\log\det(\Sigma_{T})-\log\det(\Sigma_{\star})+\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_{T}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star})-n\Bigr\}.
(36)
Using the definitions of ℒ⁡(T∣K)\mathcal{L}(T\mid K) and ℒtrue​(K)\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{true}}(K) yields (33).
∎
E.5.5 A PAC-Bayes high-probability bound for the EB/MAP TT
Let 𝒯⊂ℕ0\mathcal{T}\subset\mathbb{N}_{0} be a finite candidate set and define the EB/MAP TT
T^∈arg⁡minT∈𝒯​ℓT​(r).\widehat{T}\in\arg\min_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\ell_{T}(r).
(37)
Lemma E.17 (High-probability Bayes-gap bound for evidence minimization).
Let 𝒯\mathcal{T} be finite. For any δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta over r|Kr\mid K,
Gapn​(T^∣K)≤4​σ2​(minT∈𝒯⁡ℓT​(r)−ℒtrue​(K)+v¯​(log⁡|𝒯|+log⁡(1/δ))n).\mathrm{Gap}_{n}(\widehat{T}\mid K)\leq 4\sigma^{2}\Bigl(\min_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\ell_{T}(r)-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{true}}(K)+\sqrt{\frac{\bar{v}(\log|\mathcal{T}|+\log(1/\delta))}{n}}\Bigr).
Proof.
On the 1−δ1-\delta event of Thm. 7.2 (point selection, uniform prior),
ℒ⁡(T∣K)≤ℓT​(r)+v¯​(log⁡|𝒯|+log⁡(1/δ))/n\mathcal{L}(T\mid K)\leq\ell_{T}(r)+\sqrt{\bar{v}(\log|\mathcal{T}|+\log(1/\delta))/n} holds for all T∈𝒯T\in\mathcal{T}.
Using (33) yields, for all T∈𝒯T\in\mathcal{T} on the same event, KL(𝒩(0,Σ⋆)∥𝒩(0,ΣT))≤n(ℓT(r)−ℒtrue(K)+v¯​(log⁡|𝒯|+log⁡(1/δ))n).\mathrm{KL}\!\left(\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star})\ \|\ \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{T})\right)\leq n\Bigl(\ell_{T}(r)-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{true}}(K)+\sqrt{\tfrac{\bar{v}(\log|\mathcal{T}|+\log(1/\delta))}{n}}\Bigr).
Apply Thm. E.16 and divide by nn, then use
ℓT^​(r)=minT∈𝒯⁡ℓT​(r)\ell_{\widehat{T}}(r)=\min_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\ell_{T}(r) by definition of T^\widehat{T}.
∎
E.5.6 Asymptotic consequence: vanishing normalized Bayes gap
Lemma E.18 (One-sided concentration of ℓT\ell_{T} around ℒ⁡(T∣K)\mathcal{L}(T\mid K)).
Fix T∈ℕ0T\in\mathbb{N}_{0}. Let
AT=Σ⋆1/2​ΣT−1​Σ⋆1/2A_{T}=\Sigma_{\star}^{1/2}\Sigma_{T}^{-1}\Sigma_{\star}^{1/2} and vT=1n​tr​(AT2)v_{T}=\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2}).
Then for any δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta over r|Kr\mid K,
ℓT​(r)≤ℒ⁡(T∣K)+vT​log⁡(1/δ)n+‖AT‖op​log⁡(1/δ)n.\ell_{T}(r)\leq\mathcal{L}(T\mid K)+\sqrt{\frac{v_{T}\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{\|A_{T}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log(1/\delta)}{n}.
(38)
Proof.
Write r=Σ⋆1/2​Zr=\Sigma_{\star}^{1/2}Z with Z∼𝒩⁡(0,In)Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n}). Then
ℓT​(r)−ℒ⁡(T∣K)=12​n​(Z⊤​AT​Z−tr⁡(AT)).\ell_{T}(r)-\mathcal{L}(T\mid K)=\frac{1}{2n}\Bigl(Z^{\top}A_{T}Z-\mathrm{tr}(A_{T})\Bigr).
Diagonalize AT=U​diag​(ai)​U⊤A_{T}=U\mathrm{diag}(a_{i})U^{\top} with ai≥0a_{i}\geq 0 and Z~=U⊤​Z∼𝒩⁡(0,I)\widetilde{Z}=U^{\top}Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I).
Then Z⊤​AT​Z=∑iai​Z~i2Z^{\top}A_{T}Z=\sum_{i}a_{i}\widetilde{Z}_{i}^{2}.
A standard Chernoff bound for Gaussian quadratic forms [48]
gives: for any t>0t>0,
ℙ⁡(Z⊤​AT​Z−tr⁡(AT)≥ 2​tr⁡(AT2)​t+2​‖AT‖op​t)≤e−t.\mathbb{P}\!\left(Z^{\top}A_{T}Z-\mathrm{tr}(A_{T})\ \geq\ 2\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2})\,t}+2\|A_{T}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,t\right)\leq e^{-t}.
Setting t=log⁡(1/δ)t=\log(1/\delta) and dividing by 2​n2n yields (38)
using tr⁡(AT2)=n​vT\mathrm{tr}(A_{T}^{2})=nv_{T}.
∎
Theorem E.19 (Vanishing normalized Bayes gap).
Let {𝒯n}n≥1\{\mathcal{T}_{n}\}_{n\geq 1} be a sequence of finite candidate sets and let T^n\widehat{T}_{n} be the EB selector
(37). Assume:
(A1)
(Approximation / well-specification.) There exists a (possibly nn-dependent) comparator
Tn⋆∈𝒯nT_{n}^{\star}\in\mathcal{T}_{n} such that
αn:=1nKL(𝒩(0,Σ⋆)∥𝒩(0,ΣTn⋆))→0.\alpha_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\,\mathrm{KL}\!\left(\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{\star})\ \|\ \mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{T_{n}^{\star}})\right)\to 0.
(In particular, αn≡0\alpha_{n}\equiv 0 in the well-specified case Σ⋆=ΣTn⋆\Sigma_{\star}=\Sigma_{T_{n}^{\star}}.)
(A2)
(Complexity.) v¯n:=supT∈𝒯nvT=O⁡(1)\bar{v}_{n}:=\sup_{T\in\mathcal{T}_{n}}v_{T}=O(1) and log⁡|𝒯n|=o⁡(n)\log|\mathcal{T}_{n}|=o(n).
(A3)
(Boundedness.) ‖ATn⋆‖op=O⁡(1)\|A_{T_{n}^{\star}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O(1).
Then, for any fixed δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1), with probability at least 1−2​δ1-2\delta (over r|Kr\mid K),
Gapn​(T^n∣K)\displaystyle\mathrm{Gap}_{n}(\widehat{T}_{n}\mid K)
(39)
≤ 4​σ2​(αn+v¯n​(log⁡|𝒯n|+log⁡(1/δ))nCLOSE\displaystyle\leq\ 4\sigma^{2}\Big(\alpha_{n}+\sqrt{\frac{\bar{v}_{n}(\log|\mathcal{T}_{n}|+\log(1/\delta))}{n}}
(40)
OPEN+vTn⋆​log⁡(1/δ)n+‖ATn⋆‖op​log⁡(1/δ)n),\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{\frac{v_{T_{n}^{\star}}\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\frac{\|A_{T_{n}^{\star}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log(1/\delta)}{n}\Big),
(41)
and in particular Gapn​(T^n∣K)→0\mathrm{Gap}_{n}(\widehat{T}_{n}\mid K)\to 0 in probability (and along the above high-probability event).
Proof.
Apply Lem. E.17 with confidence δ\delta to get, with probability ≥1−δ\geq 1-\delta, Gapn​(T^n∣K)≤4​σ2​(ℓTn⋆​(r)−ℒtrue​(K)+v¯n​(log⁡|𝒯n|+log⁡(1/δ))n)\mathrm{Gap}_{n}(\widehat{T}_{n}\mid K)\leq 4\sigma^{2}\Bigl(\ell_{T_{n}^{\star}}(r)-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{true}}(K)+\sqrt{\tfrac{\bar{v}_{n}(\log|\mathcal{T}_{n}|+\log(1/\delta))}{n}}\Bigr),
since ℓT^n​(r)≤ℓTn⋆​(r)\ell_{\widehat{T}_{n}}(r)\leq\ell_{T_{n}^{\star}}(r).
Next, apply Lem. E.18 to T=Tn⋆T=T_{n}^{\star} with confidence δ\delta to obtain, with probability
≥1−δ\geq 1-\delta,
ℓTn⋆​(r)≤ℒ⁡(Tn⋆∣K)+vTn⋆​log⁡(1/δ)n+‖ATn⋆‖op​log⁡(1/δ)n.\ell_{T_{n}^{\star}}(r)\leq\mathcal{L}(T_{n}^{\star}\mid K)+\sqrt{\tfrac{v_{T_{n}^{\star}}\log(1/\delta)}{n}}+\tfrac{\|A_{T_{n}^{\star}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log(1/\delta)}{n}.
Intersect the two events (probability ≥1−2​δ\geq 1-2\delta) and combine with
ℒ⁡(Tn⋆∣K)−ℒtrue​(K)=αn\mathcal{L}(T_{n}^{\star}\mid K)-\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{true}}(K)=\alpha_{n} by (33).
This yields (41). The stated convergence follows from assumptions (i)–(iii).
∎
Summary: Evidence tuning does more than “avoid overfit”
•
KL controls Bayes gap: evidence closeness ⇒\Rightarrow prediction gap shrinks (App. E.5.3).
•
EB/MAP comes with guarantees: it yields high-probability Bayes-gap bounds, not just heuristics.
E.6 Bayes-optimal update subspaces: proofs and extensions
This appendix provides proofs and extensions for Sec. 8. We work under the
linear-Gaussian correction model (Assumption 8.1).
Note that Assumption 8.1 implies Assumption 5.1.
Lemma E.20 (Assumption 8.1 induces the Gaussian benchmark).
Under Assumption 8.1, condition on (Jw,jx)(J_{w},j_{x}) and define
f⋆:=Jw​u⋆∈ℝnf_{\star}:=J_{w}u_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} and fx:=jx⊤​u⋆∈ℝf_{x}:=j_{x}^{\top}u_{\star}\in\mathbb{R}.
Then (f⋆,fx)(f_{\star},f_{x}) is jointly Gaussian with Cov⁡(f⋆)=Jw​Σ​Jw⊤,Cov⁡(f⋆,fx)=Jw​Σ​jx,\mathrm{Cov}(f_{\star})=J_{w}\Sigma J_{w}^{\top},\mathrm{Cov}(f_{\star},f_{x})=J_{w}\Sigma j_{x}, and Var⁡(fx)=jx⊤​Σ​jx\mathrm{Var}(f_{x})=j_{x}^{\top}\Sigma j_{x}.
In particular, taking Σ=Π𝒰\Sigma=\Pi_{\mathcal{U}} yields the benchmark in Assumption 5.1 with
K=Jw​Π𝒰​Jw⊤K=J_{w}\Pi_{\mathcal{U}}J_{w}^{\top} and kx=Jw​Π𝒰​jxk_{x}=J_{w}\Pi_{\mathcal{U}}j_{x} (and κx=jx⊤​Π𝒰​jx\kappa_{x}=j_{x}^{\top}\Pi_{\mathcal{U}}j_{x}).
Remark on singular QQ (whitened coordinates).
When the query metric Q⪰0Q\succeq 0 is singular (e.g., Q=jx​jx⊤Q=j_{x}j_{x}^{\top} in the Query-Aware specialization), we interpret the whitening map v:=Q1/2​uv:=Q^{1/2}u as mapping into range⁡(Q)\mathrm{range}(Q) and restrict all vv-space objects (including any projector Π~\widetilde{\Pi}) to range⁡(Q)\mathrm{range}(Q) (equivalently, quotient out null⁡(Q)\mathrm{null}(Q)). When mapping back to uu, we understand Q−1/2Q^{-1/2} as the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse on range⁡(Q)\mathrm{range}(Q) (cf. the implementation note in Sec. 8).
Lemma E.21 (Posterior mean and covariance).
Under Assumption 8.1, the posterior is Gaussian
u⋆|r∼𝒩⁡(u¯,Σpost)u_{\star}\mid r\sim\mathcal{N}(\bar{u},\Sigma_{\mathrm{post}}) with u¯=𝔼⁡[u⋆∣r]=Σ​Jw⊤​(Jw​Σ​Jw⊤+σ2​In)−1​r,\bar{u}=\mathbb{E}[u_{\star}\mid r]=\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}(J_{w}\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}r, and Σpost=Σ−Σ​Jw⊤​(Jw​Σ​Jw⊤+σ2​In)−1​Jw​Σ\Sigma_{\mathrm{post}}=\Sigma-\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}(J_{w}\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}J_{w}\Sigma.
Proof.
This is the standard Gaussian conditioning formula for linear observations [see 7, §2.3].
∎
Lemma E.22 (Constrained Bayes estimator equals projection of the posterior mean).
Fix an orthogonal projector Π\Pi of rank dd in ℝp\mathbb{R}^{p}. Among all measurable estimators
u^=u^​(r)\widehat{u}=\widehat{u}(r) satisfying u^​(r)∈range​(Π)\widehat{u}(r)\in\mathrm{range}(\Pi) almost surely, the unique minimizer of
𝔼⁡[‖u^−u⋆‖22∣r]\mathbb{E}[\|\widehat{u}-u_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\mid r] is
u^Π​(r)=Π​u¯​(r)=Π​𝔼​[u⋆∣r].\widehat{u}_{\Pi}(r)=\Pi\,\bar{u}(r)=\Pi\,\mathbb{E}[u_{\star}\mid r].
Remark.
Lemma E.22 is purely Euclidean and applies verbatim to the whitened coordinates v=Q1/2​uv=Q^{1/2}u: replacing (u⋆,u¯,Π)(u_{\star},\bar{u},\Pi) with (v⋆,v¯,Π~)(v_{\star},\bar{v},\widetilde{\Pi}) yields the constrained estimator v^Π~​(r)=Π~​v¯​(r)\widehat{v}_{\widetilde{\Pi}}(r)=\widetilde{\Pi}\,\bar{v}(r).
Proof.
Condition on rr and decompose u⋆=Π​u⋆+(I−Π)​u⋆u_{\star}=\Pi u_{\star}+(I-\Pi)u_{\star} into orthogonal components. For any
u^∈range⁡(Π)\widehat{u}\in\mathrm{range}(\Pi),
‖u^−u⋆‖22=‖u^−Π​u⋆‖22+‖(I−Π)​u⋆‖22\|\widehat{u}-u_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\widehat{u}-\Pi u_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}+\|(I-\Pi)u_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}.
The second term is independent of u^\widehat{u}, so the conditional risk is minimized by
u^=𝔼⁡[Π​u⋆∣r]=Π​u¯\widehat{u}=\mathbb{E}[\Pi u_{\star}\mid r]=\Pi\bar{u}. Uniqueness follows from strict convexity.
∎
E.6.1 Proof of Thm. 8.2
Proof.
Work in the QQ-whitened coordinates v:=Q1/2​uv:=Q^{1/2}u (restricted to range⁡(Q)\mathrm{range}(Q) if QQ is singular). Define v⋆:=Q1/2​u⋆v_{\star}:=Q^{1/2}u_{\star}, v¯​(r):=Q1/2​u¯​(r)\bar{v}(r):=Q^{1/2}\bar{u}(r), and ℐQ:=Var⁡(v¯​(r))=Q1/2​ℐ​Q1/2⪰0\mathcal{I}_{Q}:=\mathrm{Var}(\bar{v}(r))=Q^{1/2}\mathcal{I}Q^{1/2}\succeq 0.
Fix a rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} Euclidean projector Π~\widetilde{\Pi} acting on vv-space and restrict estimators to
v^​(r)∈range​(Π~)\widehat{v}(r)\in\mathrm{range}(\widetilde{\Pi}) almost surely. By Lem. E.22 (applied to (v⋆,v¯)(v_{\star},\bar{v})),
the unique Bayes-optimal constrained estimator is v^Π~​(r)=Π~​v¯​(r)\widehat{v}_{\widetilde{\Pi}}(r)=\widetilde{\Pi}\,\bar{v}(r).
Write v⋆=v¯+zv_{\star}=\bar{v}+z with z:=v⋆−v¯z:=v_{\star}-\bar{v}. Then 𝔼⁡[z∣r]=0\mathbb{E}[z\mid r]=0 and
Var⁡(v⋆)=Var⁡(v¯)+𝔼⁡[Var⁡(v⋆∣r)]=ℐQ+𝔼⁡[Var⁡(v⋆∣r)]\mathrm{Var}(v_{\star})=\mathrm{Var}(\bar{v})+\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(v_{\star}\mid r)]=\mathcal{I}_{Q}+\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(v_{\star}\mid r)].
Moreover, v¯\bar{v} and zz are uncorrelated (and jointly Gaussian), so the cross term vanishes:
ℛ⁡(Π~)\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\widetilde{\Pi})
=𝔼⁡[‖v^Π~​(r)−v⋆‖22]=𝔼⁡[‖Π~​v¯−(v¯+z)‖22]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|\widehat{v}_{\widetilde{\Pi}}(r)-v_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|\widetilde{\Pi}\bar{v}-(\bar{v}+z)\|_{2}^{2}\bigr]
=𝔼⁡[‖(I−Π~)​v¯‖22]+𝔼⁡[‖z‖22]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|(I-\widetilde{\Pi})\bar{v}\|_{2}^{2}\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[\|z\|_{2}^{2}\bigr]
=tr⁡((I−Π~)​ℐQ)+tr⁡(𝔼⁡[Var⁡(v⋆∣r)])\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\!\bigl((I-\widetilde{\Pi})\mathcal{I}_{Q}\bigr)+\mathrm{tr}\!\bigl(\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(v_{\star}\mid r)]\bigr)
=tr⁡(Var⁡(v⋆))−tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ)\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Var}(v_{\star}))-\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\mathcal{I}_{Q})
=tr⁡(Q1/2​Σ​Q1/2)−tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ)\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}(Q^{1/2}\Sigma Q^{1/2})-\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\mathcal{I}_{Q})
=tr⁡(Q​Σ)−tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ).\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}(Q\Sigma)-\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\mathcal{I}_{Q}).
Therefore, minimizing ℛ⁡(Π~)\mathcal{R}(\widetilde{\Pi}) over rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} projectors is equivalent to maximizing
tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ)\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\mathcal{I}_{Q}). Let ℐQ=∑i=1pλi​vi​vi⊤\mathcal{I}_{Q}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\lambda_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\top} with
λ1≥⋯≥λp≥0\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{p}\geq 0. The maximum of tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ)\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\mathcal{I}_{Q}) over rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}}
projectors is achieved by taking Π~\widetilde{\Pi} to project onto span⁡{v1,…,vkbudget}\mathrm{span}\{v_{1},\dots,v_{k_{\text{budget}}}\}.
∎
Let J:=Jw​(Pn)J:=J_{w}(P_{n}) denote the prompt Jacobian (random via random prompts). In this subsection, we take the query metric Q⪰0Q\succeq 0 to be global across prompts (e.g., Q=𝔼⁡[jX​jX⊤]Q=\mathbb{E}[j_{X}j_{X}^{\top}] under the test-time query distribution). 22
2
If one instead lets QQ depend on the prompt, the same derivation holds conditionally, but mapping back to an uu-space update subspace involves Q−1/2(Pn)Q^{-1/2}(P_{n}) and can become prompt-dependent. Define ℐ⁡(J):=Σ​J⊤​(J​Σ​J⊤+σ2​In)−1​J​Σ\mathcal{I}(J):=\Sigma J^{\top}(J\Sigma J^{\top}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}J\Sigma, ℐQ​(J):=Q1/2​ℐ​(J)​Q1/2\mathcal{I}_{Q}(J):=Q^{1/2}\mathcal{I}(J)Q^{1/2}, and ℐQ,glob:=𝔼⁡[ℐQ​(J)]\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}:=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{Q}(J)], where the expectation is over the prompt distribution (equivalently over JJ).
Corollary E.23 (Global optimal subspace).
Any rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} projector Π~⋆\widetilde{\Pi}^{\star} onto the top-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} eigenspace of
ℐQ,glob\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}} maximizes tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ,glob)\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\,\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}) and hence minimizes
𝔼​[ℛJ,Q​(Π~)]\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}_{J,Q}(\widetilde{\Pi})] over rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} projectors, where
ℛJ,Q​(Π~)\mathcal{R}_{J,Q}(\widetilde{\Pi}) is the conditional risk in Thm. 8.2.
Proof.
By Thm. 8.2 applied conditionally on (J,Q)(J,Q),
ℛJ,Q​(Π~)=tr⁡(Q​Σ)−tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ​(J))\mathcal{R}_{J,Q}(\widetilde{\Pi})=\mathrm{tr}(Q\Sigma)-\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\,\mathcal{I}_{Q}(J)).
Taking expectation over random prompts (equivalently over JJ) yields
𝔼⁡[ℛJ,Q​(Π~)]=𝔼⁡[tr⁡(Q​Σ)]−tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ,glob)\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}_{J,Q}(\widetilde{\Pi})]=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{tr}(Q\Sigma)]-\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\,\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}).
Thus minimizing 𝔼​[ℛJ,Q​(Π~)]\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}_{J,Q}(\widetilde{\Pi})] is equivalent to maximizing
tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ,glob)\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\,\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}), achieved by the top-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} eigenspace of
ℐQ,glob\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}.
∎
E.6.2 Finite-sample plug-in subspace selection and a regret bound
Suppose we estimate ℐQ,glob\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}} from mm prompts, yielding ℐQ​(J1),…,ℐQ​(Jm)\mathcal{I}_{Q}(J_{1}),\dots,\mathcal{I}_{Q}(J_{m}), and define
ℐ^Q:=1m​∑j=1mℐQ​(Jj).\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}:=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{I}_{Q}(J_{j}).
Let Π~^\widehat{\widetilde{\Pi}} be the rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} projector onto the top-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} eigenspace of
ℐ^Q\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}, and let Π~⋆\widetilde{\Pi}^{\star} be the analogous projector for ℐQ,glob\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}.
Assumption E.24 (Matrix-martingale concentration conditions for ℐ^Q\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}).
Let ℱj:=σ⁡(J1,…,Jj)\mathcal{F}_{j}:=\sigma(J_{1},\dots,J_{j}) and define the centered increments Xj:=ℐQ​(Jj)−ℐQ,glob∈ℝdQ×dQX_{j}:=\mathcal{I}_{Q}(J_{j})-\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{Q}\times d_{Q}}.
Assume:
(A1)
(Martingale-difference mean.)
𝔼⁡[Xj∣ℱj−1]=0\mathbb{E}[X_{j}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}]=0 a.s. for all jj.
(In particular, this holds when JjJ_{j} are i.i.d.)
(A2)
(Uniform boundedness.)
There exists R>0R>0 such that ‖Xj‖op≤R\|X_{j}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq R a.s. for all jj.
(A3)
(Predictable quadratic variation bound.)
Let
Vm:=∑j=1m𝔼⁡[Xj2∣ℱj−1].V_{m}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{E}[X_{j}^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{j-1}].
Assume ‖Vm‖op≤ν\|V_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\nu a.s. for some ν>0\nu>0.
Theorem E.25 (Subspace selection regret via matrix Bernstein/Freedman).
Under Assump. E.24, for any δ∈(0,1)\delta\in(0,1),
with probability at least 1−δ1-\delta over the mm prompts used to form ℐ^Q\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q},
‖ℐ^Q−ℐQ,glob‖op≤εm,δ,\bigl\|\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}-\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \varepsilon_{m,\delta},
(42)
where εm,δ:=1m​(2​ν​log⁡2​dQδ+2​R3​log⁡2​dQδ)\varepsilon_{m,\delta}:=\frac{1}{m}(\sqrt{2\nu\log\frac{2d_{Q}}{\delta}}+\frac{2R}{3}\log\frac{2d_{Q}}{\delta}).
Consequently, on the same event,
𝔼⁡[ℛJ,Q​(Π~^)]−𝔼⁡[ℛJ,Q​(Π~⋆)]≤ 2​kbudget​εm,δ.\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}_{J,Q}(\widehat{\widetilde{\Pi}})]-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}_{J,Q}(\widetilde{\Pi}^{\star})]\ \leq\ 2k_{\text{budget}}\,\varepsilon_{m,\delta}.
(43)
Proof.
Let Sm:=∑j=1mXj=m⁡(ℐ^Q−ℐQ,glob)S_{m}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}X_{j}=m(\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}-\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}).
Under Assump. E.24 (A1)–(A3), the self-adjoint matrix Freedman inequality [45] states
that for all t≥0t\geq 0,
ℙ⁡(‖Sm‖op≥t)≤ 2​dQ⋅exp⁡(−t22​ν+2​R​t3).\mathbb{P}\!\left(\|S_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq t\right)\ \leq\ 2d_{Q}\cdot\exp\!\left(-\frac{t^{2}}{2\nu+\frac{2Rt}{3}}\right).
Setting
t=2​ν​log⁡2​dQδ+2​R3​log⁡2​dQδt=\sqrt{2\nu\log\frac{2d_{Q}}{\delta}}+\frac{2R}{3}\log\frac{2d_{Q}}{\delta}
yields ℙ⁡(‖Sm‖op≥t)≤δ\mathbb{P}(\|S_{m}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\geq t)\leq\delta.
Dividing by mm gives (42).
On the event ‖ℐ^Q−ℐQ,glob‖op≤εm,δ\|\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}-\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\varepsilon_{m,\delta},
write ℐ^Q=ℐQ,glob+E\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}=\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}+E with ‖E‖op≤εm,δ\|E\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\varepsilon_{m,\delta}.
Since Π~^\widehat{\widetilde{\Pi}} maximizes tr⁡(Π~​ℐ^Q)\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\,\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}) over rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}}
projectors,
tr⁡(Π~^​ℐ^Q)≥tr⁡(Π~⋆​ℐ^Q).\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{\widetilde{\Pi}}\,\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q})\geq\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}^{\star}\,\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}).
Expanding ℐ^Q=ℐQ,glob+E\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}=\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}+E gives
tr⁡(Π~⋆​ℐQ,glob)−tr⁡(Π~^​ℐQ,glob)≤tr⁡(Π~⋆​E)−tr⁡(Π~^​E).\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}^{\star}\,\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}})-\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{\widetilde{\Pi}}\,\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}})\leq\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}^{\star}E)-\mathrm{tr}(\widehat{\widetilde{\Pi}}E).
For any rank-kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} projector Π~\widetilde{\Pi},
|tr⁡(Π~​E)|≤kbudget​‖E‖op|\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}E)|\leq k_{\text{budget}}\|E\|_{\mathrm{op}},
so the RHS is at most 2​kbudget​εm,δ2k_{\text{budget}}\varepsilon_{m,\delta}.
Finally, by the global-risk identity
𝔼⁡[ℛJ,Q​(Π~)]=const−tr⁡(Π~​ℐQ,glob)\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{R}_{J,Q}(\widetilde{\Pi})]=\text{const}-\mathrm{tr}(\widetilde{\Pi}\,\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}})
(cf. Cor. E.23),
this implies (43).
∎
Remark E.26 (i.i.d. prompts).
If J1,…,JmJ_{1},\dots,J_{m} are i.i.d., then (A1) holds. One may take
ν=m​‖𝔼⁡[X12]‖op\nu=m\,\bigl\|\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{2}]\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}
and (42) becomes
‖ℐ^Q−ℐQ,glob‖op≤2​‖𝔼⁡[X12]‖op​log⁡2​dQδm+2​R3​m​log⁡2​dQδ.\bigl\|\widehat{\mathcal{I}}_{Q}-\mathcal{I}_{Q,\mathrm{glob}}\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\ \leq\ \sqrt{\frac{2\bigl\|\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{2}]\bigr\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log\frac{2d_{Q}}{\delta}}{m}}+\frac{2R}{3m}\log\frac{2d_{Q}}{\delta}.
Summary: Subspace design can be learned globally
•
Global mechanism: average ℐQ\mathcal{I}_{Q} over prompts, take top eigenspace (App. E.6).
•
Finite-sample stability: plug-in top-eigenspace has a concentration and regret bound.
Appendix F Validity & Robustness
F.1 Finite-step validity of the local linearization
In the main text, starting in Sec. 3, we suppose the locally linearized (kernel/NTK) regime. This appendix analyzes how accurately the linearized model represents the actual nonlinear gradient updates used in prompt-only TTT. We show that this linear model is a reliable approximation in Theorem F.5. As long as the local conditions are smooth and the step size is moderate, the difference between the true nonlinear updates and the linear version remains small for a few steps (TT).
F.1.1 Nonlinear prompt dynamics and linearized dynamics
Given a prompt PnP_{n}, we define sw​(Pn)∈ℝns_{w}(P_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n} as the vector of predictions produced by the model at weights ww for the nn supervision points.
Let w0w_{0} be pretrained weights and r:=y−sw0​(Pn)∈ℝnr:=y-s_{w_{0}}(P_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n} the base residuals. We restrict test-time updates to a dd-dimensional subspace via w⁡(θ)=w0+A​θw(\theta)=w_{0}+A\theta (Assumption 3.1).
Define the in-prompt prediction shift s⁡(θ):=sw⁡(θ)​(Pn)−sw0​(Pn)∈ℝns(\theta):=s_{w(\theta)}(P_{n})-s_{w_{0}}(P_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n} with s⁡(0)=0s(0)=0. Consider the squared prompt loss
L⁡(θ):=12​σ2​‖r−s⁡(θ)‖22.L(\theta):=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\|r-s(\theta)\|_{2}^{2}.
(44)
Let the Jacobian in update parameters be J⁡(θ):=∇θs​(θ)∈ℝn×dJ(\theta):=\nabla_{\theta}s(\theta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d} and the fixed Jacobian be Φ:=J⁡(0)∈ℝn×d\Phi:=J(0)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}.
Nonlinear TTT (true GD).
Gradient descent with step size η>0\eta>0 on (44) is
θt+1=θt−η∇L(θt)=θt+ρJ(θt)⊤(r−s(θt)),\theta_{t+1}=\theta_{t}-\eta\nabla L(\theta_{t})=\theta_{t}+\rho\,J(\theta_{t})^{\top}\bigl(r-s(\theta_{t})\bigr),
(45)
where θ0=0\theta_{0}=0 and ρ:=η/σ2\rho:=\eta/\sigma^{2}.
Define the induced prompt-space correction and residual ft:=s⁡(θt)∈ℝnf_{t}:=s(\theta_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}, and et:=r−ft=r−s⁡(θt)∈ℝne_{t}:=r-f_{t}=r-s(\theta_{t})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}.
Linearized TTT (fixed Jacobian).
The linearized model replaces s⁡(θ)s(\theta) by Φ​θ\Phi\theta. The corresponding GD iterates satisfy
θt+1lin=θtlin+ρ​Φ⊤​(r−Φ​θtlin),θ0lin=0,\theta_{t+1}^{\mathrm{lin}}=\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}+\rho\,\Phi^{\top}\bigl(r-\Phi\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}\bigr),\quad\theta_{0}^{\mathrm{lin}}=0,
(46)
with prompt-space correction ftlin:=Φ​θtlin∈ℝnf_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}:=\Phi\theta_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} and residual etlin:=r−ftline_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}:=r-f_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}.
Let the (prompt-dependent) restricted kernel at initialization be K0:=Φ​Φ⊤⪰0K_{0}:=\Phi\Phi^{\top}\succeq 0. Then, ftlinf_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}} satisfies the closed recursion
ft+1lin=ftlin+ρ​K0​(r−ftlin)=ftlin+ρ​K0​etlin,f_{t+1}^{\mathrm{lin}}=f_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}+\rho K_{0}\,(r-f_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}})=f_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}+\rho K_{0}\,e_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}},
(47)
and hence fTlin=(In−(In−ρ​K0)T)​rf_{T}^{\mathrm{lin}}=\bigl(I_{n}-(I_{n}-\rho K_{0})^{T}\bigr)r.
F.1.2 Finite-step deviation bound
We impose standard regularity assumptions in a neighborhood of θ=0\theta=0.
Assumption F.1 (Bounded and Lipschitz Jacobian).
There exist R>0R>0 and constants B,L>0B,L>0 such that for all θ,θ′∈ℝd\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d} with
‖θ‖2≤R\|\theta\|_{2}\leq R and ‖θ′‖2≤R\|\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}\leq R,
‖J⁡(θ)‖op\displaystyle\|J(\theta)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
≤B,\displaystyle\leq B,
(48)
‖J⁡(θ)−J⁡(θ′)‖op\displaystyle\|J(\theta)-J(\theta^{\prime})\|_{\mathrm{op}}
≤L​‖θ−θ′‖2.\displaystyle\leq L\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}.
(49)
Assumption F.1 implies a second-order Taylor remainder bound.
Lemma F.2 (Second-order remainder bound).
Under Assumption F.1, for any θ,u∈ℝd\theta,u\in\mathbb{R}^{d} such that ‖θ‖2≤R\|\theta\|_{2}\leq R and ‖θ+u‖2≤R\|\theta+u\|_{2}\leq R, ‖s⁡(θ+u)−s⁡(θ)−J⁡(θ)​u‖2≤L2​‖u‖22\|s(\theta+u)-s(\theta)-J(\theta)u\|_{2}\ \leq\ \frac{L}{2}\,\|u\|_{2}^{2} holds.
Proof.
By the integral form of Taylor’s theorem, s⁡(θ+u)−s⁡(θ)=∫01J⁡(θ+t​u)​u​𝑑ts(\theta+u)-s(\theta)=\int_{0}^{1}J(\theta+tu)\,u\,dt. Hence, s⁡(θ+u)−s⁡(θ)−J⁡(θ)​u=∫01(J⁡(θ+t​u)−J⁡(θ))​u​𝑑ts(\theta+u)-s(\theta)-J(\theta)u=\int_{0}^{1}(J(\theta+tu)-J(\theta))u\,dt, and (49) yields
‖s⁡(θ+u)−s⁡(θ)−J⁡(θ)​u‖2≤∫01L​t​‖u‖22​𝑑t=L2​‖u‖22\|s(\theta+u)-s(\theta)-J(\theta)u\|_{2}\leq\int_{0}^{1}Lt\|u\|_{2}^{2}\,dt=\frac{L}{2}\|u\|_{2}^{2}.
∎
Suppose the step-size regime
0<ρ≤1B2,0<\rho\leq\frac{1}{B^{2}},
(50)
which ensures ‖In−ρ​Kt‖op≤1\|I_{n}-\rho K_{t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1 for all tt as long as ‖J⁡(θt)‖op≤B\|J(\theta_{t})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq B, where Kt:=J⁡(θt)​J​(θt)⊤K_{t}:=J(\theta_{t})J(\theta_{t})^{\top}.
Assumption F.3 (Iterate stability).
For a given T∈ℕT\in\mathbb{N}, the nonlinear GD iterates (45) satisfy
‖θt‖2≤R\|\theta_{t}\|_{2}\leq R for all t=0,1,…,Tt=0,1,\dots,T.
Define the time-varying kernel and the linearized kernel Kt:=J⁡(θt)​J​(θt)⊤⪰0K_{t}:=J(\theta_{t})J(\theta_{t})^{\top}\succeq 0, and K0:=J⁡(0)​J​(0)⊤=Φ​Φ⊤K_{0}:=J(0)J(0)^{\top}=\Phi\Phi^{\top}. We first derive a function-space recursion for the nonlinear dynamics.
Lemma F.4 (Nonlinear prompt-space recursion).
Under Assumption F.1, if ‖θt‖2≤R\|\theta_{t}\|_{2}\leq R and ‖θt+1‖2≤R\|\theta_{t+1}\|_{2}\leq R, then ft+1=ft+ρ​Kt​et+ξtf_{t+1}=f_{t}+\rho K_{t}e_{t}+\xi_{t}, where the remainder term ξt∈ℝn\xi_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{n} satisfies ‖ξt‖2≤L2​‖θt+1−θt‖22\|\xi_{t}\|_{2}\ \leq\ \frac{L}{2}\,\|\theta_{t+1}-\theta_{t}\|_{2}^{2}.
Proof.
Let Δ​θt:=θt+1−θt=ρ​J​(θt)⊤​et\Delta\theta_{t}:=\theta_{t+1}-\theta_{t}=\rho J(\theta_{t})^{\top}e_{t} by (45). Apply Lem. F.2 with θ=θt\theta=\theta_{t} and u=Δ​θtu=\Delta\theta_{t}:
ft+1−ft=s⁡(θt+Δ​θt)−s⁡(θt)=J⁡(θt)​Δ​θt+ξt,f_{t+1}-f_{t}=s(\theta_{t}+\Delta\theta_{t})-s(\theta_{t})=J(\theta_{t})\Delta\theta_{t}+\xi_{t},
where ‖ξt‖2≤L2​‖Δ​θt‖22.\|\xi_{t}\|_{2}\leq\frac{L}{2}\|\Delta\theta_{t}\|_{2}^{2}. Since J⁡(θt)​Δ​θt=ρ​J​(θt)​J​(θt)⊤​et=ρ​Kt​etJ(\theta_{t})\Delta\theta_{t}=\rho J(\theta_{t})J(\theta_{t})^{\top}e_{t}=\rho K_{t}e_{t}, we obtain the statement.
∎
We now bound the deviation fT−fTlinf_{T}-f_{T}^{\mathrm{lin}}.
Theorem F.5 (Finite-step deviation between nonlinear and linearized prompt corrections).
Fix a T∈ℕT\in\mathbb{N}. Assume Assumptions F.1 and F.3,
and the step-size condition (50). Let fT=s⁡(θT)f_{T}=s(\theta_{T}) be the nonlinear prompt correction after
TT steps of (45), and let fTlin=Φ​θTlinf_{T}^{\mathrm{lin}}=\Phi\theta_{T}^{\mathrm{lin}} be the linearized prompt
correction after TT steps of (46). Define the residual scale inside the stability ball ER:=sup‖θ‖2≤R‖r−s⁡(θ)‖2.E_{R}:=\sup_{\|\theta\|_{2}\leq R}\|r-s(\theta)\|_{2}.
Then
‖fT−fTlin‖2\displaystyle\|f_{T}-f_{T}^{\mathrm{lin}}\|_{2}
≤32​L​B2​ρ2​T2​(max⁡{ER,‖r‖2})2\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\,L\,B^{2}\,\rho^{2}\,T^{2}\,\bigl(\max\{E_{R},\|r\|_{2}\}\bigr)^{2}
(51)
≤32​L​B2​ρ2​T2​ER2.\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}\,L\,B^{2}\,\rho^{2}\,T^{2}\,E_{R}^{2}.
(52)
In particular, for fixed local regularity constants (B,L)(B,L) and residual scale ERE_{R}, the discrepancy is
O⁡((ρ​T)2)O\bigl((\rho T)^{2}\bigr).
Proof.
Define the discrepancy δt:=ft−ftlin∈ℝn\delta_{t}:=f_{t}-f_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}. By
Lem. F.4 and (47),
δt+1\displaystyle\delta_{t+1}
=(ft+ρ​Kt​et+ξt)−(ftlin+ρ​K0​etlin)\displaystyle=\bigl(f_{t}+\rho K_{t}e_{t}+\xi_{t}\bigr)-\bigl(f_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}+\rho K_{0}e_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}\bigr)
=δt+ρ​Kt​(r−ft)−ρ​K0​(r−ftlin)+ξt\displaystyle=\delta_{t}+\rho K_{t}(r-f_{t})-\rho K_{0}(r-f_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}})+\xi_{t}
=δt+ρ​Kt​(etlin−δt)−ρ​K0​etlin+ξt\displaystyle=\delta_{t}+\rho K_{t}(e_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}-\delta_{t})-\rho K_{0}e_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}+\xi_{t}
=(In−ρ​Kt)​δt+ρ⁡(Kt−K0)​etlin+ξt.\displaystyle=(I_{n}-\rho K_{t})\delta_{t}+\rho(K_{t}-K_{0})e_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}+\xi_{t}.
Taking norms and using ‖In−ρ​Kt‖op≤1\|I_{n}-\rho K_{t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1 (from (50) and ‖J⁡(θt)‖op≤B\|J(\theta_{t})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq B),
‖δt+1‖2≤‖δt‖2+ρ​‖Kt−K0‖op​‖etlin‖2+‖ξt‖2.\|\delta_{t+1}\|_{2}\leq\|\delta_{t}\|_{2}+\rho\|K_{t}-K_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|e_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}\|_{2}+\|\xi_{t}\|_{2}.
(53)
Under (50), 0⪯In−ρ​K0⪯In0\preceq I_{n}-\rho K_{0}\preceq I_{n}, so
etlin=(In−ρ​K0)t​re_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}=(I_{n}-\rho K_{0})^{t}r implies ‖etlin‖2≤‖r‖2\|e_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}\|_{2}\leq\|r\|_{2} for all tt.
Write Jt:=J⁡(θt)J_{t}:=J(\theta_{t}). Then, Kt−K0=Jt​Jt⊤−J0​J0⊤=(Jt−J0)​Jt⊤+J0​(Jt−J0)⊤,K_{t}-K_{0}=J_{t}J_{t}^{\top}-J_{0}J_{0}^{\top}=(J_{t}-J_{0})J_{t}^{\top}+J_{0}(J_{t}-J_{0})^{\top},
so
‖Kt−K0‖op≤‖Jt−J0‖op​‖Jt‖op+‖J0‖op​‖Jt−J0‖op≤2​B​‖Jt−J0‖op.\|K_{t}-K_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\|J_{t}-J_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|J_{t}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|J_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|J_{t}-J_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2B\,\|J_{t}-J_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}.
By (49), ‖Jt−J0‖op≤L​‖θt‖2\|J_{t}-J_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq L\|\theta_{t}\|_{2}. Moreover, from (45) and
(48), we have
‖θt+1−θt‖2\displaystyle\|\theta_{t+1}-\theta_{t}\|_{2}
=ρ​‖J​(θt)⊤​et‖2\displaystyle=\rho\|J(\theta_{t})^{\top}e_{t}\|_{2}
≤ρ​‖J⁡(θt)‖op​‖et‖2\displaystyle\leq\rho\,\|J(\theta_{t})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\,\|e_{t}\|_{2}
≤ρ​B​ER,\displaystyle\leq\rho\,B\,E_{R},
since ‖et‖2=‖r−s⁡(θt)‖2≤ER\|e_{t}\|_{2}=\|r-s(\theta_{t})\|_{2}\leq E_{R} for ‖θt‖2≤R\|\theta_{t}\|_{2}\leq R.
Thus ‖θt‖2≤t​ρ​B​ER\|\theta_{t}\|_{2}\leq t\,\rho BE_{R}, and hence
‖Kt−K0‖op≤2​B​L​‖θt‖2≤2​L​B2​ρ​t​ER.\|K_{t}-K_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2BL\|\theta_{t}\|_{2}\leq 2LB^{2}\,\rho\,t\,E_{R}.
(54)
From Lem. F.4 and the bound on ‖θt+1−θt‖2\|\theta_{t+1}-\theta_{t}\|_{2},
‖ξt‖2≤L2​(ρ​B​ER)2=L2​B2​ρ2​ER2.\|\xi_{t}\|_{2}\leq\frac{L}{2}\,(\rho BE_{R})^{2}=\frac{L}{2}\,B^{2}\rho^{2}E_{R}^{2}.
(55)
Plug (54), ‖etlin‖2≤‖r‖2\|e_{t}^{\mathrm{lin}}\|_{2}\leq\|r\|_{2}, and (55) into (53):
‖δt+1‖2≤‖δt‖2+ρ⁡(2​L​B2​ρ​t​ER)​‖r‖2+L2​B2​ρ2​ER2.\|\delta_{t+1}\|_{2}\leq\|\delta_{t}\|_{2}+\rho(2LB^{2}\rho tE_{R})\|r\|_{2}+\frac{L}{2}B^{2}\rho^{2}E_{R}^{2}.
Let E:=max⁡{ER,‖r‖2}E:=\max\{E_{R},\|r\|_{2}\}. Then ‖r‖2≤E\|r\|_{2}\leq E and ER≤EE_{R}\leq E, so
‖δt+1‖2≤‖δt‖2+2​L​B2​ρ2​t​E2+L2​B2​ρ2​E2.\|\delta_{t+1}\|_{2}\leq\|\delta_{t}\|_{2}+2LB^{2}\rho^{2}\,t\,E^{2}+\frac{L}{2}B^{2}\rho^{2}E^{2}.
Since δ0=0\delta_{0}=0, summing from t=0t=0 to T−1T-1 gives
‖δT‖2\displaystyle\|\delta_{T}\|_{2}
≤2​L​B2​ρ2​E2​∑t=0T−1t+L2​B2​ρ2​E2​∑t=0T−11\displaystyle\leq 2LB^{2}\rho^{2}E^{2}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}t+\frac{L}{2}B^{2}\rho^{2}E^{2}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}1
=L​B2​ρ2​E2​T​(T−1)+L2​B2​ρ2​E2​T\displaystyle=LB^{2}\rho^{2}E^{2}T(T-1)+\frac{L}{2}B^{2}\rho^{2}E^{2}T
≤32​L​B2​ρ2​E2​T2,\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}LB^{2}\rho^{2}E^{2}T^{2},
which is (52).
∎
Corollary F.6 (Kernel drift along nonlinear TTT).
Under the assumptions of Thm. F.5, for every t≤Tt\leq T,
‖Kt−K0‖op≤2​L​B2​ρ​t​ER.\|K_{t}-K_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 2LB^{2}\,\rho\,t\,E_{R}.
(56)
Deviation bound for query predictions
Thm. F.5 controls discrepancies on the prompt supervision values. A similar bound can be derived for query-point predictions under additional smoothness assumptions. Concretely, suppose the query prediction shift
Δ⁡(x,θ):=fw⁡(θ)​(x,Pn)−fw0​(x,Pn)\Delta(x;\theta):=f_{w(\theta)}(x;P_{n})-f_{w_{0}}(x;P_{n})
is twice differentiable in θ\theta on the same ball {∥θ∥2≤R}\{\|\theta\|_{2}\leq R\}, with Jacobian ∇θΔ​(x,θ)\nabla_{\theta}\Delta(x;\theta) bounded and Lipschitz uniformly in θ\theta. Then, one can repeat the argument above to show that the gap between the nonlinear query correction Δ⁡(x,θT)\Delta(x;\theta_{T}) and its linearized form ϕ​(x,Pn)⊤​θTlin\phi(x;P_{n})^{\top}\theta_{T}^{\mathrm{lin}} is O⁡((ρ​T)2)O((\rho T)^{2}). We omit the algebraic details to avoid repetition: the proof follows the same contraction, kernel-drift, Taylor-remainder decomposition as in Thm. F.5, merely replacing the prompt map with the query map.
F.2 Generalizing Filter Mismatch to Non-Gaussian Priors
In Sec. 5, we analyzed the Bayes gap assuming Gaussianity.
This appendix discusses the robustness of the mode-wise MSE decomposition and the squared mismatch identity. The statements require only independence and finite second moments.
Proposition F.7 (Mode-wise MSE and squared mismatch without Gaussianity).
Fix K⪰0K\succeq 0 and suppose that r=f⋆+ε,𝔼⁡[f⋆]=0,Cov⁡(f⋆)=C⋆r=f_{\star}+\varepsilon,\mathbb{E}[f_{\star}]=0,\ \mathrm{Cov}(f_{\star})=C_{\star}\ 𝔼⁡[ε]=0,Cov⁡(ε)=σ2​In,f⋆⟂ε.\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon]=0,\ \mathrm{Cov}(\varepsilon)=\sigma^{2}I_{n},f_{\star}\perp\varepsilon. Let SS be any symmetric matrix such that S​K=K​SSK=KS. Then, there exists an orthogonal matrix UU such that KK and SS are simultaneously diagonalized as K=U​diag​(λ1,…,λn)​U⊤,S=U​diag​(s1,…,sn)​U⊤K=U\,\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})\,U^{\top},S=U\,\mathrm{diag}(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})\,U^{\top}. Define ci:=ui⊤​C⋆​ui=Var⁡(ui⊤​f⋆)≥0c_{i}:=u_{i}^{\top}C_{\star}u_{i}=\mathrm{Var}(u_{i}^{\top}f_{\star})\geq 0, where uiu_{i} is the iith column of UU. Then, the MSE of f^S:=S​r\hat{f}_{S}:=Sr satisfies
𝔼[∥f^S−f⋆∥22|K,S]=∑i=1n{ci(1−si)2+σ2si2}.\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{S}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K,S\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl\{c_{i}(1-s_{i})^{2}+\sigma^{2}s_{i}^{2}\Bigr\}.
(57)
Moreover, for each coordinate ii, the unique minimizer over si∈ℝs_{i}\in\mathbb{R} is si⋆=cici+σ2s_{i}^{\star}=\frac{c_{i}}{c_{i}+\sigma^{2}} and the excess risk admits the exact squared-mismatch identity
𝔼[∥f^S−f⋆∥22|K,S]−𝔼[∥f^S⋆−f⋆∥22|K,S]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{S}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K,S\right]-\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{S^{\star}}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K,S\right]
(58)
=∑i=1n(ci+σ2)​(si−si⋆)2,\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}+\sigma^{2})\,(s_{i}-s_{i}^{\star})^{2},
(59)
where S⋆:=U​diag​(s1⋆,…,sn⋆)​U⊤S^{\star}:=U\,\mathrm{diag}(s_{1}^{\star},\dots,s_{n}^{\star})\,U^{\top}.
This proposition suggests that, relying only on second-moment conditions (no Gaussianity), the estimation error decomposes into independent mode-wise shrinkage problems in the eigenbasis of KK. The optimal shrinkage is given by si⋆=cici+σ2s_{i}^{\star}=\frac{c_{i}}{c_{i}+\sigma^{2}}, and the excess risk admits an exact identity as the weighted sum of squared mismatches (si−si⋆)2(s_{i}-s_{i}^{\star})^{2}.
Proof.
Let r~:=U⊤​r\tilde{r}:=U^{\top}r, f~:=U⊤​f⋆\tilde{f}:=U^{\top}f_{\star}, and ε~:=U⊤​ε\tilde{\varepsilon}:=U^{\top}\varepsilon. Since UU is orthogonal, ‖f^S−f⋆‖22=‖f^~−f~‖22\|\hat{f}_{S}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}=\|\widetilde{\hat{f}}-\tilde{f}\|_{2}^{2}, where f^~=diag⁡(si)​r~\widetilde{\hat{f}}=\mathrm{diag}(s_{i})\tilde{r} and r~=f~+ε~\tilde{r}=\tilde{f}+\tilde{\varepsilon}.
Thus,
‖f^~−f~‖22=∑i=1n(si​r~i−f~i)2=∑i=1n((si−1)​f~i+si​ε~i)2.\|\widetilde{\hat{f}}-\tilde{f}\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(s_{i}\tilde{r}_{i}-\tilde{f}_{i})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\bigl((s_{i}-1)\tilde{f}_{i}+s_{i}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}\bigr)^{2}.
Taking expectations and using f⋆⟂εf_{\star}\perp\varepsilon (hence 𝔼⁡[f~i​ε~i]=0\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}_{i}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}]=0),
𝔼⁡[f~i2]=ci\mathbb{E}[\tilde{f}_{i}^{2}]=c_{i}, and 𝔼⁡[ε~i2]=σ2\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}]=\sigma^{2} yields (57).
For fixed ii, the scalar risk is a strictly convex quadratic
ci​(1−si)2+σ2​si2c_{i}(1-s_{i})^{2}+\sigma^{2}s_{i}^{2}, whose unique minimizer is si⋆=cici+σ2s_{i}^{\star}=\frac{c_{i}}{c_{i}+\sigma^{2}}. Completing the square gives ci​(1−s)2+σ2​s2=ci​(1−s⋆)2+σ2​(s⋆)2+(ci+σ2)​(s−s⋆)2c_{i}(1-s)^{2}+\sigma^{2}s^{2}=c_{i}(1-s^{\star})^{2}+\sigma^{2}(s^{\star})^{2}+(c_{i}+\sigma^{2})(s-s^{\star})^{2}, which implies (59) after summing over ii.
∎
Remark F.8 (Connection to the GP benchmark in Sec. 5).
If C⋆=τ2​KC_{\star}=\tau^{2}K for some τ2>0\tau^{2}>0 (covariance alignment), then ci=τ2​λic_{i}=\tau^{2}\lambda_{i} and si⋆=λi/(λi+λ⋆)s_{i}^{\star}=\lambda_{i}/(\lambda_{i}+\lambda_{\star}) with λ⋆=σ2/τ2\lambda_{\star}=\sigma^{2}/\tau^{2}, recovering the shrinkage g⋆​(λ)g_{\star}(\lambda) used in the main text. If, in addition, (f⋆,ε)(f_{\star},\varepsilon) are jointly Gaussian, then f^S⋆\hat{f}_{S^{\star}} coincides with the Bayes posterior mean for that GP model.
F.3 Extension to anisotropic priors
This subsection extends the GP benchmark by relaxing the aligned covariance assumption (covariance proportional to KK) in Assumption 5.1, allowing anisotropic task variability in the update parameters. Specifically, we show that appropriate preconditioning yields Bayes-consistent filtering under the induced anisotropic geometry.
Assumption F.9 (Anisotropic Gaussian prior in update parameters).
Let Φ∈ℝn×d\Phi\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d} be the prompt feature matrix and consider the linearized residual model r=Φ​θ⋆+ε,ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)r=\Phi\theta_{\star}+\varepsilon,\,\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}). Assume an anisotropic prior on the latent update parameter signal θ⋆∼𝒩⁡(0,τ2​Σ)\theta_{\star}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}\Sigma), with Σ∈ℝd×d\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d} and λ⋆:=σ2/τ2\lambda_{\star}:=\sigma^{2}/\tau^{2}.
Proposition F.10 (Bayes-optimal predictor on prompt values under anisotropic priors).
Under Assumption F.9, the latent prompt-level signal f⋆:=Φ​θ⋆f_{\star}:=\Phi\theta_{\star} satisfies f⋆∼𝒩⁡(0,τ2​K~),K~:=Φ​Σ​Φ⊤,f_{\star}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}\widetilde{K}),\,\widetilde{K}:=\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{\top}, and the Bayes-optimal estimator of f⋆f_{\star} given rr under squared loss is
fBayes=𝔼⁡[f⋆∣r]=K~​(K~+λ⋆​In)−1​r.f_{\mathrm{Bayes}}=\mathbb{E}[f_{\star}\mid r]=\widetilde{K}(\widetilde{K}+\lambda_{\star}I_{n})^{-1}r.
(60)
Proof.
Since θ⋆∼𝒩⁡(0,τ2​Σ)\theta_{\star}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}\Sigma) and f⋆=Φ​θ⋆f_{\star}=\Phi\theta_{\star} is linear in θ⋆\theta_{\star},
we have f⋆∼𝒩⁡(0,τ2​Φ​Σ​Φ⊤)=𝒩⁡(0,τ2​K~)f_{\star}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{\top})=\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}\widetilde{K}).
With r=f⋆+εr=f_{\star}+\varepsilon and ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}), Gaussian conditioning yields the
posterior mean (60).
∎
Theorem F.11 (Preconditioned GD induces spectral filtering under K~\widetilde{K}).
Under Assumption F.9, consider preconditioned GD in θ\theta-space
θt+1=θt+ρ​Σ​Φ⊤​(r−Φ​θt),\theta_{t+1}=\theta_{t}+\rho\,\Sigma\,\Phi^{\top}(r-\Phi\theta_{t}),
(61)
where θ0=0\theta_{0}=0 and ρ:=η/σ2\rho:=\eta/\sigma^{2}. Let f^t:=Φ​θt\hat{f}_{t}:=\Phi\theta_{t}. Then, f^t+1=f^t+ρ​K~​(r−f^t)\hat{f}_{t+1}=\hat{f}_{t}+\rho\,\widetilde{K}\,(r-\hat{f}_{t}) holds, where K~:=Φ​Σ​Φ⊤\widetilde{K}:=\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{\top} and hence, for any T≥0T\geq 0, f^T=G~T​r\hat{f}_{T}=\widetilde{G}_{T}r holds, where G~T:=In−(In−ρ​K~)T\widetilde{G}_{T}:=I_{n}-(I_{n}-\rho\widetilde{K})^{T}.
Consequently, all spectral-filter and Bayes-gap results for TTT apply verbatim after replacing KK by K~\widetilde{K}: TTT selects an implicit prior under the geometry induced by K~\widetilde{K}, and the Bayes-optimal shrinkage on prompt values is K~​(K~+λ⋆​In)−1\widetilde{K}(\widetilde{K}+\lambda_{\star}I_{n})^{-1}.
Proof.
Multiply (61) by Φ\Phi to get
f^t+1=Φ​θt+1\displaystyle\hat{f}_{t+1}=\Phi\theta_{t+1}
=Φ​θt+ρ​Φ​Σ​Φ⊤​(r−Φ​θt)\displaystyle=\Phi\theta_{t}+\rho\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{\top}(r-\Phi\theta_{t})
=f^t+ρ​K~​(r−f^t).\displaystyle=\hat{f}_{t}+\rho\widetilde{K}(r-\hat{f}_{t}).
Solving the linear recursion concludes the proof.
∎
Remark F.12 (Interpretation: whitening and Bayes-consistency).
Under the anisotropic prior, the Bayes-optimal predictor depends on K~=Φ​Σ​Φ⊤\widetilde{K}=\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{\top} rather than K=Φ​Φ⊤K=\Phi\Phi^{\top}. Preconditioning by Σ\Sigma reshapes the effective geometry so that the induced GD filter family can be viewed as Bayes-consistent shrinkage after whitening the update parameter prior.
Summary: robustness of the spectral theory
•
Even non-Gaussian: excess risk is still a squared spectral shrinkage mismatch under 2nd moments (App. F.2).
•
Anisotropic tasks: preconditioning replaces KK by K~=Φ​Σ​Φ⊤\widetilde{K}=\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{\top} (App. F.3).
Appendix G Gradient flow and random-matrix theory view of the update horizon TT
This appendix explains how the update horizon is governed by the spectrum of the prompt geometry K⁡(Pn)K(P_{n}). We recast discrete TT-step updates via the continuous diffusion time t≈ρ​Tt\approx\rho T, under which the estimator becomes an explicit spectral filter gt​(λ)=1−e−t​λg_{t}(\lambda)=1-e^{-t\lambda}, making “how far to adapt” a one-parameter shrinkage rule over eigenmodes. We then use this view to define an oracle (risk-optimal) horizon and to motivate deterministic proxies from random-matrix concentration, clarifying why the best TT is inherently prompt- and subspace-dependent.
G.1 Gradient flow as the canonical horizon parameter
Fix a prompt of length nn. Let K∈ℝn×nK\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n} be the prompt geometry, and r∈ℝnr\in\mathbb{R}^{n} be the prompt residual vector. Write an eigendecomposition K=U​diag​(λ1,…,λn)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})U^{\top} with λi≥0\lambda_{i}\geq 0.
Proposition G.1 (Gradient flow solution and spectral filter).
Consider the gradient-flow ODE f˙​(t)=K​(r−f​(t))\dot{f}(t)=K(r-f(t)) with f⁡(0)=0f(0)=0.
Then
f⁡(t)=(I−e−t​K)​r=U​diag​(gt​(λ1),…,gt​(λn))​U⊤​r,f(t)=(I-e^{-tK})r=U\,\mathrm{diag}\!\bigl(g_{t}(\lambda_{1}),\dots,g_{t}(\lambda_{n})\bigr)\,U^{\top}r,
(62)
where gt​(λ):=1−e−t​λ.g_{t}(\lambda):=1-e^{-t\lambda}.
Proof.
Let e⁡(t):=r−f⁡(t)e(t):=r-f(t). Then e˙​(t)=−K​e​(t)\dot{e}(t)=-Ke(t) and e⁡(0)=re(0)=r, so e⁡(t)=e−t​K​re(t)=e^{-tK}r, hence f⁡(t)=r−e⁡(t)f(t)=r-e(t).
Diagonalization yields the scalar filter.
∎
Each eigenmode uiu_{i} is learned at rate λi\lambda_{i}, so large-λ\lambda modes fit quickly and small-λ\lambda modes fit slowly.
Note that our discrete GD recursion is ft+1=ft+ρ​K​(r−ft)f_{t+1}=f_{t}+\rho K(r-f_{t}).
The gradient flow is written in the rescaled time that absorbs ρ\rho, and thus the canonical horizon parameter is the diffusion time t≃ρ​Tt\simeq\rho T.
Recall the discrete TT-step GD filter (main text)
gT​(λ)=1−(1−ρ​λ)T,0<ρ<1/λmax​(K).g_{T}(\lambda)=1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T},\qquad 0<\rho<1/\lambda_{\max}(K).
Lemma G.2 (Discrete GD ≈\approx gradient flow).
Fix λ≥0\lambda\geq 0 and assume ρ​λ<1\rho\lambda<1. Let t:=ρ​Tt:=\rho T. Then
0≤gT​(λ)−gt​(λ)=e−t​λ−(1−ρ​λ)T≤T​(ρ​λ)22​(1−ρ​λ)​e−t​λ.0\leq g_{T}(\lambda)-g_{t}(\lambda)=e^{-t\lambda}-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T}\leq\frac{T(\rho\lambda)^{2}}{2(1-\rho\lambda)}\,e^{-t\lambda}.
(63)
Proof.
Let x=ρ​λ∈[0,1)x=\rho\lambda\in[0,1). Since 1−x≤e−x1-x\leq e^{-x}, (1−x)T≤e−x​T(1-x)^{T}\leq e^{-xT}, giving gT≥gtg_{T}\geq g_{t}. Also log⁡(1−x)=−x−∑k≥2xk/k≥−x−x2/(2​(1−x))\log(1-x)=-x-\sum_{k\geq 2}x^{k}/k\geq-x-x^{2}/(2(1-x)), hence (1−x)T≥e−x​Texp{−Tx2/(2(1−x))}(1-x)^{T}\geq e^{-xT}\exp\{-Tx^{2}/(2(1-x))\}. Rearranging and using 1−e−y≤y1-e^{-y}\leq y concludes the proof.
∎
G.2 Bayes gap for a fixed prompt: oracle diffusion time and mixture
We evaluate horizons under the aligned GP benchmark (used throughout the main text for filter-match analysis).
Assumption G.3 (Aligned GP benchmark).
Conditioned on KK, f⋆∼𝒩⁡(0,τ2​K)f_{\star}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tau^{2}K) and r=f⋆+εr=f_{\star}+\varepsilon with ε∼𝒩⁡(0,σ2​In)\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}I_{n}) independent.
Define λ⋆:=σ2/τ2\lambda_{\star}:=\sigma^{2}/\tau^{2} and g⋆​(λ):=λ/(λ+λ⋆)g_{\star}(\lambda):=\lambda/(\lambda+\lambda_{\star}).
Proposition G.4 (Mode-wise risk and mismatch identity (recall)).
Under Assump. G.3 and conditioned on K=U​diag​(λi)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{i})U^{\top}, for any measurable gg with g⁡(0)=0g(0)=0,
the spectral estimator f^g=g⁡(K)​r\hat{f}_{g}=g(K)r satisfies
𝔼⁡[‖f^g−f⋆‖22∣K]=∑i=1n[τ2​λi​(1−g⁡(λi))2+σ2​g​(λi)2].\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{g}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\mid K\right]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl[\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}(1-g(\lambda_{i}))^{2}+\sigma^{2}g(\lambda_{i})^{2}\Bigr].
(64)
Moreover, g⋆​(λ)=λ/(λ+λ⋆)g_{\star}(\lambda)=\lambda/(\lambda+\lambda_{\star}) uniquely minimizes the bracketed term, and the excess Bayes risk is
𝔼⁡[‖f^g−f⋆‖22∣K]−𝔼⁡[‖g⋆​(K)​r−f⋆‖22∣K]\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}_{g}-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\mid K\right]-\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|g_{\star}(K)r-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\mid K\right]
(65)
=∑i=1nai​(g⁡(λi)−g⋆​(λi))2,\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\bigl(g(\lambda_{i})-g_{\star}(\lambda_{i})\bigr)^{2},
(66)
where ai:=τ2​λi+σ2a_{i}:=\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2}.
Specializing to gradient flow f^​(t)=gt​(K)​r\hat{f}(t)=g_{t}(K)r with gt​(λ)=1−e−t​λg_{t}(\lambda)=1-e^{-t\lambda} gives a one-parameter family of risks. Define the oracle time
t⋆​(K)∈arg⁡mint≥0⁡𝔼⁡[‖gt​(K)​r−f⋆‖22∣K].t^{\star}(K)\in\arg\min_{t\geq 0}\ \mathbb{E}\!\left[\|g_{t}(K)r-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\mid K\right].
(67)
G.2.1 Mode-wise ideal time
For a single eigenvalue λ>0\lambda>0, matching gt​(λ)=g⋆​(λ)g_{t}(\lambda)=g_{\star}(\lambda) yields an explicit ideal time.
Lemma G.5 (Mode-wise Bayes-matching time).
For λ>0\lambda>0, the unique tt satisfying gt​(λ)=g⋆​(λ)g_{t}(\lambda)=g_{\star}(\lambda) is
tλ⋆=1λ​log⁡(1+λλ⋆).t_{\lambda}^{\star}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\log\!\Bigl(1+\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_{\star}}\Bigr).
(68)
Proof.
gt​(λ)=1−e−t​λg_{t}(\lambda)=1-e^{-t\lambda} equals g⋆​(λ)=λ/(λ+λ⋆)g_{\star}(\lambda)=\lambda/(\lambda+\lambda_{\star}) iff
e−t​λ=λ⋆/(λ+λ⋆)e^{-t\lambda}=\lambda_{\star}/(\lambda+\lambda_{\star}), i.e. (68).
∎
Why a single time is a compromise.
The Bayes-matching time tλ⋆t^{\star}_{\lambda} depends on λ\lambda, so unless the spectrum is nearly degenerate, there is no single tt that simultaneously matches gt​(λi)=g⋆​(λi)g_{t}(\lambda_{i})=g_{\star}(\lambda_{i}) for all modes. Consequently, any fixed horizon must trade off over-fitting fast modes (large λ\lambda) against under-fitting slow modes (small λ\lambda). This motivates selecting a representative eigenvalue (e.g. μ⁡(K)\mu(K)) or using a time-mixture.
A simple near-oracle proxy matches at a representative eigenvalue. Define the weighted mean
μ⁡(K):=∑i=1nai​λi∑i=1nai,ai=τ2​λi+σ2.\mu(K):=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\lambda_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}},\qquad a_{i}=\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2}.
(69)
Set the match-at-μ\mu time
tμ:=1μ⁡(K)​log⁡(1+μ⁡(K)λ⋆)⇒gtμ​(μ⁡(K))=g⋆​(μ⁡(K)).t_{\mu}:=\frac{1}{\mu(K)}\log\!\Bigl(1+\frac{\mu(K)}{\lambda_{\star}}\Bigr)\,\Rightarrow\,g_{t_{\mu}}(\mu(K))=g_{\star}(\mu(K)).
(70)
Proposition G.6 (Bayes as an exponential mixture of diffusion times).
For λ⋆>0\lambda_{\star}>0 and all λ≥0\lambda\geq 0,
g⋆​(λ)=∫0∞λ⋆​e−λ⋆​s​(1−e−s​λ)​𝑑s.g_{\star}(\lambda)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{\star}e^{-\lambda_{\star}s}\,\bigl(1-e^{-s\lambda}\bigr)\,ds.
(71)
The same identity holds at the matrix level g⋆​(K)=∫0∞λ⋆​e−λ⋆​s​(I−e−s​K)​𝑑sg_{\star}(K)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{\star}e^{-\lambda_{\star}s}(I-e^{-sK})ds.
Proof.
Compute the scalar integral:
∫0∞λ⋆​e−λ⋆​s​𝑑s=1\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{\star}e^{-\lambda_{\star}s}ds=1 and
∫0∞λ⋆​e−(λ⋆+λ)​s​𝑑s=λ⋆/(λ⋆+λ)\int_{0}^{\infty}\lambda_{\star}e^{-(\lambda_{\star}+\lambda)s}ds=\lambda_{\star}/(\lambda_{\star}+\lambda).
Subtract to obtain g⋆​(λ)g_{\star}(\lambda). Apply spectral calculus for the matrix form.
∎
Interpretation (random stopping time).
Let S∼Exp⁡(λ⋆)S\sim\mathrm{Exp}(\lambda_{\star}) be an exponential random time. Then, Prop. G.6 is equivalent to g⋆​(K)​r=𝔼⁡[(I−e−S​K)​r]g_{\star}(K)r=\mathbb{E}[(I-e^{-SK})r]. In words, the Bayes shrinkage equals the expected gradient-flow predictor evaluated at a random diffusion time. This highlights why broad spectra are problematic for a single deterministic tt and Bayes effectively averages over a range of times.
G.2.2 A continuous-time spectral concentration bound
Define the mismatch dt​(λ):=gt​(λ)−g⋆​(λ)d_{t}(\lambda):=g_{t}(\lambda)-g_{\star}(\lambda).
Proposition G.7 (Spectral concentration of mismatch (gradient flow)).
For any t≥0t\geq 0,
∑i=1nai​dt​(λi)2≤2​(∑i=1nai)​dt​(μ)2+2​(t+1λ⋆)2​∑i=1nai​(λi−μ)2\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\,d_{t}(\lambda_{i})^{2}\leq 2\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}\Big)\,d_{t}(\mu)^{2}+2\Big(t+\frac{1}{\lambda_{\star}}\Big)^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}(\lambda_{i}-\mu)^{2}, where ai=τ2​λi+σ2a_{i}=\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2} and μ=∑iai​λi∑iai\mu=\frac{\sum_{i}a_{i}\lambda_{i}}{\sum_{i}a_{i}}.
Proof.
gt′​(λ)=t​e−t​λ≤tg_{t}^{\prime}(\lambda)=te^{-t\lambda}\leq t so gtg_{t} is tt-Lipschitz.
Also g⋆′​(λ)=λ⋆(λ+λ⋆)2≤1/λ⋆g_{\star}^{\prime}(\lambda)=\frac{\lambda_{\star}}{(\lambda+\lambda_{\star})^{2}}\leq 1/\lambda_{\star}.
Hence dtd_{t} is (t+1/λ⋆)(t+1/\lambda_{\star})-Lipschitz.
For each ii, |dt​(λi)|≤|dt​(μ)|+(t+1/λ⋆)​|λi−μ||d_{t}(\lambda_{i})|\leq|d_{t}(\mu)|+(t+1/\lambda_{\star})|\lambda_{i}-\mu|.
Square and sum with weights aia_{i} to obtain the inequality.
∎
Interpretation (horizon instability).
The risk identity decomposes the error into two meaningful components: dt​(μ)2d_{t}(\mu)^{2} represents the mismatch at a representative eigenvalue, while ∑iai​(λi−μ)2\sum_{i}a_{i}(\lambda_{i}-\mu)^{2} penalizes spectral spread via weighted variance. Notably, the Lipschitz factor (t+1/λ⋆)(t+1/\lambda_{\star}) grows linearly with tt, suggesting that longer diffusion times heighten the procedure’s sensitivity to spectral heterogeneity. This scaling relationship provides a principled explanation for horizon instability, particularly why performance degrades when the prompt geometry KK possesses a broad spectrum.
Summary: Why TT is prompt-dependent (diffusion intuition)
•
Instability comes from spectral heterogeneity: wide spectra make a single tt overfit fast modes and underfit slow ones, so performance becomes sensitive to TT .
•
Bayes behaves like mixing times: the Bayes shrinkage equals a mixture over stopping times.
G.3 RMT: deterministic oracle curves from asymptotic spectra
Define the per-token risk under gradient flow
ℛ¯in,n​(t∣K):=1n​∑i=1n[τ2​λi​e−2​t​λi+σ2​(1−e−t​λi)2].\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{in},n}(t\mid K):=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigl[\tau^{2}\lambda_{i}e^{-2t\lambda_{i}}+\sigma^{2}(1-e^{-t\lambda_{i}})^{2}\Bigr].
Spectral Concentration and Horizon Selection.
Although the prompt kernel KK is random, in high dimensions, its spectrum is highly predictable. Specifically, averages over eigenvalues 1n​∑i=1nh⁡(λi​(K))\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h(\lambda_{i}(K)) fluctuate little and converge to integrals under a limiting law (e.g., the Marchenko–Pastur distribution). Since our normalized risk can be expressed as such an eigenvalue-average for each tt (i.e., ℛ¯in,n​(t∣K)=1n​tr​ψt​(K)\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{in},n}(t\mid K)=\frac{1}{n}\mathrm{tr}\,\psi_{t}(K) for an appropriate function ψt\psi_{t}), the entire risk curve t↦ℛ¯in,n​(t∣K)t\mapsto\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{in},n}(t\mid K) and its minimizer admit deterministic approximations. This provides a rigorous foundation for using simple spectral moments, such as μ⁡(K)\mu(K), as stable and robust horizon predictors.
Assumption G.8 (Wishart kernel model).
Let Φ=1d​X∈ℝn×d\Phi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d} with i.i.d. mean-zero, variance-one subgaussian entries, and n,d→∞n,d\to\infty with n/d→γ∈(0,∞)n/d\to\gamma\in(0,\infty).
Set K=Φ​Φ⊤=1d​X​X⊤K=\Phi\Phi^{\top}=\frac{1}{d}XX^{\top}.
Proposition G.9 (Asymptotic risk under a Marchenko–Pastur geometry).
Let Φ∈ℝn×d\Phi\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d} have i.i.d. entries Φi​j∼𝒩⁡(0,1/d)\Phi_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1/d) and set
K:=Φ​Φ⊤K:=\Phi\Phi^{\top}.
Assume n,d→∞n,d\to\infty with n/d→γ∈(0,∞)n/d\to\gamma\in(0,\infty).
Under the aligned GP benchmark (Assump. G.3), consider the gradient-flow estimator
f^​(t)=(I−e−t​K)​r\hat{f}(t)=(I-e^{-tK})r.
Then, almost surely,
1n​𝔼​[‖f^​(t)−f⋆‖22|K]⟶∫ψt​(λ)​μγ​(𝑑λ),\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}(t)-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K\right]\ \longrightarrow\ \int\psi_{t}(\lambda)\,\mu_{\gamma}(d\lambda),
(72)
where ψt​(λ):=τ2​λ​e−2​t​λ+σ2​(1−e−t​λ)2\psi_{t}(\lambda):=\tau^{2}\lambda e^{-2t\lambda}+\sigma^{2}(1-e^{-t\lambda})^{2} and μγ\mu_{\gamma} is the
Marchenko–Pastur law with aspect ratio γ\gamma, i.e.
μγ​(d​λ)=(1−1γ)+​δ0​(d​λ)+(λ+−λ)​(λ−λ−)2​π​γ​λ​ 1[λ−,λ+]​(λ)​d​λ,\mu_{\gamma}(d\lambda)=\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{\gamma}\Bigr)_{+}\delta_{0}(d\lambda)+\frac{\sqrt{(\lambda_{+}-\lambda)(\lambda-\lambda_{-})}}{2\pi\gamma\lambda}\,\mathbf{1}_{[\lambda_{-},\lambda_{+}]}(\lambda)\,d\lambda, with λ±=(1±γ)2.\lambda_{\pm}=(1\pm\sqrt{\gamma})^{2}.
Proof.
By Prop. G.4 with g=gt​(λ)=1−e−t​λg=g_{t}(\lambda)=1-e^{-t\lambda}, conditioning on
K=U​diag​(λi)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{i})U^{\top} gives
1n​𝔼​[‖f^​(t)−f⋆‖22|K]=1n​∑i=1nψt​(λi),\frac{1}{n}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\|\hat{f}(t)-f_{\star}\|_{2}^{2}\,\middle|\,K\right]=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{t}(\lambda_{i}),
where ψt​(λ)=τ2​λ​e−2​t​λ+σ2​(1−e−t​λ)2.\psi_{t}(\lambda)=\tau^{2}\lambda e^{-2t\lambda}+\sigma^{2}(1-e^{-t\lambda})^{2}.
Let μn:=1n​∑i=1nδλi\mu_{n}:=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\lambda_{i}} be the empirical spectral measure of KK.
For K=Φ​Φ⊤=(1/d)​X​X⊤K=\Phi\Phi^{\top}=(1/d)XX^{\top} with Xi​j∼𝒩⁡(0,1)X_{ij}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1), the Marchenko-Pastur theorem yields μn⇒μγ\mu_{n}\Rightarrow\mu_{\gamma} almost surely as n,d→∞n,d\to\infty with n/d→γn/d\to\gamma
[29, 5].
Since ψt\psi_{t} is bounded and continuous on [0,∞)[0,\infty), we have
∫ψt​d​μn→∫ψt​d​μγ\int\psi_{t}\,d\mu_{n}\to\int\psi_{t}\,d\mu_{\gamma} almost surely.
∎
G.3.1 Moment proxy: deterministic “representative eigenvalue”
The weighted mean eigenvalue μ⁡(K)\mu(K) in (69) admits a deterministic limit under MP moments.
Since m1​(γ):=∫λ​νγ​(𝑑λ)=1m_{1}(\gamma):=\int\lambda\,\nu_{\gamma}(d\lambda)=1 and m2​(γ):=∫λ2​νγ​(𝑑λ)=1+γm_{2}(\gamma):=\int\lambda^{2}\,\nu_{\gamma}(d\lambda)=1+\gamma,
Proposition G.10 (Deterministic equivalent of μ⁡(K)\mu(K)).
Under Assump. G.8,
μ⁡(K)→a.s.μ∞:=τ2​m2​(γ)+σ2​m1​(γ)τ2​m1​(γ)+σ2=(1+γ)+λ⋆1+λ⋆.\mu(K)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{a.s.}}\mu_{\infty}:=\frac{\tau^{2}m_{2}(\gamma)+\sigma^{2}m_{1}(\gamma)}{\tau^{2}m_{1}(\gamma)+\sigma^{2}}=\frac{(1+\gamma)+\lambda_{\star}}{1+\lambda_{\star}}.
(73)
Thus the match-at-μ\mu time concentrates around
tμ∞=1μ∞​log⁡(1+μ∞λ⋆).t_{\mu_{\infty}}=\frac{1}{\mu_{\infty}}\log\!\Bigl(1+\frac{\mu_{\infty}}{\lambda_{\star}}\Bigr).
(74)
Proof.
Write μ⁡(K)=τ2​1n​∑λi2+σ2​1n​∑λiτ2​1n​∑λi+σ2\mu(K)=\frac{\tau^{2}\frac{1}{n}\sum\lambda_{i}^{2}+\sigma^{2}\frac{1}{n}\sum\lambda_{i}}{\tau^{2}\frac{1}{n}\sum\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2}} and use the Marchenko-Pastur theorem.
∎
Concentration replacement (what is random).
Under Assump. G.8, KK (hence {λi}\{\lambda_{i}\}, μ⁡(K)\mu(K), and ℛ¯in,n\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{in},n}) is random.
The limits νγ\nu_{\gamma}, μ∞\mu_{\infty}, and ℛ∞\mathcal{R}_{\infty} are deterministic. Standard concentration for linear spectral statistics implies μ⁡(K)\mu(K) and ℛ¯in,n​(t∣K)\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{\mathrm{in},n}(t\mid K) concentrate around these limits (proof sketch omitted; see standard subgaussian random matrix concentration).
G.4 Transformer head example: head choice ⇒\Rightarrow spectrum ⇒\Rightarrow horizon
We specialize to Value-matrix updates in Transformer head hh under the global-upstream regime. With attention matrix 𝒜(h)\mathcal{A}^{(h)} (entries αk​s(h)\alpha_{ks}^{(h)}), residual stream stack Z∈ℝn×dzZ\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d_{z}}, and upstream direction β(h)\beta^{(h)}, the head-induced prompt kernel is
K(h,V)≈‖β(h)‖22​𝒜(h)​(Z​Z⊤)​𝒜(h)⊤,K=∑h∈ℋK(h,V).K^{(h,V)}\approx\|\beta^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\,\mathcal{A}^{(h)}(ZZ^{\top})\mathcal{A}^{(h)\top},\,K=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}K^{(h,V)}.
(75)
Thus head selection ℋ\mathcal{H} changes the eigenvalues {λi​(K)}\{\lambda_{i}(K)\} and therefore changes the near-oracle time (e.g. tμt_{\mu} in (70)).
G.4.1 Two regimes and a toy calculation
(i) Spiky / low-rank head.
Suppose 𝒜(h)​Z\mathcal{A}^{(h)}Z is approximately rank-one:
𝒜(h)​Z≈κ​v​u⊤\mathcal{A}^{(h)}Z\approx\sqrt{\kappa}\,vu^{\top} with ‖v‖2=1\|v\|_{2}=1.
Then K(h,V)≈λsp​v​v⊤K^{(h,V)}\approx\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}\;vv^{\top} with spike
λsp:=‖β(h)‖22​κ.\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}:=\|\beta^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\,\kappa.
