Toy horizon. If K≈λsp​v​v⊤K\approx\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}\,vv^{\top} exactly, matching gt​(λsp)=g⋆​(λsp)g_{t}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}})=g_{\star}(\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}) yields
tsp=1λsp​log⁡(1+λspλ⋆),Tsp≈tsp/ρ.t_{\mathrm{sp}}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}}\log\!\Bigl(1+\frac{\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}}{\lambda_{\star}}\Bigr),\qquad T_{\mathrm{sp}}\approx t_{\mathrm{sp}}/\rho.
(76)
Intuition (spike ⇒\Rightarrow step-sensitivity).
A dominant spike makes the effective dynamics essentially one-dimensional and very fast. The residual energy along the spike direction decays on the scale t∼1/λspt\sim 1/\lambda_{\mathrm{sp}}.
Hence small changes in TT (or ρ\rho) can move the predictor from under- to over-adaptation quickly, producing sharp performance cliffs.
(ii) Mixing head / MP-like bulk.
If 𝒜(h)​Z\mathcal{A}^{(h)}Z behaves like a high-rank random feature matrix, then K⁡(ℋ)K(\mathcal{H}) can have an MP-like bulk.
Let dℋd_{\mathcal{H}} denote the effective feature dimension contributed by selected heads, and γℋ:=n/dℋ\gamma_{\mathcal{H}}:=n/d_{\mathcal{H}}.
Then the deterministic proxy (73) suggests
μ∞​(γℋ)=(1+γℋ)+λ⋆1+λ⋆\mu_{\infty}(\gamma_{\mathcal{H}})=\frac{(1+\gamma_{\mathcal{H}})+\lambda_{\star}}{1+\lambda_{\star}}, and tμ∞​(γℋ)=1μ∞​(γℋ)​log⁡(1+μ∞​(γℋ)λ⋆)t_{\mu_{\infty}(\gamma_{\mathcal{H}})}=\frac{1}{\mu_{\infty}(\gamma_{\mathcal{H}})}\log\!\Bigl(1+\frac{\mu_{\infty}(\gamma_{\mathcal{H}})}{\lambda_{\star}}\Bigr).
Intuition.
Adding heads expands the effective dimension dℋd_{\mathcal{H}}, thereby reducing the aspect ratio γℋ\gamma_{\mathcal{H}}. This adjustment reshapes the spectral distribution of K⁡(ℋ)K(\mathcal{H}) and, consequently, shifts the predicted near-oracle horizon, reflecting a higher adaptation capacity.
Summary: spectrum couples how far and which heads to adapt
•
Spiky heads: large eigenvalues ⇒\Rightarrow faster diffusion ⇒\Rightarrow smaller near-oracle times.
•
Mixing heads: bulk spectra ⇒\Rightarrow horizons are well-approximated by deterministic equivalents.
Which heads to adapt and how far to adapt are spectrally coupled through the prompt geometry.
Appendix H Transformer-specific derivations
H.1 Technical details for Transformer Jacobian features
H.1.1 Outer-product Jacobian for linear layers
Lemma H.1 (Outer-product Jacobian for linear layers (exact at w0w_{0})).
Let y=W​xy=Wx with W∈ℝdout×dinW\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}\times d_{\mathrm{in}}} be a linear layer in the computation graph,
and let si​(w)s_{i}(w) be any scalar output. Evaluate all forward activations and backpropagated gradients at w0w_{0}, and define ai:=x|w0∈ℝdina_{i}:=x\big|_{w_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{in}}} and bi:=∇ysi​(w)|w0∈ℝdoutb_{i}:=\nabla_{y}s_{i}(w)\big|_{w_{0}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{out}}}. Then, using the column-major convention for vec⁡(⋅)\mathrm{vec}(\cdot),
∇vec⁡(W)si​(w)|w0=ai⊗bi.\nabla_{\mathrm{vec}(W)}s_{i}(w)\big|_{w_{0}}=a_{i}\otimes b_{i}.
Consequently, the kernel contribution induced by a full update of WW satisfies
Ki​j(W)=⟨∇vec⁡(W)si,∇vec⁡(W)sj⟩=(ai⊤​aj)​(bi⊤​bj).K_{ij}^{(W)}=\Bigl\langle\nabla_{\mathrm{vec}(W)}s_{i},\ \nabla_{\mathrm{vec}(W)}s_{j}\Bigr\rangle=(a_{i}^{\top}a_{j})\,(b_{i}^{\top}b_{j}).
Proof.
By the chain rule for y=W​xy=Wx, holding xx fixed when differentiating w.r.t. WW,
∇Wsi​(w)|w0=(∇ysi​(w)|w0)​(x|w0)⊤=bi​ai⊤.\nabla_{W}s_{i}(w)\big|_{w_{0}}=\bigl(\nabla_{y}s_{i}(w)\big|_{w_{0}}\bigr)\,\bigl(x\big|_{w_{0}}\bigr)^{\top}=b_{i}a_{i}^{\top}.
Under the column-major convention, vec⁡(b​a⊤)=a⊗b\mathrm{vec}(ba^{\top})=a\otimes b, hence
∇vec⁡(W)si​(w)|w0=vec⁡(bi​ai⊤)=ai⊗bi\nabla_{\mathrm{vec}(W)}s_{i}(w)\big|_{w_{0}}=\mathrm{vec}(b_{i}a_{i}^{\top})=a_{i}\otimes b_{i}.
Finally,
⟨ai⊗bi,aj⊗bj⟩=(ai⊤​aj)​(bi⊤​bj)\langle a_{i}\otimes b_{i},\ a_{j}\otimes b_{j}\rangle=(a_{i}^{\top}a_{j})(b_{i}^{\top}b_{j}).
∎
H.1.2 Local linearization regime: fixed forward/backward features
Assumption H.2 (Fixed forward/backward features in the local regime).
When forming Jacobian features for selected parameter blocks and the induced geometry KK, we evaluate all forward activations (e.g. residual streams zsz_{s} and attention weights αt​s(h)\alpha^{(h)}_{ts})
and all upstream gradients (e.g. gig_{i}) at w0w_{0} and treat them as constants under the local linearization.
Equivalently, we ignore second-order feedback effects whereby changing one block perturbs intermediate activations/gradients
used to define Jacobian features of another block.
Remark (why attention can be treated as fixed for WV/WOW_{V}/W_{O} features).
For standard attention, αt​s(h)\alpha^{(h)}_{ts} depends on (WQ(h),WK(h))(W_{Q}^{(h)},W_{K}^{(h)}) and the input activations,
but not directly on WV(h)W_{V}^{(h)} or WO(h)W_{O}^{(h)}.
Thus, when differentiating w.r.t. WV(h)W_{V}^{(h)} or WO(h)W_{O}^{(h)} at w0w_{0}, holding α(h)\alpha^{(h)} fixed is exact at the layer level;
Assumption H.2 only rules out indirect, higher-order changes of α(h)\alpha^{(h)} through upstream activation shifts when multiple blocks are updated.
Similar “frozen-attention” simplifications also appear in theoretical analyses of attention in random-feature regimes [15].
H.1.3 Derivation: value-matrix updates WVW_{V}
Fix a coordinate ii that supervises token k=k⁡(i)k=k(i) and let gi:=∇ok(h)si​(w)|w0g_{i}:=\nabla_{o_{k}^{(h)}}s_{i}(w)\big|_{w_{0}} be the upstream gradient into ok(h)o_{k}^{(h)} at w0w_{0}.
Under Assumption H.2, the first-order dependence on WV(h)W_{V}^{(h)} is
δ​si=⟨gi,WO(h)​∑s≤kαk​s(h)​δ​WV(h)​zs⟩+o⁡(‖δ​WV(h)‖).\delta s_{i}=\Bigl\langle g_{i},\;W_{O}^{(h)}\sum_{s\leq k}\alpha_{ks}^{(h)}\,\delta W_{V}^{(h)}z_{s}\Bigr\rangle\;+\;o(\|\delta W_{V}^{(h)}\|).
Define βi(h):=(WO(h))⊤​gi∈ℝdh,\beta_{i}^{(h)}:=(W_{O}^{(h)})^{\top}g_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{h}}, and mi(h):=∑s≤k⁡(i)αk⁡(i)​s(h)​zs∈ℝdmodelm_{i}^{(h)}:=\sum_{s\leq k(i)}\alpha_{k(i)s}^{(h)}z_{s}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\mathrm{model}}}. Then δ​si=⟨βi(h),δ​WV(h)​mi(h)⟩+o⁡(‖δ​WV(h)‖)\delta s_{i}=\langle\beta_{i}^{(h)},\ \delta W_{V}^{(h)}m_{i}^{(h)}\rangle+o(\|\delta W_{V}^{(h)}\|), so by
Lem. H.1 applied at w0w_{0},
∇vec⁡(WV(h))si​(w)|w0=mi(h)⊗βi(h),Ki​j(h,V)=(mi(h)⊤​mj(h))​(βi(h)⊤​βj(h)).\nabla_{\mathrm{vec}(W_{V}^{(h)})}s_{i}(w)\big|_{w_{0}}=m_{i}^{(h)}\otimes\beta_{i}^{(h)},\quad K_{ij}^{(h,V)}=(m_{i}^{(h)\top}m_{j}^{(h)})\,(\beta_{i}^{(h)\top}\beta_{j}^{(h)}).
Matrix form and PSD.
Under tokenwise supervision (i≡ki\equiv k), stacking mk(h)m_{k}^{(h)} and βk(h)\beta_{k}^{(h)} gives the Hadamard form
(3). Since M(h)​M(h)⊤⪰0M^{(h)}M^{(h)\top}\succeq 0 and 𝐁(h)​𝐁(h)⊤⪰0\mathbf{B}^{(h)}\mathbf{B}^{(h)\top}\succeq 0,
the Schur product theorem implies K(h,V)⪰0K^{(h,V)}\succeq 0.
The global-upstream specialization yields the attention-sandwiched Gram (4).
H.1.4 Derivation: output-matrix updates WOW_{O}
If we update WO(h)W_{O}^{(h)} only, then (at w0w_{0})
δ​si=⟨gi,δ​WO(h)​uk⁡(i)(h)⟩+o⁡(‖δ​WO(h)‖),\delta s_{i}=\langle g_{i},\ \delta W_{O}^{(h)}u_{k(i)}^{(h)}\rangle\;+\;o(\|\delta W_{O}^{(h)}\|),
so Lem. H.1 yields
∇vec⁡(WO(h))si​(w)|w0=uk⁡(i)(h)⊗gi,Ki​j(h,O)=(uk⁡(i)(h)⊤​uk⁡(j)(h))​(gi⊤​gj).\nabla_{\mathrm{vec}(W_{O}^{(h)})}s_{i}(w)\big|_{w_{0}}=u_{k(i)}^{(h)}\otimes g_{i},\,K_{ij}^{(h,O)}=\bigl(u_{k(i)}^{(h)\top}u_{k(j)}^{(h)}\bigr)\,\bigl(g_{i}^{\top}g_{j}\bigr).
Since u(h)=𝒜(h)​V(h)u^{(h)}=\mathcal{A}^{(h)}V^{(h)} with V(h):=Z​WV(h)⊤V^{(h)}:=ZW_{V}^{(h)\top}, this again exhibits attention-induced structure.
H.1.5 Derivation: MLP block updates
For an MLP block hk=σ⁡(W1​zk)h_{k}=\sigma(W_{1}z_{k}) and mlpk=W2​hk\mathrm{mlp}_{k}=W_{2}h_{k},
updating W2W_{2} yields Jacobian features hk⊗gkh_{k}\otimes g_{k} (Gram of hidden activations).
Updating W1W_{1} yields features
zk⊗(σ′​(W1​zk)⊙(W2⊤​gk))z_{k}\otimes\bigl(\sigma^{\prime}(W_{1}z_{k})\odot(W_{2}^{\top}g_{k})\bigr),
again an outer product (input ×\times backpropagated gate).
H.1.6 Additivity across updated blocks
If ℬ\mathcal{B} is a set of disjoint parameter blocks that are updated, stacking Jacobians blockwise yields
Φ=[Φ(b)]b∈ℬ\Phi=[\Phi^{(b)}]_{b\in\mathcal{B}} and hence K=Φ​Φ⊤=∑b∈ℬK(b)K=\Phi\Phi^{\top}=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}}K^{(b)}.
H.2 A simple stability bound for the step size
In the global-upstream regime (4) for a head hh,
K(h,V)=‖β(h)‖22​M(h)​M(h)⊤,M(h)=𝒜(h)​Z.K^{(h,V)}=\|\beta^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\,M^{(h)}M^{(h)\top},\quad M^{(h)}=\mathcal{A}^{(h)}Z.
Therefore,
λmax​(K(h,V))=‖β(h)‖22​‖M(h)‖op2≤‖β(h)‖22​‖𝒜(h)‖op2​‖Z‖op2.\lambda_{\max}(K^{(h,V)})=\|\beta^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\,\|M^{(h)}\|_{\rm op}^{2}\leq\|\beta^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}\,\|\mathcal{A}^{(h)}\|_{\rm op}^{2}\,\|Z\|_{\rm op}^{2}.
Since K=∑b∈ℬK(b)K=\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}}K^{(b)} is PSD, λmax​(K)≤∑b∈ℬλmax​(K(b))\lambda_{\max}(K)\leq\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}}\lambda_{\max}(K^{(b)}),
so enlarging the updated block set tightens the admissible step-size range 0<ρ<1/λmax​(K)0<\rho<1/\lambda_{\max}(K).
H.3 Representable prompt subspace for value updates
Lemma H.3 (Representable prompt subspace for WVW_{V} updates (global-β\beta case)).
Assume tokenwise supervision and a global upstream direction gk≡gg_{k}\equiv g, so that
βk(h)≡β(h)\beta_{k}^{(h)}\equiv\beta^{(h)} with ‖β(h)‖>0\|\beta^{(h)}\|>0 for each head hh.
For a head hh with WVW_{V} updates, K(h,V)=‖β(h)‖22​M(h)​M(h)⊤K^{(h,V)}=\|\beta^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}M^{(h)}M^{(h)\top} where M(h)=𝒜(h)​ZM^{(h)}=\mathcal{A}^{(h)}Z.
Hence range⁡(K(h,V))=range⁡(M(h))=range⁡(𝒜(h)​Z)\mathrm{range}(K^{(h,V)})=\mathrm{range}(M^{(h)})=\mathrm{range}(\mathcal{A}^{(h)}Z).
If we update a head set ℋ\mathcal{H} (and sum their kernel components), then
range⁡(∑h∈ℋK(h,V))\displaystyle\mathrm{range}\!\left(\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}K^{(h,V)}\right)
=∑h∈ℋrange⁡(M(h))\displaystyle=\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{range}(M^{(h)})
(77)
=range⁡([M(h)]h∈ℋ),\displaystyle=\mathrm{range}\!\left([\,M^{(h)}\,]_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\right),
(78)
where [M(h)]h∈ℋ[\,M^{(h)}\,]_{h\in\mathcal{H}} denotes column-wise concatenation.
Proof.
In the global-β\beta case, K(h,V)=‖β(h)‖22​M(h)​M(h)⊤K^{(h,V)}=\|\beta^{(h)}\|_{2}^{2}M^{(h)}M^{(h)\top}, hence
range⁡(K(h,V))=range⁡(M(h)​M(h)⊤)=range⁡(M(h))\mathrm{range}(K^{(h,V)})=\mathrm{range}(M^{(h)}M^{(h)\top})=\mathrm{range}(M^{(h)}).
For the sum, note that for symmetric PSD matrices {Sℓ}\{S_{\ell}\},
null(∑ℓSℓ)=∩ℓnull(Sℓ)\mathrm{null}(\sum_{\ell}S_{\ell})=\cap_{\ell}\mathrm{null}(S_{\ell}) because
x⊤​(∑ℓSℓ)​x=0x^{\top}(\sum_{\ell}S_{\ell})x=0 implies x⊤​Sℓ​x=0x^{\top}S_{\ell}x=0 for every ℓ\ell; by PSD-ness this gives Sℓ​x=0S_{\ell}x=0.
Taking orthogonal complements yields
range⁡(∑ℓSℓ)=∑ℓrange⁡(Sℓ)\mathrm{range}(\sum_{\ell}S_{\ell})=\sum_{\ell}\mathrm{range}(S_{\ell}).
Finally, ∑h∈ℋrange⁡(M(h))=range⁡([M(h)]h∈ℋ)\sum_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathrm{range}(M^{(h)})=\mathrm{range}([M^{(h)}]_{h\in\mathcal{H}}) is standard.
∎
H.4 Computational cost, memory, and scalable approximations
This appendix complements Sections 7 and 8 by (i) making
the computational overhead explicit (time, memory, and additional forward/backward passes), and (ii) describing
practical approximations for large Jacobians. We emphasize that the expressions
(Jw​Σ​Jw⊤+σ2​I)−1(J_{w}\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}+\sigma^{2}I)^{-1} and ℐ\mathcal{I} can look expensive, but the core linear algebra lives in
prompt space (dimension nn) and can be implemented via Jacobian–vector products (JVP/VJP), without
materializing Jw∈ℝn×pJ_{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}.
H.4.1 Bookkeeping and primitive costs
We use the following dimensions (consistent with the main text):
•
pp: number of model parameters.
•
nn: number of prompt positions / residual terms (Sec. 3).
•
dd: dimension of the update subspace 𝒰=range⁡(A)\mathcal{U}=\mathrm{range}(A) (Assumption 3.1).
•
ℬall\mathcal{B}_{\rm all}: partition of parameters into blocks (e.g., by layer/head/matrix type)
used in Sec. 8.
To keep the discussion model-agnostic, we express runtime in terms of primitive passes:
•
CfwdC_{\mathrm{fwd}}: cost of one forward pass on the prompt objective (producing the supervision outputs
sw​(Pn)∈ℝns_{w}(P_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{n} and the scalar loss).
•
Cbwd​(𝒮)C_{\mathrm{bwd}}(\mathcal{S}): cost of one backward pass producing gradients restricted to a parameter set
𝒮\mathcal{S} (e.g., the updated parameters, or a single block bb).
In practice, Cbwd​(𝒮)C_{\mathrm{bwd}}(\mathcal{S}) is typically of the same order as a full backward pass unless
specialized kernels or partial-backprop are used.
Throughout, the memory footprint at test time is dominated by storing activations needed for a backward pass.
Importantly, memory does not scale with the number of adaptation steps TT when updates are performed
sequentially (activations are freed after each step), but time does scale with TT.
H.4.2 Baseline: no-update inference vs. standard TTT
Let TT be the number of test-time gradient steps on the prompt loss.
A coarse but useful accounting is:
•
No-update inference (ICL / standard inference):
one forward pass on the prompt+query, and no backward pass.
•
Standard TTT (few-step adaptation):
each step requires (at least) one forward pass to form the prompt loss and one backward pass to compute gradients,
followed by a final forward pass for the query prediction. Thus,
TTT runtime≈T⁡(Cfwd+Cbwd​(𝒰))+Cfwd,query.\text{TTT runtime}\approx T\,(C_{\mathrm{fwd}}+C_{\mathrm{bwd}}(\mathcal{U}))+C_{\mathrm{fwd,query}}.
This matches common lightweight TTT baselines (e.g., updating only a small parameter subset / LoRA state)
where the dominant cost is the backward pass, not the parameter update itself.
H.4.3 Constructing the prompt geometry KK without forming JwJ_{w}
Many quantities in Sections 4–7 depend on
K=Φ​Φ⊤=Jw​Π𝒰​Jw⊤∈ℝn×nK=\Phi\Phi^{\top}=J_{w}\Pi_{\mathcal{U}}J_{w}^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}.
Crucially, KK is an n×nn\times n matrix: when nn is modest (few-shot prompts or a small set of tokens),
forming and factorizing KK is cheap, and the real bottleneck is accessing Jacobian information.
Explicit feature construction (Φ\Phi).
Recall Φ∈ℝn×d\Phi\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d} stacks the Jacobian features
ϕ​(xi,Pn(−i))⊤=∇θsi​(w⁡(θ))|θ=0\phi(x_{i};P_{n}^{(-i)})^{\top}=\nabla_{\theta}s_{i}(w(\theta))|_{\theta=0}.
If one explicitly builds Φ\Phi, the simplest approach is nn reverse-mode calls (one per sis_{i}) restricted to
the updated parameters:
cost to build ​Φ≈n⋅Cbwd​(𝒰),\text{cost to build }\Phi\;\approx\;n\cdot C_{\mathrm{bwd}}(\mathcal{U}),
memory≈one backward-pass footprint.\text{memory}\;\approx\;\text{one backward-pass footprint}.
Once Φ\Phi is available, K=Φ​Φ⊤K=\Phi\Phi^{\top} costs O⁡(n2​d)O(n^{2}d) flops and stores O⁡(n2)O(n^{2}) numbers.
Implicit matrix–vector products (recommended for large pp and/or larger nn).
Many downstream procedures do not require Φ\Phi itself, only products of the form K​vKv or
(K+λ​I)−1​v(K+\lambda I)^{-1}v. These can be computed without forming JwJ_{w}:
K​v=Jw​Π𝒰​Jw⊤​v.Kv\;=\;J_{w}\Pi_{\mathcal{U}}J_{w}^{\top}v.
(79)
A single K​vKv can be computed with one VJP and one JVP (or two reverse-mode passes via Pearlmutter’s trick):
1.
compute g:=Jw⊤​vg:=J_{w}^{\top}v (one VJP / backward pass),
2.
project g𝒰:=Π𝒰​gg_{\mathcal{U}}:=\Pi_{\mathcal{U}}g (cheap: masking or low-rank projection),
3.
compute K​v:=Jw​g𝒰Kv:=J_{w}g_{\mathcal{U}} (one JVP / forward-mode, or equivalent).
Thus,
cost of one ​K​v≈Cbwd​(params)+Cjvp​(params),\text{cost of one }Kv\;\approx\;C_{\mathrm{bwd}}(\text{params})+C_{\mathrm{jvp}}(\text{params}),
where CjvpC_{\mathrm{jvp}} depends on the autodiff implementation. This is the standard way to scale kernel methods
and Gauss–Newton/Fisher computations in large models.
Solves with KK for larger nn.
When nn is small, one can form KK explicitly and use a Cholesky factorization at O⁡(n3)O(n^{3}) time.
When nn is larger, one can solve linear systems with conjugate gradients (CG) using the matvec in
(79). Each CG iteration costs one K​vKv (plus O⁡(n)O(n) vector ops), and the number of iterations
depends on the conditioning of K+λ​IK+\lambda I.
H.4.4 Cost of PAC-Bayes horizon selection (Sec. 7)
Horizon selection evaluates (for T∈𝒯T\in\mathcal{T}) the evidence score
ℓT(r)=12​n{logdet(ΣT)+r⊤ΣT−1r},\ell_{T}(r)=\frac{1}{2n}\left\{\log\det(\Sigma_{T})+r^{\top}\Sigma_{T}^{-1}r\right\},
where
ΣT=σ2​(I−ρ​K)−T\Sigma_{T}=\sigma^{2}(I-\rho K)^{-T}.
Given an eigendecomposition K=U​diag​(λi)​U⊤K=U\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{i})U^{\top}, we have
logdet(ΣT)=nlogσ2−∑i=1nTlog(1−ρλi),\log\det(\Sigma_{T})=n\log\sigma^{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}T\log(1-\rho\lambda_{i}),
r⊤​ΣT−1​r=1σ2​∑i=1n(1−ρ​λi)T​(ui⊤​r)2,r^{\top}\Sigma_{T}^{-1}r=\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\rho\lambda_{i})^{T}(u_{i}^{\top}r)^{2},
so evaluating ℓT\ell_{T} for all T∈𝒯T\in\mathcal{T} costs O⁡(|𝒯|​n)O(|\mathcal{T}|\,n) once {λi,ui⊤​r}\{\lambda_{i},u_{i}^{\top}r\} are known.
Therefore, the total overhead of evidence-based selection is:
cost to obtain ​K⏟dominant (Jacobian access)+O⁡(n3)⏟eig/Cholesky (cheap if n is small)+O⁡(|𝒯|​n)⏟negligible.\underbrace{\text{cost to obtain }K}_{\text{dominant (Jacobian access)}}\;+\;\underbrace{O(n^{3})}_{\text{eig/Cholesky (cheap if $n$ is small)}}\;+\;\underbrace{O(|\mathcal{T}|\,n)}_{\text{negligible}}.
In typical few-shot regimes (small nn), the extra prompt-space linear algebra is negligible relative to even a
single backward pass.
Net compute can decrease.
A practical point is that horizon selection can reduce total test-time compute by choosing small TT (or T=0T=0)
when adaptation is predicted to be unhelpful, potentially offsetting the cost of estimating KK.
H.4.5 Cost of Bayes-optimal subspace design (Sec. 8)
Thm. 8.2 involves the information-capture matrix
ℐ=Σ​Jw⊤​(Jw​Σ​Jw⊤+σ2​In)−1​Jw​Σ∈ℝp×p,\mathcal{I}=\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}(J_{w}\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}J_{w}\Sigma\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p},
whose full formation is infeasible for modern pp. In practice, we advocate two implementation regimes.
(A) Offline global design.
In many deployments, the update mechanism (which blocks/heads are trainable at test time) is fixed ahead of time.
One can estimate a global design from a development set of prompts (Cor. E.23)
and then keep the selected subspace fixed at test time.
In this regime, there is no additional test-time cost beyond the chosen TTT updates.
(B) Prompt-adaptive scoring via prompt-space formulas.
When restricting to block on/off selection and (optionally) an isotropic correction prior Σ=Ip\Sigma=I_{p}, Sec. 8.2 shows that the block score admits a purely prompt-space form:
sb:=tr⁡(ℐb​b)=tr⁡(Kfull(b)​(Kfull+σ2​In)−1),s_{b}:=\mathrm{tr}(\mathcal{I}_{bb})=\mathrm{tr}\!\left(K_{\rm full}^{(b)}(K_{\rm full}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}\right),
(80)
where Kfull=∑bKfull(b)K_{\rm full}=\sum_{b}K_{\rm full}^{(b)} and Kfull(b)=J(b)​J(b)⊤K_{\rm full}^{(b)}=J^{(b)}J^{(b)\top}. Here the only matrix inverse is (Kfull+σ2​In)−1(K_{\rm full}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1} in ℝn×n\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}.
Thus, the linear algebra is again in prompt space.
The remaining challenge is to obtain Kfull(b)K_{\rm full}^{(b)} (or its action on vectors) without forming J(b)J^{(b)}.
This can be done with implicit Jacobian products restricted to block bb:
Kfull(b)​v=J(b)​J(b)⊤​vK_{\rm full}^{(b)}v=J^{(b)}J^{(b)\top}v
via one VJP and one JVP restricted to block bb.
Hutchinson estimator for large block sets.
If scoring every block exactly is expensive, one can approximate (80) with
RR Hutchinson probes zr∼{±1}nz_{r}\sim\{\pm 1\}^{n}:
sb=𝔼z​[z⊤​Kfull(b)​(Kfull+σ2​I)−1​z]≈1R​∑r=1Rzr⊤​Kfull(b)​ur,s_{b}=\mathbb{E}_{z}\big[z^{\top}K_{\rm full}^{(b)}(K_{\rm full}+\sigma^{2}I)^{-1}z\big]\approx\frac{1}{R}\sum_{r=1}^{R}z_{r}^{\top}K_{\rm full}^{(b)}u_{r},
(81)
where ur:=(Kfull+σ2​I)−1​zru_{r}:=(K_{\rm full}+\sigma^{2}I)^{-1}z_{r}.
When nn is small, each uru_{r} can be computed by a direct n×nn\times n solve; when nn is large, CG with the matvec
Kfull​vK_{\rm full}v applies. The expensive part per probe is applying Kfull(b)K_{\rm full}^{(b)} to uru_{r}, which can be done
implicitly without storing gradients.
Top-eigenspace computation without forming ℐ\mathcal{I}.
If one truly requires the top-dd eigenspace of ℐ\mathcal{I} (e.g., within a block for low-rank adaptation),
one can use randomized SVD/Lanczos with only ℐ\mathcal{I}–vector products.
A matvec ℐ​v\mathcal{I}v can be implemented as:
ℐ​v=Σ​Jw⊤​(Jw​Σ​Jw⊤+σ2​In)−1⏟n×n solve​Jw​Σ​v,\mathcal{I}v=\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}\underbrace{(J_{w}\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}}_{\text{$n\times n$ solve}}J_{w}\Sigma v,
which requires (i) one JVP to compute Jw​Σ​vJ_{w}\Sigma v, (ii) one prompt-space solve, and (iii) one VJP to apply Jw⊤J_{w}^{\top},
plus multiplications by Σ\Sigma (cheap if Σ\Sigma is diagonal/structured). This yields a scalable route to
approximate eigenspaces without explicit p×pp\times p matrices.

H.4.7 Practical guidance and regimes of tractability
When is the matrix inverse cheap?
The inverse that appears in our design objectives, (Jw​Σ​Jw⊤+σ2​In)−1(J_{w}\Sigma J_{w}^{\top}+\sigma^{2}I_{n})^{-1}, is an n×nn\times n matrix.
Thus it is cheap whenever nn is modest. If nn is large (e.g., token-level supervision on long prompts),
one should not form this inverse explicitly; instead use CG/Lanczos with implicit matvecs.
Where is the true bottleneck?
The dominant cost is accessing Jacobian information, not the n×nn\times n linear algebra.
This is why we emphasize (i) restricting test-time updates to a small parameter subset, and
(ii) using implicit Jacobian products instead of explicit Jacobian materialization.
Amortization via offline design.
The subspace-design step can be performed offline once (global design) and reused at test time, eliminating
any additional inference-time overhead while still benefiting from a principled update mechanism.
Compute–robustness tradeoff.
Our framework explicitly trades compute for robustness:
step selection can avoid harmful over-adaptation (and may reduce TT), while subspace design can restrict updates
to identifiable, redundancy-aware directions (reducing the effective dimension of adaptation).
Both tradeoffs are consistent with the empirical observation that “more test-time optimization” can be worse
when the prompt signal is weak or misspecified.
Appendix I Experimental Details
I.1 Task and Data Generation
We evaluate our framework using distilgpt2 on a synthetic integer regression task.
We use the pretrained GPT-2 family model and, in particular, the distilgpt2 checkpoint released via the Hugging Face Transformers library [36, 53, 41].
Task Distribution. Each task is defined by a hidden shift s∈{0,…,9}s\in\{0,\dots,9\}. Data pairs (x,y)(x,y) are generated such that the clean label is y=(x+s)(mod10)y=(x+s)\pmod{10}. At test time, we sample n=10n=10 input digits and one query digit from 0,…,9{0,\ldots,9}.
Noise and Templates. We introduce label noise by replacing the target yy with a uniformly random digit sampled from {0,…,9}\{0,\dots,9\} with probability pnoisep_{\text{noise}}. We simulate two regimes: a clean regime where pnoise=0.0p_{\text{noise}}=0.0, and a noisy regime where pnoise=0.55p_{\text{noise}}=0.55. These values create a bimodal distribution over prompt quality, allowing us to test adaptive TT selection under varying signal-to-noise ratios.
The model sees the data formatted via two templates, selected randomly per task: "x->y" (Template 0) or "x:y" (Template 1).
I.2 Model and TTT Optimization
Architecture. We use the pretrained distilgpt2 model (6 layers, 12 heads, 768 embedding dim).
Update Subspace. We restrict optimization to the attention Value projection weights. For a selected block (l,h)(l,h) (layer ll, head hh), we construct a binary mask Ml,hM_{l,h} targeting the indices corresponding to the Value matrix in the fused c attn linear layer (indices 2​dmodel2\,d_{\text{model}} to 3​dmodel3\,d_{\text{model}}). This corresponds to the WV(h)W_{V}^{(h)} parameters in the notation of Section 3.3.
Optimization Objective.
We map the digit label y∈{0,…,9}y\in\{0,\ldots,9\} to y~:=y/9∈[0,1]\tilde{y}:=y/9\in[0,1] and predict y^w⁡(θ)​(x,Pn):=19​∑j=09j​pd\widehat{y}_{w(\theta)}(x;P_{n}):=\frac{1}{9}\sum_{j=0}^{9}j\,p_{d}, where pdp_{d} are the softmax probabilities of the digit tokens. For each prompt position ii, we use leave-one-out supervision and define the residual
ri​(θ):=y~i−y^w⁡(θ)​(xi,Pn(−i))r_{i}(\theta):=\tilde{y}_{i}-\widehat{y}_{w(\theta)}(x_{i};P_{n}^{(-i)}).
The prompt loss is L⁡(θ)=12​σ2​∑i=1nri​(θ)2L(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}r_{i}(\theta)^{2}.
In the few-step regime, linearizing y^w⁡(θ)\widehat{y}_{w(\theta)} at θ=0\theta=0 yields
r⁡(θ)≈r−Φ​θr(\theta)\approx r-\Phi\theta, recovering the quadratic surrogate L⁡(θ)=12​σ2​‖r−Φ​θ‖22L(\theta)=\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\|r-\Phi\theta\|_{2}^{2} as in Section 3.2.
Updates are performed via the stochastic gradient descent on the leave-one-out prompt residuals for TT steps with step size η=ρ​σ2\eta=\rho\sigma^{2} (equivalently, scaled step size ρ\rho).
I.3 Evidence-Based TT Selection
We select the horizon TT by minimizing the negative log-evidence of the prompt residuals rr. We assume the residuals follow a zero-mean Gaussian induced by the TTT filter: r∼𝒩⁡(0,ΣT)r\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma_{T}).
Using the eigen-decomposition of the kernel K=U​Λ​U⊤K=U\Lambda U^{\top} with eigenvalues λi\lambda_{i}, the covariance is ΣT=σ2​(I−ρ​K)−T\Sigma_{T}=\sigma^{2}(I-\rho K)^{-T}. We implement two selection criteria:
1. Fixed-σ\sigma Evidence. We estimate a constant noise variance σ^2=1n​‖r‖2\hat{\sigma}^{2}=\frac{1}{n}\|r\|^{2} from the unadapted residuals. We then select TT to minimize:
ℒ(T)=−Tn∑i=1nlog(1−ρλi)+1n​σ^2∑i=1n(1−ρλi)T(r~i)2,\mathcal{L}(T)=-\frac{T}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(1-\rho\lambda_{i})+\frac{1}{n\hat{\sigma}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\rho\lambda_{i})^{T}(\tilde{r}_{i})^{2},
(82)
where r~=U⊤​r\tilde{r}=U^{\top}r are the rotated residuals. This corresponds to the fixed-σ2\sigma^{2} variant discussed in Section 7.
2. MLE-σ\sigma Evidence. We treat σ2\sigma^{2} as a free parameter to be profiled out [34]. We minimize the profile log-likelihood: ℒMLE​(T)=log⁡(1n​∑i=1n(1−ρ​λi)T​(r~i)2)−Tn​∑i=1nlog⁡(1−ρ​λi)\mathcal{L}_{\text{MLE}}(T)=\log\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\rho\lambda_{i})^{T}(\tilde{r}_{i})^{2}\right)-\frac{T}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log(1-\rho\lambda_{i}). Both methods select TMAP∈arg⁡minT∈𝒯⁡ℒ⁡(T)T_{\mathrm{MAP}}\in\arg\min_{T\in\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{L}(T) where 𝒯={0,1,2,…,30}\mathcal{T}=\{0,1,2,\ldots,30\}.
I.4 Subspace Selection Algorithms
In Experiment 2, we select a subset of heads from the last 4 layers (48 candidate blocks). We vary kbudget∈{1,2,4,8,16,32}k_{\text{budget}}\in\{1,2,4,8,16,32\} to assess scaling behavior.
Trace-TopK. We compute the trace score for each block bb as Tr​(K(b)​(Ksum+σ2​I)−1)\text{Tr}(K^{(b)}(K_{\text{sum}}+\sigma^{2}I)^{-1}), where K(b)K^{(b)} is the kernel for block bb and KsumK_{\text{sum}} is the sum of all candidate kernels.
Query-Aware.
Consistent with Theorem 8.2, which identifies the subspace maximizing posterior variance reduction, we prioritize update directions with significant query-aligned gradients. In practice, this corresponds to iteratively selecting the block bb that maximizes the squared magnitude of the predicted correction h2h^{2}, where h=kx⊤​QT​(Kcurr+K(b))​rh=k_{x}^{\top}Q_{T}(K_{\text{curr}}+K^{(b)})r. Here, kxk_{x} is the prompt-query coupling vector, KcurrK_{\text{curr}} is the current kernel sum, and QT​(λ)=1−(1−ρ​λ)TλQ_{T}(\lambda)=\frac{1-(1-\rho\lambda)^{T}}{\lambda} is the TTT filter response.
Trace-BottomK.
We use the same trace score as Trace-TopK, but select the kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} blocks with the smallest scores sbs_{b}. We include Trace-BottomK as a negative-control baseline for query-agnostic selection.
Random (Uniform).
As a baseline, we sample kbudgetk_{\text{budget}} blocks uniformly at random without replacement from the candidate set (e.g., the 48 (layer, head) blocks), and update only the selected blocks.
I.5 Hyperparameters
We selected the prior scale cc via a pilot study on 20 tasks distinct from the test set, sweeping c∈{0.05,0.1,0.2}c\in\{0.05,0.1,0.2\} with fixed T=8T=8. For the main experiments, we used a step size scale ρ≈c/λmax\rho\approx c/\lambda_{\max} to ensure stability (ρ<1/λmax​(K)\rho<1/\lambda_{\max}(K) as required by the monotone-filter regime in Section 4).
Evaluation Protocol.
We evaluate all methods on 2,000 independent test tasks for Experiment 1, and 500 tasks for Experiment 2 (due to the additional computational cost of multiple block configurations).
Appendix J Supplementary Real-Data Experiment on SST-2
We include a small real-data experiment on SST-2 to check whether the two design principles from the main text also appear outside the synthetic digit-shift task. This experiment is intended as supplementary mechanism-level evidence, not as a definitive benchmark.
Dataset and task construction.
SST-2 is a binary sentence-level sentiment classification dataset [43]. We sample tasks from the validation split. Each task consists of 10 labeled prompt sentences and one held-out query sentence, sampled without replacement within the task. The query label is used only for evaluation. We encode the negative and positive labels as 00 and 11, respectively, and use a fixed text-label template: each prompt example is written as Text: {sentence} Label: {0/1}, followed by the query prefix Text: {query} Label:. To keep the setting close to the squared-loss theory, we cast the binary classification problem as scalar regression on labels in {0,1}\{0,1\} and evaluate query mean-squared error.
Prediction model.
We use distilgpt2 as a causal language model. The prediction at the query is computed from the next-token distribution restricted to the two single-token label strings for 00 and 11: if p0,p1p_{0},p_{1} are the restricted softmax probabilities, the scalar prediction is y^=p1\hat{y}=p_{1} (equivalently, the expected normalized label). For computational consistency across tasks, input texts are truncated before constructing the prompt, and all model weights are reset to the pretrained checkpoint before each TTT run.
TTT protocol.
We use the same few-step TTT protocol as in the main experiments. Prompt residuals are computed in a leave-one-out manner to avoid label leakage: when forming the residual for a prompt example, its own label is not included in the prompt used by the base predictor. The prompt loss is the squared error between these residuals and the linearized prediction shifts. Adaptation updates only attention Value weights, selected at the attention-head level; for GPT-2’s fused c_attn parameter, this corresponds to the Value slice from 2​dmodel2d_{\rm model} to 3​dmodel3d_{\rm model}. Gradients outside the selected Value-head slices are masked to zero. We use 50 sampled tasks in this supplementary run.
Kernel and step-size construction.
For each task, we compute the prompt kernel and prompt–query coupling from Jacobian features with respect to the candidate Value-head parameters. The effective noise level is estimated by the plug-in value σ^2=‖r‖22/n\hat{\sigma}^{2}=\|r\|_{2}^{2}/n from the base leave-one-out residuals. The scaled step size is chosen by the same stable normalization as in the synthetic experiment, ρ=c/λmax​(K)\rho=c/\lambda_{\max}(K) with c=0.1c=0.1 and clipped so that ρ<1/λmax​(K)\rho<1/\lambda_{\max}(K).
Horizon selection.
For the update horizon experiment, we compare a fixed-horizon baseline with T=8T=8 against evidence-based per-prompt selection. The evidence methods search over the same grid as in the synthetic experiment, T∈{0,1,…,30}T\in\{0,1,\ldots,30\}, using either a fixed plug-in estimate of σ\sigma or the profiled MLE-σ\sigma variant. The evidence score is evaluated from the prompt-kernel eigenspectrum and the rotated residuals, so the candidate horizons are not evaluated by rerunning TTT from scratch.
Subspace selection.
For the block/head selection experiment, we consider 48 candidate attention heads from the last four layers of distilgpt2. We evaluate budgets kbudget∈{1,2,4,8,16,32}k_{\text{budget}}\in\{1,2,4,8,16,32\} and compare Query-Aware, Trace-TopK, Trace-BottomK, and Random selection. Query-Aware uses the prompt–query coupling to greedily select heads under the fixed horizon T=8T=8; Trace-TopK ranks heads by the query-agnostic trace score; Trace-BottomK is the corresponding negative-control baseline. The Random baseline is averaged over five independent random head selections for each task and budget. As in the main text, subspace results are reported as query-MSE improvement relative to Random, so positive values indicate lower query error than Random.

Table 4: SST-2 subspace selection. Mean query-MSE improvement relative to Random selection; positive values are better. Query-Aware improves over Random across budgets and is stronger than Trace-TopK for small-to-moderate budgets, while Trace-BottomK consistently hurts.
Overall, the results show the same qualitative pattern as the synthetic experiment: evidence-based selection gives a modest improvement over a fixed horizon, and query-aware update directions tend to improve query prediction more reliably than query-agnostic negative controls. Because this study uses only 50 tasks and a small distilled language model, we treat it as preliminary support for the proposed mechanisms rather than evidence of broad practical superiority.
